- **Mem0g (graph variant from the 2025 paper) and what became of it**: Graph-augmented variant of Mem0 that stores memories as a directed labeled graph (entities as nodes, relationship triplets as edges) in Neo4j, intended to help with relational, multi-hop and temporal questions. In 2026 Mem0 removed external graph stores from the open-source SDK. Memory model: STRUCTURE: G=(V,E,L); nodes are entities with a type (Person, Location, Event...), an embedding and a creation timestamp; edges are triplets (source, relation, destination) such as lives_in or prefers. WRITE: an LLM entity extractor finds entities and types; an LLM relations generator labels relationships between entity pairs; embeddings of source and destination are compared to existing nodes above a similarity threshold t to reuse or create nodes; a conflict detector flags existing edges that may clash with the new triplet; an LLM update resolver decides whether they are obsolete, and obsolete edges are marked invalid rather than deleted so temporal reasoning survives. Thresholds are not given in the paper. READ: two paths: entity-centric (find query entities, match to nodes, traverse in/out edges to build a subgraph) and semantic triplet (embed the whole query and match against embedded triplet text above a relevance threshold). 2026 status: Mem0's OSS migration guide says the graph_store config, enable_graph flag, drivers for Neo4j, Memgraph, Kuzu, Apache AGE and Neptune (about 4,000 lines) were removed from OSS; Mem0's Platform now has a native graph that records entity co-occurrence (memories sharing an entity are linked) with no typed relationships, and the old 'relations' field is always empty. Applicable to ClawCode: Do not build a graph database layer. The evidence from Mem0's own numbers (small gain, mixed by category, 2x tokens) and its removal of graph stores from OSS says a typed graph is poor value for a single-agent SQLite store. What is cheap and worth copying: (1) the conflict-detector-then-mark-obsolete step, which is the same idea as Graphiti's invalidation and can be a column update; (2) entity-centric retrieval implemented as an entities table plus a memory_entities join table in SQLite (query entities matched to rows by normalized name or embedding, then memories joined through them), which is roughly what Mem0's new native graph does (co-occurrence, untyped). Cost: an entity extraction step at write time and two small tables; no new infrastructure. Sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2504.19413, https://docs.mem0.ai/migration/oss-v2-to-v3, https://docs.mem0.ai/open-source/features/graph-memory
- **Mem0 v3 / 2026 'token-efficient' algorithm (single-pass ADD-only extraction)**: Mem0's 2026 rewrite of its extraction and retrieval. Announced April 2026 with a May 14, 2026 temporal-reasoning follow-up (the post's displayed dates and metadata dates disagree). Replaces the two-call extract-then-reconcile pipeline with one extraction call that only adds, and adds multi-signal retrieval and entity linking. Note the naming wrinkle: the OSS guide calls it v3 while the Python SDK release carrying it is tagged v2.0.0. Memory model: WRITE: one LLM call per add(), ADD only; the pipeline retrieves about 10 related existing memories as context, the extraction prompt can emit linked_memory_ids pointing at existing memories, new facts are embedded in a batch, de-duplicated by MD5 hash and inserted in a batch. Changed facts are stored alongside old ones; add() no longer emits UPDATE or DELETE events. Agent-generated facts are stored with the same weight as user-stated facts. Relative dates are resolved against an Observation Date. The extraction prompt asks for 15 to 80 word memories that preserve proper nouns and numbers. May 2026 additions (platform only): write-time temporal metadata (when the event occurred, ongoing vs completed, precision, memory type), a state key so a new state closes the old one with an event_end, and search-time memory decay (recently used up to 1.5x boost, stale dampened toward 0.3x; nothing deleted). READ: three signals in parallel: semantic similarity, BM25 keyword, and entity overlap against a separate {collection}_entities store. Docs: 'BM25 is a boost signal, not a recall expander', i.e. only semantic hits are candidates and the other two re-rank them. Without spaCy, OSS search silently falls back to semantic-only. OSS defaults changed: top_k 100 to 20, threshold None to 0.1, rerank True to False. Optional expiration_date hides memories from search without deleting them. Applicable to ClawCode: This is the most relevant Mem0 lesson: keep the hot path cheap and non-destructive, and do reconciliation later. Concrete adoption for ClawCode: (1) single-pass ADD-only extraction at write time with content-hash dedupe (cheap, ClawCode can use a SHA of normalized text in a UNIQUE index); (2) give the extractor about 10 nearest existing memories (sqlite-vec) as context so it can emit linked_memory_ids, stored as a links table; (3) add rank fusion over what ClawCode already has: sqlite-vec KNN for candidates, FTS5 BM25 and entity overlap as boosts (Mem0's design where only semantic hits are candidates), fused with reciprocal rank fusion; (4) search-time recency/access decay as a multiplier rather than deletion, which fits tiered memories; (5) make dreaming do what #4956 shows ADD-only lacks: find same-subject memories, mark older ones superseded (see Graphiti entry), so recency is handled structurally, not by hoping the ranker prefers new facts; (6) resolve relative dates against the source message's timestamp (Discord message time, document date), never now(), the failure in #3944. Cost: an entities table, a links table, a few columns, an RRF function; no new infrastructure. Sources: https://mem0.ai/blog/mem0-the-token-efficient-memory-algorithm, https://mem0.ai/blog/the-token-efficient-memory-algorithm-now-has-temporal-reasoning, https://docs.mem0.ai/migration/oss-v2-to-v3, https://docs.mem0.ai/core-concepts/memory-operations/add, https://docs.mem0.ai/core-concepts/memory-evaluation, https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mem0ai/mem0/main/mem0/configs/prompts.py
- **Graphiti (open-source temporal knowledge graph engine behind Zep)**: Apache-2.0 Python framework for building and querying temporal context graphs for agents. It ingests unstructured and structured data as episodes, extracts entities and facts, resolves duplicates, tracks when each fact was true, and invalidates rather than deletes facts when information changes. You supply a graph DB (Neo4j 5.26, FalkorDB, Amazon Neptune; Kuzu is marked deprecated in the README), an LLM and an embedder. Described in the Zep paper (arXiv 2501.13956, 20 January 2025); repo has about 32k stars. The Zep Community Edition is deprecated and Zep points self-hosters to Graphiti. Memory model: STRUCTURE (three tiers): (1) episode subgraph: episodic nodes hold the raw input (message, text or JSON) losslessly, with episodic edges to the entities they mention; (2) semantic entity subgraph: entity nodes (resolved, with summaries) and entity edges that hold facts as natural-language 'fact' strings with a fact embedding and an episodes list (provenance); (3) community subgraph: community nodes summarizing clusters of strongly connected entities. BI-TEMPORAL: timeline T (valid time) stores valid_at and invalid_at (when the fact became and stopped being true); timeline T' (transaction time) stores created_at and expired_at (when the system recorded and when it retired the edge). Per edges.py comments: valid_at is when the fact became true, invalid_at when it stopped (soft deadlines are not invalid_at), future-tense plans use the episode reference time. WRITE (per episode): entity extraction from the current message plus the last 4 messages (speaker always extracted, a reflection step to cut hallucinations); entity resolution by embedding (1024-dim) plus full-text candidates, then an LLM duplicate judgment; fact extraction between distinct entities; edge de-duplication restricted to edges between the same entity pair; temporal extraction by an LLM that resolves relative dates against the episode's reference timestamp (ISO 8601; nulls unless the date establishes or changes the relationship); edge invalidation: a broader hybrid search finds semantically related existing edges, an LLM returns duplicate_facts and contradicted_facts, and for overlapping contradictions where the old edge started earlier the code sets old.invalid_at = new.valid_at and old.expired_at = now (new information wins on the transaction timeline). Graph writes use predefined Cypher, not LLM-written queries. COMMUNITIES: label propagation (chosen over Leiden because it extends incrementally); a new entity joins the plurality community of its neighbors and the summary is updated; the paper admits these drift from a full recompute so periodic rebuilds are needed (build_communities() deletes and rebuilds; update_communities=True on add_episode adds latency). READ: three searches over facts, entity names and community names: cosine, Okapi BM25, and BFS up to n hops (can be seeded by recent episodes); rerankers: RRF, MMR, episode-mentions, node-distance, cross-encoder (most accurate, most expensive); a constructor formats facts with valid date ranges, entity summaries and community summaries into a context string. Official README claims 'sub-second' typical query latency (no benchmark given). FORGETTING: none by design: 'old facts are invalidated, not deleted'. Applicable to ClawCode: Copy the data model and the invalidation rule, not the infrastructure. (1) Add four nullable columns to the memories table: valid_from, valid_to (event time), recorded_at, superseded_at, plus superseded_by and a source_ids list (Discord message ids, document chunk ids). Default recall adds WHERE superseded_at IS NULL; an as-of query uses valid_from/valid_to. (2) Port the rule from edge_operations.py: when a new memory contradicts an overlapping older one that started earlier, set old.valid_to = new.valid_from and old.superseded_at = now. (3) Scope the two candidate searches as Graphiti does: dedupe candidates restricted to the same subject (same entity tags), contradiction candidates from a broader hybrid search (sqlite-vec plus FTS5, RRF). Both run in dreaming, not per message. (4) Keep raw turns and document chunks as the lossless 'episode' layer and link every derived memory to them, so extraction can be re-run with a better prompt or model. (5) Treat community nodes as an output of dreaming: cluster memories by entity co-occurrence or embedding, write a summary memory in a higher tier, and rebuild periodically instead of incrementally; no ablation in the Zep paper shows communities improve accuracy, so make this the last thing built. (6) Resolve relative dates against the source timestamp at extraction. Do not copy: a Neo4j/FalkorDB/Neptune dependency (Kuzu, the embedded option, was archived upstream in October 2025), 6 to 10 LLM calls per message, or community updates on every write. Cost of the recommended subset: a schema migration, one contradiction prompt, a changed default query filter, and dreaming-time LLM calls only. Sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2501.13956, https://github.com/getzep/graphiti, https://raw.githubusercontent.com/getzep/graphiti/main/graphiti_core/edges.py, https://raw.githubusercontent.com/getzep/graphiti/main/graphiti_core/utils/maintenance/edge_operations.py, https://help.getzep.com/graphiti/core-concepts/communities, https://help.getzep.com/graphiti/getting-started/overview
- **Zep (managed memory service on Graphiti) and its DMR / LongMemEval results**: Commercial memory/context layer that builds a temporal knowledge graph per user from conversations and business data and returns an assembled context string at query time. Paper: 'Zep: A Temporal Knowledge Graph Architecture for Agent Memory', arXiv 2501.13956, 20 January 2025. Evaluated on DMR (the MemGPT benchmark) and LongMemEval; later on LoCoMo (see next entry). Memory model: As in the Graphiti entry. Zep-specific points from the paper and its 2026 research page: embeddings and reranking use BAAI BGE-m3; graph construction used gpt-4o-mini-2024-07-18; retrieval returned the top 10 edges and nodes for DMR and the 20 most relevant edges and entity nodes for LongMemEval; 2026 multi-scope retrieval returns 20 edges, 10 nodes, 10 episodes, 5 thread summaries and 5 observations with cross-encoder reranking. Zep's retrieval is built to be cheap at read time (no LLM in the search path), pushing the cost to asynchronous ingestion; Mem0's paper noted that searches run immediately after ingestion often failed and worked hours later. Applicable to ClawCode: (1) Context assembly: return facts with their valid date ranges ('true from X to Y'), then entity or topic summaries, then higher-tier summaries, in one formatted block, with an explicit token budget (Zep used 1.6k tokens in 2025, 4.4k to 5.8k in 2026; Mem0 about 6.8k to 7k). ClawCode should set a budget and measure accuracy against it. (2) Optional cross-encoder rerank of the top 20 to 50 candidates: Zep lists it as the most accurate and most expensive reranker, with no ablation in the paper I read; for ClawCode this means loading a second local model (for example a BGE reranker) and paying per-query latency, so try RRF first and add the reranker only if an eval shows a gain. (3) Zep's weak spot is a warning: single-session-assistant accuracy fell below full-context, because facts were extracted from conversation content and the assistant's own earlier statements were lost. ClawCode agents are persistent and their own outputs (decisions, commitments, file changes) matter, so extraction must cover agent turns. (4) Async ingestion lag: keep the raw recent turns searchable through FTS5 immediately, and let extraction and dreaming catch up, to avoid the 'search right after ingest finds nothing' failure. (5) Use LongMemEval's question types (knowledge-update, temporal-reasoning, multi-session, single-session-preference, abstention) as the template for ClawCode's own regression eval. Cost: context-assembly formatting and a budget are cheap; reranker and eval harness are the real work. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.13956, https://arxiv.org/html/2501.13956, https://www.getzep.com/research/, https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.10813
- **LoCoMo results, the Mem0 vs Zep dispute, and how far to trust any of these numbers**: LoCoMo (Maharana et al., arXiv 2402.17753, Feb 2024, older) is the benchmark Mem0's paper used. Original conversations average about 300 turns and 9K tokens over up to 35 sessions; Mem0's paper measured about 26k tokens each. 10 conversations; after dropping category 5 (adversarial, no usable ground truth) about 1,540 scored questions in single-hop, multi-hop, open-domain and temporal categories. LongMemEval (arXiv 2410.10813, Oct 2024, older; 500 questions, five abilities, about 115k tokens in the S setting per the Zep paper) is the harder, longer alternative that Zep prefers. Memory model: Not a memory system; this entry records the evaluation dispute chronology. (1) 28 Apr 2025: Mem0's paper reports Zep at 65.99 J, below Mem0 at 66.88 and Mem0g at 68.44. (2) May 2025: Zep's rebuttal (blog 'Lies, Damn Lies, & Statistics: Is Mem0 Really SOTA in Agent Memory?') says Mem0 ran Zep wrongly: assigned the user role to both speakers (so Zep likely saw one user whose identity changed every message), appended timestamps to the message text instead of using the created_at field, and ran searches sequentially rather than in parallel, inflating latency. Zep reports 75.14% +/- 0.17 for a correct setup, and the page carries a correction note that an earlier Zep score had been miscalculated (a secondary Japanese/Korean write-up says the first figure was about 84% and that Mem0's CTO objected that category 5 was dropped from the denominator but kept in the numerator; I could not confirm that from a primary source). Zep also argues LoCoMo is too short (16k to 26k tokens fits in modern context windows) and has no knowledge-update test. (3) Aug 2025: Letta reports 74.0% on LoCoMo with a plain filesystem agent (grep, search_files, open, close, GPT-4o-mini) against what it calls Mem0's reported 68.5% for its graph variant, says Mem0 did not respond to requests to explain how its numbers were computed, and argues agent capability matters more than the memory tool. (4) 2026: Mem0 re-reports 91.6 then 92.5 on LoCoMo with a new algorithm, new answerer/judge and top-200 retrieval; Zep reports 94.7 with gpt-5.4. (5) Independent critique: Penfield Labs (a memory-product vendor, so an interested party) audited the 1,540 questions and reports 99 score-corrupting answer-key errors (6.4%, including 24 speaker-attribution errors) and that the standard gpt-4o-mini judge accepted 62.81% of intentionally wrong but topically adjacent answers (catching specific factual errors about 89% of the time); it estimates a perfect-system ceiling near 93.6%. Mem0's own 'how to read memory benchmarks' page (Oct 2026) now cites this audit and says LoCoMo is a baseline, 'less so' a deciding benchmark. Applicable to ClawCode: Do not pick architecture from leaderboard numbers. Build a small internal eval modeled on LongMemEval's categories using ClawCode's own Discord threads and ingested documents: fixed answerer model, fixed judge model and prompt, 10 repeated runs with mean and SD (as Mem0's paper did), report accuracy plus tokens injected plus p95 retrieval latency, and always include two baselines: full-context (when it fits) and a plain FTS5 plus sqlite-vec RRF baseline. Letta's filesystem result and Mem0's own full-context row both show simple baselines are hard to beat on short corpora, so ClawCode's extra tiers and dreaming must show a gain on knowledge-update, temporal and abstention questions, which LoCoMo barely tests. Add a handful of ClawCode-specific cases: fact changed in Discord three weeks later, fact stated only in an OCR'd document, agent's own earlier commitment. Cost: a few hundred hand-checked questions and a runner script; this is the cheapest and highest-value item in the list because it tells you whether any of the other changes helped. Sources: https://www.getzep.com/blog/lies-damn-lies-statistics-is-mem0-really-sota-in-agent-memory/, https://arxiv.org/html/2504.19413, https://www.letta.com/blog/benchmarking-ai-agent-memory, https://dev.to/penfieldlabs/we-audited-locomo-64-of-the-answer-key-is-wrong-and-the-judge-accepts-up-to-63-of-intentionally-33lg, https://mem0.ai/library/agent-memory/how-to-read-memory-benchmarks, https://braindetox.kr/en/posts/memory_benchmark_scores_reliability_2026.html

#### Academic agent memory architectures 2023-2026 (Generative Agents, A-MEM, HippoRAG 1/2, Reflexion, MemoryBank, Memory^3/MemOS/MemoryOS, and 2025-2026 consolidation and forgetting work): what is measured to work and what is not

- Write selectively, with an outcome label; 'store everything' is measurably worse than a gated store. Evidence: Add-all was the worst policy on 3 of 4 agents (RegAgent 55.48 vs 67.53 for a fixed memory and 70.95 for strict addition; EHRAgent 13.05 vs 38.50 strict; AgentDriver 32.32 vs 51.00); add-all errors compounded over time. Future task outcomes can serve as free quality labels. ReasoningBank gets gains from distilled items extracted from both successes and failures using a self-judge that was only 72.7% accurate. Source: https://arxiv.org/html/2505.16067 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2509.25140
- Reflection and consolidation need an external signal; without one they can degrade quality. Evidence: Reflexion's gains rely on evaluators (unit tests, environment heuristics); dropping test generation fell to 52% vs 60% on hard Rust problems, and it failed on WebShop where reflections were unhelpful. LLMs without external feedback struggle to self-correct reasoning and can get worse (ICLR 2024). ACE gains shrink without execution feedback and noisy Reflectors make context harmful. The Generative Agents reflection ablation is worth only about 3 TrueSkill points (29.89 vs 26.88) on believability, not task success. Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2303.11366 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.01798 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2510.04618 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2304.03442v2
- Update memory by deltas and versions, never by wholesale LLM rewrite; keep inputs immutable and write to a new snapshot. Evidence: ACE documents context collapse: a rewrite shrank an 18,282-token context to 122 tokens and accuracy fell below the no-adaptation baseline; its incremental itemised deltas merged by non-LLM code cut latency 82.3% vs GEPA. LightMem inserts immediately and reconciles offline. Anthropic's Dreams writes a new output store and never modifies the input store. SSGM argues for decoupling memory evolution from execution with pre-consolidation checks. Source: https://arxiv.org/html/2510.04618 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2510.18866v4 ; https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/dreams ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.11768
- Pruning mostly buys storage and latency, not accuracy; archive rather than delete, and use utility (not age) to choose what to drop. Evidence: Pruning 9.8% of graph nodes left token F1 unchanged (0.292 to 0.293) and judge accuracy slightly lower (0.300 to 0.284), neither significant, with no random control. Periodic deletion cut EHRAgent memory 1012 to 302 with ACC 38.67 to 38.59; history-based deletion (utility from later outcomes) raised EHRAgent to 42.06 and CIC-IoT to 89.60 because the deleted records were measurably worse. RSPM pruned 43% on one LoCoMo conversation and ended 19.5 points below baseline, and archival hurt questions about historical states. FSFM lost about 30% of labelled important content for a 30% storage cut. Source: https://arxiv.org/html/2608.28978v1 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2505.16067 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2610.25561 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2604.20300
- Time-decay forgetting curves (Ebbinghaus style) are unproven for accuracy; the measured gains in these papers come from consolidation, fusion and conflict handling. Evidence: MemoryBank has no ablation of its forgetting mechanism and no baseline. FadeMem's ablations show fusion (-53.7%) and the dual-layer structure (-33.9%) matter more than anything else, decay was not ablated alone, and its margin over Mem0 on LoCoMo multi-hop is about 1 F1 on an author-built benchmark. MemoryOS and FadeMem both use recall counts and recency as ranking signals, not accuracy-verified deletion rules. Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2305.10250 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2601.18642v2 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2506.06326
- Knowledge update and supersession is the least solved memory operation; do it explicitly with timestamps and an LLM check at mutation time, not through retrieval. Evidence: MemoryAgentBench FactConsolidation multi-hop: best 28.0 (GPT-5-mini long-context), HippoRAG-v2 5.0, MemGPT 3.0, Mem0 2.0. HaluMem: update omission above 50% for every system (Mem0 98.5% on Long), best correct updates 47.3% (Zep). EvalMem: rollback questions 47.3%. ForgetEval: an LLM hook at mutation time reaches 91.7 to 93.2% overall while deterministic primitives fail canonicalization (5%, 0% cross-lingual); LLM at inscribe time alone gets 0% on intent-aware deletion. Source: https://arxiv.org/html/2507.05257 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2511.03506v2 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2609.22231 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15903
- Keep raw verbatim text and index it well; most measured retrieval gains come from indexing, not from fancier structure. Evidence: LongMemEval: round-level indexing beats session-level; appending extracted facts to the key adds +9.4% recall and +5.4% accuracy; time-aware query expansion adds +6.8 to 11.3% recall on temporal questions; replacing values with summaries or facts generally hurts. A graph memory built from extracted entities scored below flat vector RAG (F1 0.417 vs 0.468; single-session assistant recall 0.607 vs 0.911). Nemori keeps raw text for the top 2 episodes. A 2026 diagnostic finds write-side loss exceeds retrieval-side loss for four of six baselines. Source: https://arxiv.org/html/2410.10813 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2608.28978v1 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2508.03341 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24579
- Retrieval quality is the dominant failure layer; add a recall-first retry before adding more memory machinery. Evidence: EvalMem across seven systems: retrieval-defect rate 18.3 to 25.9% vs encoding 7.7 to 9.7% and generation 5.0 to 6.5%; recall-first agentic RAG raised evidence recall from 70.2% to 95.6% on LoCoMo. REALM's gains came from evidence utilisation more than discovery. RMM's citation-feedback reranker raised LongMemEval accuracy 63.6 to 70.4 over plain RAG. Source: https://arxiv.org/html/2609.22231 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2609.16053 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2503.08026
- Full-context is a strong baseline; memory wins on cost, latency and beyond-window scale, not always on accuracy. Evidence: Mem0 paper: full-context 72.90 J vs Mem0 66.88, but 9.87 s p50 and 17.1 s p95 latency vs 0.71 and 1.44 s, and 26,031 vs 1,764 retrieved tokens. Nemori ties full context on LoCoMo (80.8 vs 80.6) but wins on LongMemEval-S (74.6 vs 65.6) with about 4K vs 101K tokens. LightMem with Qwen on LoCoMo is below FullText (72.60 vs 74.87). MemoryAgentBench: long-context agents lead most competencies. MemoryArena: external memory 'not universally beneficial' vs long-context history. Source: https://arxiv.org/html/2504.19413 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2508.03341 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2510.18866v4 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2507.05257 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2602.16313v1
- Graph or link structure gives modest, task-specific gains at a large write-time cost; benchmark against hybrid dense plus BM25 first. Evidence: HippoRAG 2 beats NV-Embed-v2 by 2.8 average F1 (59.8 vs 57.0), with +9.5 on 2Wiki but +1.4 on NQ and +0.2 on HotpotQA; its indexing used 9.2M input and 3.0M output tokens for 11,656 passages; independently it loses to plain RAG on NQ (61.03 vs 64.78) and KG-only retrieval misses about 34% of answer entities. A-MEM's links give most of its gain but an independent reproduction needed about 13 LLM calls per query and its write-side scaling is unreported. MemoryAgentBench shows HippoRAG-v2 strongest at retrieval (65.1) but weak at updating (5.0 multi-hop). Source: https://arxiv.org/html/2502.14802v2 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2502.11371 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2502.12110 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2506.06326 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2507.05257
- Importance scoring by an LLM per write is not shown to pay for itself; use cheap usage-derived signals (recall counts, citations, outcomes). Evidence: Generative Agents never ablated importance or the retrieval weights; the one coding-agent replication found GA-style retrieval not significantly better than FIFO (-0.375 repeated calls, CI [-1.25, 0.375]) while adding 285 rating calls and about 67 s per task. RMM's citation-derived rewards match an LLM judge at about 87% precision. MemoryOS heat and history-based deletion use counts and later utility with no extra LLM calls. Source: https://arxiv.org/html/2304.03442v2 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2608.31057 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2503.08026 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2506.06326 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2505.16067
- Spend offline (dreaming) compute where future queries are predictable, and make consolidation novelty-gated. Evidence: Sleep-time compute: about 5x less test-time compute, up to +13% and +18% accuracy, 2.5x lower per-query cost when amortised, with the benefit tied to query predictability. Nemori's prediction-error distillation scored 73.0 vs 52.0 for direct distillation and cut stored memory 45 to 64% when plugged into other systems. RSPM's sleep cycle was its most important component (+7.6 to 11.9 points) but with a high-variance failure case. Source: https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.13171 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2508.03341 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2610.25561
- Do not trust published memory deltas of a few points; benchmarks are noisy, vendor-run and often unreproducible. Evidence: LoCoMo audit (memory vendor, unreviewed): 6.4% of the answer key is wrong and the judge accepts 62.81% of wrong-but-adjacent answers. A-Mem scores 48.38 J in Mem0's paper but its own paper reports far different numbers and a different metric. Papers examined here have table/text mismatches (MemoryOS percentages, FSFM latency 30% vs about 23%, REALM column headers, FadeMem 45% vs 55% storage). MemOS, Hindsight and DolphinBench are vendor-authored; Hindsight borrows baseline numbers. Source: https://dev.to/penfieldlabs/we-audited-locomo-64-of-the-answer-key-is-wrong-and-the-judge-accepts-up-to-63-of-intentionally-33lg ; https://arxiv.org/html/2504.19413 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2506.06326 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2604.20300 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2609.16053 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2609.24971
- Passive-recall benchmarks do not predict agentic task success; evaluate multi-session task reuse and operation-level errors. Evidence: MemoryArena: average success 0.14 to 0.23 across memory and RAG systems, group travel planning 0.00 for all, despite saturating recall-style benchmarks. HaluMem: extraction recall under 60% for every system, which is what drives downstream update and QA errors. The Always-On survey finds the literature under-measures governance, recovery and relinquishment. Source: https://arxiv.org/html/2602.16313v1 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2511.03506v2 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30306
- Provenance and reversibility are cheap and are what make consolidation safe. Evidence: Summarisation drift, self-reinforcing reflection errors and error propagation are reported across surveys and HaluMem; MINJA shows ordinary queries can plant memory records; Dreams, MemOS (rollback, freezing, provenance) and SSGM (pre-consolidation verification) all build in reversibility; RSPM's raw-episode deletion made bad distillations unrecoverable. Source: https://arxiv.org/html/2603.07670v1 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03704 ; https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/dreams ; https://arxiv.org/html/2507.03724v4 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.11768 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2610.25561
- Learned memory controllers help, but need training signal; with a closed model, a lightweight bandit or outcome counters is the feasible form. Evidence: Memory-R1 (RL on 152 QA pairs) reached F1 45.02 vs 30.41 for Mem0 and an untrained manager scored 34.5 vs 41.0 for the trained one. MemCon's UCB bandit adds no LLM calls and reports +2.6 to 4.2 points over G-Memory, but with single runs per cell. RMM's REINFORCE reranker alone collapses accuracy to 31.0 if it fine-tunes the retriever directly. Source: https://arxiv.org/html/2508.19828 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2607.13591 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2503.08026

Anti-patterns: Append-all experience memory with unverified outputs: stored errors are replayed (experience-following) and compound; add-all was worst on 3 of 4 agents in the controlled study.; Asking an LLM to rewrite the whole memory or playbook during consolidation: causes context collapse (18,282 tokens to 122 in ACE) and semantic drift.; Deleting raw episodes after distilling them, or hard-deleting on a decay score: RSPM ended 19.5 points below baseline on a high-error conversation and hurt historical-state questions; FSFM lost about 30% of important content; overwrites lose information (LightMem's Tokyo-to-Kyoto case).; Age-only decay as the forgetting policy, justified by the Ebbinghaus analogy: MemoryBank never ablated it, FadeMem never isolated it, and the one significance-tested pruning study showed no accuracy change.; Reflection or dreaming with no external outcome signal: intrinsic self-correction is unreliable and reflective memory can reinforce its own errors; Reflexion degraded when its test signal was removed.; Blaming whichever memory was in context for a failed task (RSPM's failure-association assumption): task difficulty, not memory, often caused the failure.; An LLM importance rating on every write in the hot path: no ablation of it in Generative Agents, no significant gain in a coding-agent replication, plus extra calls and latency.; Relying on similarity retrieval to resolve contradictions or knowledge updates: FactConsolidation multi-hop scores of 2 to 5% for retrieval and memory systems, and update omission above 50% in HaluMem.; Replacing text with extracted entities or summaries and discarding verbatim content: graph memory lost to flat RAG (0.607 vs 0.911 on assistant-turn recall), and summaries hurt LongMemEval QA outside multi-session reasoning.; Treating A-MEM-style evolution or MemOS-style lifecycles as free: evolution rewrites old memories without ground truth, write-side cost is unreported, and an independent reproduction needed 13 LLM calls per query.; Building a knowledge graph for every corpus: HippoRAG-style indexing costs about 790 input plus 260 output tokens per passage and gives no gain on single-hop lookups (NQ, PopQA), and KG-only retrieval misses about a third of answer entities.; Picking designs from LoCoMo deltas of a few points, or from vendor-authored tables: answer-key errors (6.4%), judge leniency (62.81% of wrong answers accepted), non-reproducible baselines, and table/text mismatches in several papers.; Evaluating memory only on recall QA: scores near saturation on LoCoMo coexist with 0.14 to 0.23 success on interdependent multi-session tasks (MemoryArena).; Assuming model-internal memory results transfer to an API-based harness: Memory^3 needs memory-aware pretraining; SleepGate was shown on a 793K-parameter transformer's KV cache; neither is usable through the Claude Agent SDK.; Letting consolidated or injected content enter core memory without provenance, probation or rollback: MINJA shows ordinary queries can plant records, and HaluMem shows extraction and update errors accumulate into answers.

Systems reviewed:

- **Generative Agents (Park et al., 2023, older source)**: Memory stream plus retrieval scoring plus reflection plus planning for 25 simulated agents in a sandbox town. The canonical recency/relevance/importance retrieval design and the canonical 'reflection' consolidation step. Memory model: Storage: append-only natural-language memory stream (observations, reflections, plans) with timestamps. Write: every observation is stored; at write time an LLM rates importance as an integer 1 to 10 ('mundane' to 'poignant'). Read: score = recency + relevance + importance, each min-max normalized to [0,1], all weights set to 1; recency is exponential decay 0.995 per game hour since last retrieval; relevance is embedding cosine to the query; top items that fit the context window go in the prompt. Consolidation: reflection fires when summed importance of recent events exceeds 150 (about 2 to 3 times per agent-day); the LLM gets the 100 most recent records, proposes 3 salient questions, each question is used as a retrieval query, and the LLM writes insights that cite the supporting records. Reflections can cite other reflections, giving a tree (leaves are observations, higher nodes more abstract). Forgetting: none; only recency decay in ranking. Applicable to ClawCode: Copy: (1) a combined score in SQL (sqlite-vec cosine and FTS5 bm25 normalized, plus a recency decay and a stored importance) since all three signals are already available locally and cost nothing at read time; (2) reflections with a `derived_from` provenance table so a dream can invalidate a reflection when its sources are superseded; (3) an importance-accumulator trigger for dreaming (fire when summed importance of new memories passes a threshold) in addition to the periodic schedule. Do not copy: per-write LLM importance rating in the hot path. The only coding-agent test found no significant gain and a real latency and call cost; if kept, rate importance in batches inside the dream pass, or derive it from cheap signals (retrieval count, citation, outcome). Treat the weights as untuned hyperparameters and test against plain hybrid retrieval first. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2304.03442, https://arxiv.org/html/2304.03442v2, https://arxiv.org/html/2608.31057
- **Reflexion (Shinn et al., 2023, older source)**: Verbal reinforcement: after a failed trial an LLM writes a first-person reflection that is stored in an episodic buffer and prepended to the next attempt. No weight updates. Memory model: Storage: a small sliding-window buffer of self-reflection strings (last 1 to 3 reflections) alongside the current trajectory. Write: one reflection per failed trial, conditioned on an evaluator signal (unit test results, ALFWorld heuristics for hallucination or inefficient planning, or exact match). Read: whole buffer injected into the prompt. Forgetting: hard FIFO cap. The authors explicitly say vector or SQL stores are future work. Applicable to ClawCode: Copy: on a task failure with a hard signal (non-zero tool exit, failing test, explicit user correction in Discord), write one short lesson row tagged `kind=lesson` with the triggering signal stored in the row, and inject at most 3 recent lessons for the same task type. Cost: one cheap Claude call per failure. Do not generate reflections when there is no external signal (dreaming over pure chat with no outcome labels is the unsupported case). Cap and rotate lessons rather than letting them accumulate. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.11366, https://arxiv.org/pdf/2303.11366, https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.01798
- **MemoryBank (Zhong et al., 2023, older source)**: Long-term memory for companion chatbots (SiliconFriend) with hierarchical summaries, a user-personality portrait, and an 'Ebbinghaus forgetting curve' updater. Memory model: Storage: timestamped raw dialogue turns; daily event summaries rolled into a global summary; daily personality analyses rolled into a global user portrait (all LLM-generated). Read: dense passage retrieval over FAISS indexes of turns and summaries. Forgetting: retention R = exp(-t/S) where S is an integer strength initialised to 1; each recall increments S by 1 and resets t to 0. The authors call it 'exploratory and highly simplified'. Applicable to ClawCode: Take only the cheap, defensible part: store `recall_count` and `last_recalled_at` on every memory and bump them on retrieval (two columns, no LLM). Use them to rank candidates for archival in dreaming, not for hard deletion. Daily-to-global hierarchical summaries are reasonable for the episodic tier as long as raw turns stay retrievable. Do not claim the forgetting curve improves recall; nothing measured supports that. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.10250, https://arxiv.org/pdf/2305.10250
- **A-MEM (Xu et al., 2025, NeurIPS 2025)**: Zettelkasten-style agentic memory: each memory becomes a structured note, is automatically linked to related notes, and can trigger rewrites of older notes ('memory evolution'). Memory model: Storage: notes with 7 fields (raw content, timestamp, LLM keywords, LLM tags, LLM contextual description, embedding of the concatenated text, link set). Write: 3 LLM steps per memory: construct attributes; take top-k cosine neighbours and let an LLM choose which to link; for each neighbour let an LLM decide whether to update its context/keywords/tags (updates replace originals). Read: embed query (all-MiniLM-L6-v2), return top-k notes (k=10 default, up to 50 per category for GPT-4o-mini). Forgetting: none. Applicable to ClawCode: Fits the stack directly (note table + sqlite-vec neighbours + FTS5 over keywords/tags/context). Copy: generate keywords/tags/context and append them to the FTS5 key (this is the same trick as LongMemEval's fact-augmented key expansion, +9.4% recall, +5.4% accuracy). Copy link generation (cosine top-k as a filter, LLM picks links) but run it in the dream pass, not on the hot write path. Be careful with evolution: it rewrites old memories with no ground truth; do it only with immutable originals, a versioned `evolved_context` column and rollback. Cost: about 3 Claude calls per memory (13 calls measured in an independent reproduction), so batch them and use a small model. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.12110, https://arxiv.org/html/2502.12110, https://arxiv.org/html/2506.06326, https://arxiv.org/html/2504.19413, https://arxiv.org/html/2508.19828
- **HippoRAG 1 (2024) and HippoRAG 2 (2025, ICML 2025)**: Hippocampal-indexing-inspired retrieval: an LLM extracts triples to build a knowledge graph over a corpus, then Personalized PageRank (PPR) from query-matched nodes spreads activation to rank passages, giving multi-hop retrieval in one step. HippoRAG 2 adds passage nodes to the graph, query-to-triple matching, and an LLM recognition-memory filter on triples. Memory model: Storage: phrase nodes and passage nodes; edges are OpenIE triple relations, embedding-similarity synonym edges, and phrase-in-passage edges. Write (offline, LLM heavy): OpenIE per passage; synonym edges via embedding similarity. Read: HippoRAG 1 extracts named entities from the query and links them to nodes; HippoRAG 2 embeds the whole query against triples, filters the top 5 triples with an LLM, seeds up to 5 phrase nodes plus all passage nodes (passage weight factor 0.05), runs PPR, ranks passages. HippoRAG 1 also uses node specificity (inverse passage count, an IDF stand-in). No updating or forgetting mechanism. Applicable to ClawCode: Applies to the document-ingestion path, not to conversational memory. SQLite can hold `kg_node(id, kind, text, emb)` and `kg_edge(src, dst, kind, w)`; PPR is a few dozen power-iteration steps over a scipy sparse matrix per query (small per-agent corpora, milliseconds to seconds). Fuse PPR passage scores with the existing FTS5 and sqlite-vec scores rather than replacing them. Cost: OpenIE at ingestion is roughly 0.8M input plus 0.26M output tokens per 1,000 passages (extrapolated from the paper); use a Haiku-class model and run it as a background job after OCR. Risks: bge-small is a much weaker encoder than NV-Embed-v2, so synonym edges and query-to-triple matching will be noisier (untested); graph adds nothing on single-hop lookups (NQ, PopQA). Gate it: build the graph only for corpora where users ask multi-hop or cross-document questions, and benchmark against hybrid BM25+dense first. The graph has no update path, so a re-OCR or edited document needs a delete-and-reinsert of that document's nodes. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.14831, https://arxiv.org/html/2405.14831v3, https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.14802, https://arxiv.org/html/2502.14802v2, https://arxiv.org/html/2502.11371, https://arxiv.org/html/2507.05257
- **Memory^3 (Yang et al., 2024, older source)**: Model-architecture memory: a 2.4B LLM pretrained to attend to precomputed, sparsified attention key-values of retrieved reference chunks ('explicit memory'), positioned between parametric memory and RAG on a write-cost versus read-cost curve. Memory model: Storage: 1.1e8 reference chunks of up to 128 tokens, stored as sparse KV tensors (8 of 128 tokens kept per head, about 11.4x vector compression, 45.9 TB without and 4.02 TB with compression). Read: retrieve 5 memories every 64 generated tokens (BGE-M3 plus FAISS). Requires memory-aware pretraining (a memory-free warmup first, since training with memory from the start showed no loss gain). Forgetting: none; memories must be rebuilt after parameter updates. Applicable to ClawCode: Not applicable as built: needs model pretraining and KV access, which the Claude Agent SDK does not expose. Only the cost framing transfers: pay a write cost once and read many times, and choose the tier by expected reuse count. The closest equivalent available through the API is Anthropic prompt caching of a stable core-memory prefix. No change recommended. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.01178, https://arxiv.org/html/2407.01178
- **MemOS (MemTensor et al., 2025)**: Positions memory as a managed OS resource: a MemCube unit with metadata, three memory types (parametric, activation/KV-cache, plaintext), a scheduler and lifecycle manager. The May 2025 version (2505.22101) is a design paper; the July 2025 paper (2507.03724, v4) adds results. Memory model: MemCube = payload plus descriptive metadata (time, origin, type), governance attributes (permissions, lifespan, sensitivity, access logging) and behavioural indicators (access frequency, usage). Transformations proposed: plaintext to activation, plaintext/activation to parametric, parametric to plaintext, with no stated thresholds. Operation layer: MemScheduler (LRU, semantic similarity, label matching), MemLifecycle (state machine with rollback and freezing), MemOperator (tagging, graph, hybrid search). Interchange protocol and memory marketplace are future work. Applicable to ClawCode: Copy the metadata schema, not the OS: add columns `provenance`, `created_by` (session/agent/dream), `lifespan` or `expires_at`, `sensitivity`, `access_count`, `last_accessed_at`, `version` to the memories table, and a `state` column (active, frozen, archived) with rollback by version. These cost nothing at read time and make dreaming auditable. Skip the parametric and KV tiers (not reachable via API). Sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2505.22101, https://arxiv.org/html/2507.03724v4
- **MemoryOS (Kang et al., 2025, BAI-LAB)**: Three-tier OS-style memory for conversational agents: short-term, mid-term segments scored by 'heat', long-term persona (user profile and agent persona). Memory model: STM: dialogue pages (queue of 7) with a chain summary. MTM: pages grouped by topic into segments (max 200 pages) with LLM summaries. LPM: user profile (static attributes, user knowledge base, about 90 trait dimensions) plus agent persona; KB and traits are FIFO queues capped at 100. Updates: STM to MTM FIFO; heat = N_visit + L_interaction + R_recency (all weights 1, recency exp(-dt/1e7)); lowest-heat segments evicted when MTM is full; segments with heat above 5 are promoted to LPM and their counters reset. Read: top 5 segments by embedding cosine plus Jaccard keyword overlap, then top k pages (k=10 on LoCoMo); LPM top 10 KB and 10 traits. Applicable to ClawCode: Closest match to ClawCode's existing tiers. Copy the heat score as a SQL expression/view over `visit_count`, `interaction_length`, `last_accessed_at` and use it for promotion (to a persona/core tier) and for archival ranking inside dreaming; it needs no LLM calls. Segment-by-topic with a summary per segment is the episodic tier. Caveat: its evidence base is the authors' own LoCoMo run with a mixed table and automatic grading; the weights (all 1) and thresholds (heat 5) are untuned. Sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2506.06326, https://arxiv.org/html/2508.19828
- **Experience-following study of memory add/delete (Xiong et al., 2025)**: Controlled study of memory management policies in LLM agents. Finds an 'experience-following' property (similar input to a stored record yields a similar output, so bad records get replayed) and tests selective addition and deletion. Memory model: Addition policies: fixed memory, add-all, coarse automatic evaluators (including a fine-tuned judge), and strict (ground-truth) evaluation. Deletion policies: periodic, history-based (remove records retrieved at least n times whose average utility is below a threshold), and combined. Utility labels come from later task outcomes. Applicable to ClawCode: Strongest direct evidence for an add/delete policy. Implement: (1) only write procedural/experience memories when an outcome label exists, and store `outcome` and a running `utility` (success rate of tasks where the memory was retrieved); (2) history-based deletion in dreaming: archive (not purge) memories retrieved at least n times with mean utility below a threshold; (3) a hard cap per tier with eviction by utility. Cost: bookkeeping columns, no extra LLM calls beyond whatever judge you already have; a weak LLM judge is the main risk, so prefer verifiable outcomes (tool exit, tests, user reaction). Sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2505.16067
- **ReasoningBank (Google, 2025)**: Distills reasoning strategies from both successful and failed trajectories into short structured memory items and retrieves them at test time; adds memory-aware test-time scaling (MaTTS). Memory model: Item = title, one-sentence description, content (distilled steps or insight); at most 3 items per trajectory; the agent's own LLM judges success or failure without ground truth. Read: embed task query (gemini-embedding-001), top-k=1 by cosine, inject into system prompt. Consolidation: plain addition. MaTTS: parallel (contrast several rollouts) and sequential (self-refinement) scaling feeds memory. Applicable to ClawCode: Cheap and fits the Agent SDK: at session end, one Claude call extracts at most 3 strategy items (title, description, content) from the transcript with a self-judged success flag, and the top-1 or top-2 matches are injected at the start of similar sessions. Store failure-derived items too (they were what separated it from raw-trajectory and success-only workflow stores). Skip MaTTS (multiplies cost per task). Combine with the experience-following utility tracking so low-utility items are retired. Sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2509.25140, https://arxiv.org/html/2505.16067
- **Agentic Context Engineering, ACE (Stanford/SambaNova et al., 2025)**: Treats the agent's context as an evolving 'playbook' of itemised bullets curated by Generator, Reflector and Curator roles, using incremental delta updates instead of rewrites. Memory model: Context is a list of bullets with usage counters (helpful/harmful). Reflector extracts lessons from successes and errors; Curator emits small delta operations merged by non-LLM code; grow-and-refine appends new bullets, updates existing ones and de-duplicates semantically, either after each update or lazily when the window fills. Applicable to ClawCode: Directly applicable to the always-in-context core tier. Store core memory as rows (not one blob) with `helpful`/`harmful` counters and last-updated; have dreaming emit deltas (add, edit, remove bullet ids) that deterministic code applies, with semantic dedupe via bge-small cosine; never ask Claude to rewrite the whole core memory. Counters need a signal (outcome of sessions where the bullet was in context). Cost: one Claude call per dream batch for the Curator, plus Reflector output; KV or prompt caching keeps the long playbook cheap (ACE reports 91.8% of input tokens served from cache on GPT-5.1). Sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2510.04618
- **Reflective Memory Management, RMM (Tan et al., 2025)**: Long-term dialogue memory with two reflection loops: prospective (organise memory by topic after each session) and retrospective (learn a reranker from which retrieved memories the generator actually cited). Memory model: Prospective: an LLM extracts topic-based memories after each session and either merges each into a similar existing entry or adds it new. Retrospective: a lightweight reranker (linear layer with residuals, Gumbel noise) over a frozen retriever, top-K 20 to top-M 5, updated by REINFORCE with reward +1 for memories the generator cites and -1 for uncited ones. Applicable to ClawCode: Cheap piece to copy: log which retrieved memory ids the agent actually used (ask for cited ids in the output, or detect via tool calls) and keep per-memory used/shown counts; use the ratio as a ranking feature or reranker input. This gives a free usefulness signal without an LLM judge. Skip the REINFORCE reranker unless there is enough traffic; a simple Beta-smoothed use-rate does most of the job. Merge-or-add by topic at session end is the same operation Mem0 calls ADD/UPDATE. Sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2503.08026
- **Memory-R1 (Yan et al., 2025)**: Trains a Memory Manager (ADD, UPDATE, DELETE, NOOP) and an Answer Agent (filters retrieved memories before answering) with reinforcement learning (PPO and GRPO) using answer exact-match as the reward. Memory model: Same four-operation store as Mem0-style systems; the manager and answer agent are separate fine-tuned LLMs (LLaMA-3.1-8B, Qwen-2.5 3B/7B/14B). Training data: 152 QA pairs (81 validation, 1,307 test) from LoCoMo. Applicable to ClawCode: Not directly usable (cannot fine-tune Claude through the SDK). Two lessons transfer: (1) an untrained LLM manager is a weak policy, so wrap ADD/UPDATE/DELETE decisions in deterministic guards (similarity thresholds, never delete on one signal, require provenance) and log every decision for audit; (2) a distillation step that filters retrieved memories before they enter the prompt helped (F1 +4), which a Claude-based 'select relevant memories' call on the top-20 could replicate for harder queries at one extra call. Sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2508.19828
- **Nemori (Nan et al., 2025)**: Self-organising memory that segments conversations into episodes and distils semantic knowledge by predicting each episode from existing memory and keeping only the prediction error (the 'predict-calibrate' idea from the free-energy principle). Memory model: Buffer of w=20 messages is partitioned by an LLM into episodes at topic or intent shifts; each episode becomes a narrative plus a cue (embedded). A second check merges an episode into an existing one if it continues it. Semantic memory: LLM predicts episode content from its cue and retrieved existing knowledge (top-K, threshold 0.70), compares with the raw episode, and stores only what deviates; each insight is tagged new, merge or conflict. Read: top-10 episodes (top 2 with raw text) plus top-20 semantic entries (text-embedding-3-small). Applicable to ClawCode: Fits a dreaming pass well: for each episode, retrieve nearest existing memories (sqlite-vec), ask Claude to predict then diff, and write only the surprising residue as semantic memory (with `derived_from` the episode id). Gives a principled write gate that reduces stored volume. Keep raw episode text retrievable; the top-2-with-raw-text detail matters. Cost: about 2 extra calls per episode on top of narrative generation, mostly during dreaming. Sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2508.03341
- **LightMem (Fang et al., ICLR 2026)**: Atkinson-Shiffrin-style three-stage memory with cheap pre-compression, topic-aware short-term grouping, and sleep-time (offline) consolidation decoupled from inference. Memory model: Stage 1: LLMLingua-2 pre-compression of incoming text (ratio r) and topic segmentation by combining attention-based and embedding-similarity boundaries (512-token buffer). Stage 2: topic groups accumulate to a token threshold, then an LLM summarises each. Stage 3: summaries are inserted with timestamps immediately ('soft update'); an offline process later builds per-entry update queues and runs reconciliation in parallel. Applicable to ClawCode: Matches the dreaming design. Copy: never block a write on consolidation (insert immediately, mark `needs_reconcile`), then reconcile in dream with parallel per-entry queues; keep soft updates (supersede with a link) rather than overwrite; topic-segment OCR'd documents before summarising. LLMLingua-2 style compression before the Claude summarise call could cut dream cost but adds a local model dependency; the claimed 38x savings are partly baseline-dependent. Know the trade: offline reconciliation bought efficiency at a small accuracy cost in this paper. Sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2510.18866v4, https://proceedings.iclr.cc/paper_files/paper/2026/hash/a05b72653ec5b473732129829ae04195-Abstract-Conference.html, https://sotaverified.org/papers/251018866
- **Sleep-time Compute (Lin et al., Letta/UC Berkeley, 2025)**: Lets the model 'think' offline about a stored context between queries (anticipate questions, precompute inferences) so test-time work shrinks. The closest academic analogue to periodic 'dreaming'. Memory model: Input: a persistent context (document or state). Offline step: the model writes derived inferences, summaries and likely answers back into context. Online: queries use the enriched context. Applicable to ClawCode: Use as the decision rule for what dreaming should precompute: contexts with predictable questions (project facts, long documents queried repeatedly, standing preferences, recurring tasks) benefit; one-off topics do not. Track per-memory hit counts and only spend dream tokens on contexts that are re-queried. Dream cost is amortised across queries, so schedule it against measured reuse, not on a fixed cron alone. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.13171
- **FadeMem (Wei et al., Jan 2026)**: Biologically inspired adaptive forgetting for agent memory: dual-layer store, importance-modulated stretched-exponential decay, and LLM-based conflict resolution and fusion. Memory model: Long-term layer (capacity 1,000, slow decay, shape beta 0.8) and short-term layer (500, fast decay, beta 1.2); strength v(t)=v0*exp(-lambda*(t-tau)^beta) with lambda=lambda_base*exp(-mu*I) (lambda_base 0.1); importance I = relevance + access frequency + recency; promote at 0.7, demote at 0.3 (hysteresis); access reinforces strength. Write: for new memories above a cosine threshold an LLM labels pairs compatible, contradictory, subsumes or subsumed; contradictions suppress the older memory; similar memories (cosine above 0.75) in a time window are fused by an LLM with a preservation check. Applicable to ClawCode: Take the structure, not the claim that decay helps: hysteresis between tiers (promote and demote thresholds apart) avoids flapping; access-based reinforcement is the same as MemoryBank/MemoryOS counters; LLM pair classification (compatible, contradicts, subsumes) restricted to cosine-near neighbours is a sound way to detect supersession during dreaming (cost: one call per near-duplicate pair, so threshold the candidates). Use decay only to rank archive candidates, never to delete. The fusion step is the high-value, high-risk piece: keep sources linked and reversible. Sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2601.18642v2
- **Recursive Self-Pruning Memory, RSPM, 'Let Them Sleep' (UC Santa Cruz, Oct 2026)**: Sleep cycles that cluster recent failures, distil each cluster into one constraint, delete the raw episodes that produced it, apply temporal decay, and promote constraints from instance to category to universal level after 3 and 5 confirmations. Memory model: Cycle every 10 tasks or on a failure-rate spike. Error detection by domain: failure clustering (software tasks), an LLM conflict detector (temporal consistency), failure association (QA, assumes the memory involved in a failure caused it). Half-life W retires old items; raw episodes are deleted after distillation. Applicable to ClawCode: Supports dreaming as a consolidation mechanism, with two warnings that apply directly: (1) do not delete raw episodes after distilling; move them to a cold tier so historical-state questions and bad distillations are recoverable; (2) failure-association (blaming the memory that was in context) misfires when the task was simply hard; attribute blame only with a counterfactual or repeat signal. Use the multi-confirmation promotion rule (3 contexts, then 5) as a cheap guard for promoting lessons to core memory. Sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2610.25561
- **2026 forgetting and pruning papers: graph-based selective forgetting, FSFM, forgetting-by-design**: Three recent attempts to make importance-scored pruning a first-class memory operation: a typed-graph memory with periodic pruning (Aug 2026), a taxonomy plus scoring framework from China Mobile (Apr 2026), and a forgetting-policy benchmark (Dec 2025). Memory model: Graph paper: nodes score = 0.35 recency (90-day half-life) + 0.25 log frequency + 0.20 log degree centrality + 0.20 turns-based decay (1,000-turn half-life); every 400 turns nodes below 0.10 are pruned with incident edges; weights set a priori. FSFM: importance = 0.4 CQA + 0.3 BVE + 0.2 TRS + 0.1 SRC with safety penalties (-10 dangerous, -2 sensitive); at 70% capacity prune the lowest 10% per batch; decay lambda 0.05 long-term, 0.2 transient. Forgetful but Faithful: six policies (FIFO, LRU, Priority Decay, Reflection-Summary, Random-Drop, Hybrid) on a typed-node memory. Applicable to ClawCode: Conclusion for design: pruning is a storage and latency lever, and in the one controlled study with significance tests it did not change accuracy at a 9.8% prune rate. Budget the archive tier by size, move pruned rows to cold storage instead of deleting, and run a random-pruning control when tuning thresholds. Keep verbatim text alongside any extracted facts; the graph paper's loss of verbatim recall (0.607 vs 0.911) is the cost of replacing text with entities. Sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2608.28978v1, https://arxiv.org/html/2604.20300, https://arxiv.org/html/2512.12856v1
- **REALM, retrieval-driven memory reconsolidation (SJTU/OPPO, Sep 2026)**: After each retrieval and task-feedback step, the agent proposes edits to the memory graph topology (add, strengthen or weaken edges with confidences) so memory reorganises through use. Memory model: Graph memory; only edge weights are changed (bounded to [w_min, 1], step scaled by confidence), memory content is untouched; runs once per question; adaptive retrieval strategy selects subgraph beam search or path search. Applicable to ClawCode: The relevant idea is cheap link-weight learning: keep `memory_links.weight`, bump it when two memories are retrieved together and the answer cites both, decay it when retrieved but unused. This can be done with SQL updates and no LLM calls, unlike REALM's LLM-proposed edits. Expect small gains (about 2 points in the ablation); only worthwhile if A-Mem-style links are already adopted. Sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2609.16053
- **MemCon (Jiang et al., Jul 2026, v3 Oct 2026)**: A learned controller that wraps any memory backend and decides when and how much to retrieve, when to inject a distilled plan, when to re-retrieve with alternate queries, and when to consolidate or forget, treated as a contextual bandit over task success. Memory model: Tabular contextual bandit with UCB exploration; context features include goal type, step phase, stuck flag, memory size, plan availability and cold/warm phase; action space of 6 to 9 operations (Retrieve with top_k, PlanInject, Re-Retrieve, Consolidate, Forget, NoOp). Forget and Consolidate only call the backend's own maintenance hooks. No extra LLM calls for the controller. Applicable to ClawCode: A lightweight way to tune retrieval depth and whether to inject core memory per task type without fine-tuning: keep per-task-type counters of success under each (k, include_lessons) arm and pick by UCB. Needs a binary success signal and some tens of tasks per arm. Evidence for its forget/consolidate actions is missing, so use it for retrieval control only. Sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2607.13591
- **Hindsight (Vectorize.io, Virginia Tech, Washington Post; Dec 2025)**: Agent memory with four logical networks and three operations (retain, recall, reflect), including evolving opinions with confidence scores. Memory model: Networks: world facts, agent experiences, opinions (confidence in [0,1]), and observations (preference-neutral entity summaries). Retain: extract narrative facts with temporal ranges, resolve entities, build graph links, update opinions. Recall (TEMPR): four parallel channels (vector, BM25, graph spreading activation, temporal), Reciprocal Rank Fusion, cross-encoder rerank, token-budget packing. Opinion updates: reinforce +alpha, weaken -alpha, contradict -2 alpha, neutral 0, clamped. Backend PostgreSQL with pgvector. Applicable to ClawCode: The retrieval recipe is directly portable and local: FTS5 (BM25), sqlite-vec, entity table and a time-range filter fused with Reciprocal Rank Fusion in SQL, plus a local cross-encoder reranker if latency allows. Separating facts from opinions/inferences and storing a confidence that moves on reinforcement and contradiction is a cheap way to keep dream-generated inferences from masquerading as facts. Treat the benchmark numbers as vendor-reported. Sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2512.12818v1
- **Anthropic Dreams (Claude Managed Agents, research preview, May 2026)**: Official, productised offline consolidation: an asynchronous job reads an existing memory store plus 1 to 100 past session transcripts and writes a new reorganised memory store (duplicates merged, stale or contradicted entries replaced, new insights added). Memory model: Input store is never modified; output is a separate store you attach or discard; optional natural-language `instructions` (4,096 chars) steer synthesis but cannot make line-level edits; edits go through the Memory Stores API on the output; runs minutes to hours; billed at normal token rates for the chosen model, cost roughly linear in sessions; failed or canceled runs leave a partial output store. Applicable to ClawCode: Copy the safety shape: a dream writes to a new memory snapshot (or new rows under a `dream_run_id`) with the previous state untouched; present a diff; swap the active pointer only on approval (or auto-apply with rollback); record token usage per dream; cap the number of sessions per dream. Notes: instructions steer synthesis, not edits, so expose targeted edits through a separate path. Sources: https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/dreams, https://claude.com/blog/new-in-claude-managed-agents
- **LongMemEval (Wu et al., ICLR 2025) with its design findings**: 500-question benchmark over scalable chat histories testing information extraction, multi-session reasoning, temporal reasoning, knowledge updates and abstention; the paper also tests index, retrieve and read design choices. Memory model: Benchmark plus a design framework: indexing granularity (session vs round vs fact), key expansion, time-aware query expansion, and reading strategy. Applicable to ClawCode: The most transferable, cheap, measured retrieval upgrades for SQLite: index per round/turn (not per session), append extracted facts to the FTS5 key text, parse temporal expressions in the query and filter by timestamp range before ranking, and use a structured read prompt (notes first, then answer). Treat as the first things to build before any graph or consolidation work. Sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2410.10813, https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24579, https://dev.to/penfieldlabs/we-audited-locomo-64-of-the-answer-key-is-wrong-and-the-judge-accepts-up-to-63-of-intentionally-33lg
- **MemoryAgentBench (Hu, Wang, McAuley, 2025)**: Benchmark converting long-context datasets into incremental multi-turn form to test four competencies: accurate retrieval, test-time learning, long-range understanding and selective forgetting (FactConsolidation, built from MQuAKE counterfactual edits). Memory model: Evaluates long-context models, BM25 and embedding RAG, structure-augmented RAG (RAPTOR, GraphRAG, MemoRAG, HippoRAG-v2, Mem0, Cognee, Zep) and agentic memory (Self-RAG, MemGPT, MIRIX). Applicable to ClawCode: Use FactConsolidation-style tests as a regression suite for the dreaming and supersession logic. It shows that appending facts and retrieving by similarity does not resolve contradictions, so ClawCode needs an explicit supersession step with timestamps (newest valid fact wins) instead of relying on retrieval or the reader. Sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2507.05257
- **MemoryArena (He et al., Feb 2026)**: Benchmark of interdependent multi-session agentic tasks (bundled web shopping 150 tasks, group travel planning 270, progressive web search 256, formal reasoning 60) where later sessions depend on earlier actions. Memory model: Compares long-context, memory agents (Letta, Mem0, Mem0-g, ReasoningBank, Mirix) and RAG (BM25, text-embedding-3-small, MemoRAG, GraphRAG) with GPT-5.1-mini as task agent. Applicable to ClawCode: Relevant because ClawCode agents act over multiple sessions. Passive-recall scores (LoCoMo-style) do not predict task success; build an internal eval where a later session must use a decision or artifact from an earlier one (for example, reuse a file path, a user constraint, a prior tool result). Keep a full-history or recent-turns baseline in the comparison. Sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2602.16313v1, https://arxiv.org/html/2603.07670v1
- **HaluMem and EvalMem (operation-level diagnostics, Nov 2025 and Sep 2026)**: Benchmarks that score memory systems by operation (extraction, update, retrieval, answer) rather than end-to-end QA, to locate where errors enter. Memory model: HaluMem: memory extraction, memory updating, memory question answering over about 15k memory points, with a 1M-plus token Long variant. EvalMem: encoding, retrieval, generation and update/state-change (DynaMem-Bench) across seven systems (TiMem, O-Mem, EverMemOS, MemOS, MemBox, MemoryOS, GAM). Applicable to ClawCode: Instrument ClawCode per operation: log extraction precision and recall against a small hand-labelled set of agent sessions, update correctness for contradicted facts, and retrieval hit rate separately, so a drop can be traced to extraction, update or retrieval. Cheap mitigation from EvalMem: a recall-first second retrieval pass (rewrite the query, widen k) on low-confidence queries. Sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2511.03506v2, https://arxiv.org/html/2609.22231
- **ForgetEval and control-plane placement (Yang, Jun 2026)**: Benchmark for forgetting operations (supersede, release, purge) across thirteen memory configurations, studying where an LLM sits: in the recall path, at write ('inscribe') time, or at mutation time. Memory model: Deterministic primitives (lexical and temporal) versus an LLM at inscribe time versus an LLM hook at mutation time. Scored by deterministic substring match on 1,000 templated plus 385 adversarial cases. Applicable to ClawCode: Supports a two-layer supersession design: deterministic matching (entity key, timestamps, FTS5 exact/prefix) for the cheap cases, plus a Claude call only on the mutation path (when a candidate supersede or delete is proposed) to decide intent. Keep the LLM off the recall path to preserve latency. Cost is small because mutations are rare relative to reads. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15903
- **Benchmark reliability: LoCoMo audit, vendor benchmarks, cross-paper baselines**: Evidence about how trustworthy published memory numbers are. Memory model: Not a memory system. Covers LoCoMo (the dominant benchmark), the Mem0 paper's comparisons, DolphinBench (Mem0-authored), and Zep's earlier critique. Applicable to ClawCode: Do not use published LoCoMo deltas smaller than a few points to pick a design. Build a ClawCode-specific eval from real agent sessions (questions with deterministic answer checks, contradiction and update cases, multi-session task reuse), include full-context and plain hybrid retrieval baselines, and report cost and latency next to accuracy. With Claude's large context and prompt caching, full-history-in-context is a legitimate baseline for small stores. Sources: https://dev.to/penfieldlabs/we-audited-locomo-64-of-the-answer-key-is-wrong-and-the-judge-accepts-up-to-63-of-intentionally-33lg, https://arxiv.org/html/2504.19413, https://arxiv.org/html/2609.24971, https://arxiv.org/html/2508.03341, https://arxiv.org/html/2510.18866v4
- **Zep/Graphiti and MemGPT (context, baselines)**: The two systems most papers compare against. MemGPT (2023, older) pages memory between a prompt-resident main context and external recall and archival stores through self-issued function calls. Zep's Graphiti (Jan 2025, vendor paper) keeps a temporally aware knowledge graph that preserves historical relationships. Memory model: MemGPT: main context (system instructions, working context, FIFO queue), memory-pressure warning at about 70% of the window and eviction with recursive summaries at about 100%, external stores searched via function calls. Zep: temporal knowledge graph merging conversational and structured data. Applicable to ClawCode: Zep's useful idea is bi-temporal validity (when a fact was true vs when it was recorded) with edge invalidation instead of deletion, which maps to `valid_from`, `valid_to`, `superseded_by` columns in SQLite and directly addresses the update failures measured above. MemGPT's memory-pressure warning maps to the Agent SDK context budget: when a session nears the window, trigger a flush-and-summarise step into the episodic tier. Sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2310.08560, https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.13956, https://arxiv.org/html/2511.03506v2, https://arxiv.org/html/2507.05257
- **Experience stores: Agent Workflow Memory and Dynamic Cheatsheet (2024 and 2025)**: Procedural/strategy memory learned at test time. AWM induces reusable workflows from past trajectories (offline or online). Dynamic Cheatsheet lets a black-box model keep and curate a concise notes file across queries without labels. Memory model: AWM: workflow library induced from trajectories and supplied to the agent. DC: a self-curated cheatsheet of strategies and code snippets updated after each query, no ground truth needed. Applicable to ClawCode: These support a small, curated procedural tier (recipes, scripts, tool-use patterns) that Claude maintains itself, which is cheap and relevant to coding agents. Gains are largest when tasks repeat with structure; guard with the experience-following utility tracking so a bad recipe is retired after repeated failures. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.07429, https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07952, https://arxiv.org/html/2509.25140
- **Surveys, governance and safety of evolving memory (2025 to 2026)**: Framing and risk papers: 'Memory in the Age of AI Agents' (forms, functions, dynamics), 'Memory for Autonomous LLM Agents' (write-manage-read loop), SSGM governance framework, 'Always-On Agents' survey, and MINJA memory injection attack. Memory model: Survey taxonomy: forms (token-level, parametric, latent), functions (factual, experiential, working), dynamics (formation, evolution including local consolidation, cluster-level fusion, global integration, retrieval); forgetting as time-based, frequency-based or importance-driven. SSGM: decouple memory evolution from execution; run consistency verification, temporal decay and access control before any consolidation. Du survey proposes an unevaluated dual-buffer with probation and promotion. MINJA: an attacker writes malicious records into agent memory through ordinary queries. Applicable to ClawCode: Governance items that cost little in SQLite: provenance (who or what wrote it, from which session and source), a probation state for dream-generated memories before they enter core memory, confidence and verification fields, an audit log and rollback of every dream, and sanitising or flagging memories derived from untrusted input (Discord users, OCR'd documents) so they cannot silently become instructions. Sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2512.13564, https://arxiv.org/html/2603.07670v1, https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.11768, https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30306, https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03704

#### Anthropic's own guidance and products for agent memory and context: context engineering, Claude Code memory (CLAUDE.md, auto memory MEMORY.md), Agent SDK sessions and compaction, the API memory tool plus context editing and compaction, Managed Agents memory stores and dreaming, Contextual Retrieval, prompt caching placement rules, agent and tool design posts. Researched 2026-10-25 from live docs and Anthropic posts; per-system source lists use bracket numbers.

- Treat context as a scarce attention budget: aim for the smallest set of high-signal tokens, not the largest window. Evidence: Recall and accuracy degrade as tokens grow (context rot); Claude Code best practices say performance degrades as the window fills and tell users to prune ruthlessly; 1M windows exist but more context is not automatically better. Source: https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-context-engineering-for-ai-agents ; https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/context-windows ; https://code.claude.com/docs/en/best-practices.md
- Stable prefix rule: order requests tools, then system, then session-stable project context, then conversation; keep the prefix byte-identical across turns and sessions. Evidence: Prefix matching requires 100% identical content up to the breakpoint; Claude Code postmortems cite a timestamp in the static prompt, non-deterministic tool ordering, and tool parameter changes as cache breakers; Claude Code layer table places CLAUDE.md, auto memory and unscoped rules in a project-context layer that changes only at session start, /clear or /compact. Source: https://claude.com/blog/lessons-from-building-claude-code-prompt-caching-is-everything ; https://code.claude.com/docs/en/prompt-caching.md ; https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/prompt-caching
- Dynamic memory goes after the cache breakpoint, in the message stream, never in the system prompt; deliver updates as tagged messages. Evidence: Claude Code inserts system-reminder blocks into the next user message or tool result for time, changed files and mode changes; API docs say do not put the breakpoint on a block that changes every request or you pay a write each time and never read; SDK moves per-user auto memory text into the first user message with excludeDynamicSections for cross-session cache sharing. Source: https://claude.com/blog/lessons-from-building-claude-code-prompt-caching-is-everything ; https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/prompt-caching ; https://code.claude.com/docs/en/agent-sdk/modifying-system-prompts
- Never add, remove or reorder tools, and never switch models, mid-session; defer instead of removing. Evidence: Changing tool definitions invalidates tools, system and messages caches; per-model caches mean a Haiku question inside a long Opus session costs more than letting Opus answer; deferred tools load as tool_reference blocks so the prefix is untouched; switching effort or model forces a full uncached read in Claude Code. Source: https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/agents-and-tools/tool-use/tool-use-with-prompt-caching ; https://claude.com/blog/lessons-from-building-claude-code-prompt-caching-is-everything ; https://code.claude.com/docs/en/prompt-caching.md
- Always-loaded memory is a short index, not the content: cap it (MEMORY.md 200 lines or 25KB, one line per entry) and load bodies on demand. Evidence: Claude Code loads only the first 200 lines or 25KB of MEMORY.md, warns near the limit with 'one line per entry, move detail to topic files, merge or drop stale', errors over it, and reads topic files with normal file tools when needed; CLAUDE.md target is under 200 lines because longer files reduce adherence. Source: https://code.claude.com/docs/en/memory.md
- Only persist what cannot be re-derived, and classify it (user, feedback, project, reference). Evidence: Claude Code auto memory skips anything derivable from the codebase or already in CLAUDE.md and records type in frontmatter; best-practices exclude table lists anything Claude can figure out from code, standard conventions, frequently changing info and file-by-file descriptions. Source: https://code.claude.com/docs/en/memory.md ; https://code.claude.com/docs/en/best-practices.md
- Retrieve just in time through tools that return lightweight references first, with metadata (path, type, timestamp) that lets the agent decide what to open. Evidence: Context-engineering post: keep file paths, queries and links and load with tools at runtime; folder structure, names and timestamps signal relevance; hybrid (small upfront load plus exploration) usually works best, at the cost of slower runtime exploration. Source: https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-context-engineering-for-ai-agents
