- Durable rules must live in files that are re-injected after compaction, not in the conversation or initial prompt. Evidence: Project-root CLAUDE.md and auto memory are re-read from disk after compaction; instructions given only in conversation are lost; nested CLAUDE.md and path-scoped rules reload only on demand; SDK docs say persistent rules belong in CLAUDE.md because the system prompt snapshot is reused until compaction and early conversation instructions are summarized away. Source: https://code.claude.com/docs/en/context-window.md ; https://code.claude.com/docs/en/agent-sdk/agent-loop ; https://code.claude.com/docs/en/agent-sdk/modifying-system-prompts
- Compaction is lossy: steer it, tune for recall first, clear stale tool results before summarizing, flush important facts to memory before it fires, and compact at natural breaks while the cache is warm. Evidence: Context-engineering post: maximize recall then prune for precision and use tool-result clearing as the lightest form; context editing warns Claude before clearing so it can save to memory; custom instructions replace the default summary prompt entirely; images, documents and fetched URLs in summarized messages are lost; compacting after a cache-cold break reprocesses the full history uncached. Source: https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-context-engineering-for-ai-agents ; https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/context-editing ; https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/compaction-on-demand ; https://code.claude.com/docs/en/prompt-caching.md
- Keep an append-only, durable record of everything that happened outside the context window, and make context shaping a reversible transformation of it. Evidence: Managed Agents session is an append-only log read by positional slices; the harness applies compaction, trimming and cache-aware ordering on the way in; compacted messages stay recoverable because they remain in the log; Claude Code deletes old transcripts after cleanupPeriodDays (memory dir excluded), so mirror transcripts if you need them later. Source: https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/managed-agents ; https://code.claude.com/docs/en/memory.md ; https://code.claude.com/docs/en/agent-sdk/sessions
- For multi-window work, externalize state in structured files (JSON for statuses, freeform notes for progress, git for checkpoints) and start sessions with a prescribed read-state routine; verify before marking done. Evidence: Effective harnesses: initializer plus progress file plus JSON feature list where only the passes field is editable, session start routine, one feature at a time; prompting docs recommend JSON for structured state, text for notes, git for checkpoints and consider a fresh window over compaction because current models discover state from the filesystem. Source: https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-harnesses-for-long-running-agents ; https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/prompt-engineering/claude-prompting-best-practices
- Re-test harness assumptions every model generation; scaffolding for a weakness (context resets, sprint structures, evaluators) can become dead weight. Evidence: Context resets fixed context anxiety on Sonnet 4.5 but Opus 4.5 largely lost the behavior and Opus 4.6 let the team remove sprint scaffolding; the full harness cost over 20x the solo run on one task. Source: https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/managed-agents ; https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/harness-design-long-running-apps
- Make memory many small, focused, path-addressed files with hard caps, and scope stores by owner and lifecycle. Evidence: Managed Agents limits each memory to 100 kB (about 25k tokens) and 10,000 memories per store, up to 8 stores per session, and recommends focused stores (per user, shared domain, per project) and read_only mounts for reference; the API memory tool suggests capping file size and view output and expiring unused files. Source: https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/memory ; https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/agents-and-tools/tool-use/memory-tool
- Consolidation runs asynchronously, off the hot path, and is non-destructive: produce a new store from old store plus transcripts, review or auto-apply, keep the original for rollback. Evidence: Dreams read a store plus 1 to 100 sessions and write a separate output store; the input is never modified; you attach the output or discard it; instructions steer synthesis but line edits do nothing; cost scales with transcripts so start with a small batch. Source: https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/dreams
- Version every memory write and guard concurrent edits with a content-hash precondition; provide redaction. Evidence: Every mutation creates an immutable memory version (30-day retention, recent versions of live memories always kept), update accepts content_sha256 precondition, redact scrubs history for compliance, rollback is rewriting an old version. Source: https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/memory
- Persistent memory is a prompt-injection persistence channel; gate writes and separate trust levels. Evidence: Docs warn that a successful injection on a read_write store writes content that later sessions read as trusted memory, and recommend read_only for reference material and any store the agent does not need to modify; the memory tool docs make validation, size caps and sensitive-data stripping the developer's responsibility. Source: https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/memory ; https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/agents-and-tools/tool-use/memory-tool
- Retrieval quality for documents: contextualize chunks, search with both embeddings and BM25, rerank, pass about 20 chunks, and skip RAG when the corpus is under about 200k tokens. Evidence: Top-20 failure rate 5.7% to 3.7% (contextual embeddings), 2.9% (plus contextual BM25), 1.9% (plus reranking); 20 chunks beat 5 or 10; under 200,000 tokens just put the corpus in the prompt with caching; generic document summaries added little. Source is Sep 2024 and used Gemini and Voyage embeddings. Source: https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/contextual-retrieval
- Few consolidated, namespaced tools that return high-signal, token-bounded, human-readable results with actionable errors; evaluate them like code. Evidence: Writing tools for agents: consolidate multi-step operations, namespace, prefer names over UUIDs, offer concise vs detailed (about 72 vs 206 tokens in the example), paginate and truncate (Claude Code caps responses at 25,000 tokens), run realistic multi-call evals tracking accuracy, tokens, calls and errors. Source: https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/writing-tools-for-agents
- Defer the long tail of tool definitions and keep 3 to 5 core tools always loaded. Evidence: Tool search cut about 77K tokens of definitions to about 8.7K and raised MCP eval accuracy (Opus 4 49% to 74%, Opus 4.5 79.5% to 88.1%); use at 10 or more tools or over 10k tokens; selection accuracy degrades beyond 30 to 50 tools; deferral preserves the prompt cache. Source: https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/advanced-tool-use ; https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/agents-and-tools/tool-use/tool-search-tool
- Use sub-agents to isolate noisy work and return short summaries; they do not inherit the parent's memory, so pass what they need. Evidence: Sub-agents return 1,000 to 2,000 token digests per the context-engineering post; non-fork Claude Code subagents get a fresh context with CLAUDE.md but not the main session's auto memory, history or output style, and have a separate cache TTL bucket; multi-agent runs cost about 15x chat tokens, so use them only when an eval justifies it. Source: https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-context-engineering-for-ai-agents ; https://code.claude.com/docs/en/sub-agents.md ; https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/multi-agent-research-system
- Put long retrieved documents at the top of the user turn, structured with XML source metadata, and the question at the end. Evidence: Prompting best practices: long-form data first, queries at the end can improve quality by up to 30% in Anthropic tests (especially multi-document inputs); wrap each in document, source and document_content tags. Source: https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/prompt-engineering/claude-prompting-best-practices
- Context is guidance, not enforcement; use hooks for anything that must always happen, and monitor cache hit rate as an operational metric. Evidence: Claude Code docs: CLAUDE.md is context not enforced config and hooks are deterministic; Claude Code engineers alert on cache hit rate like uptime; the API reports cache_read and cache_creation tokens per response. Source: https://code.claude.com/docs/en/memory.md ; https://claude.com/blog/lessons-from-building-claude-code-prompt-caching-is-everything ; https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/prompt-caching
- Start simple and add complexity only when it measurably improves outcomes. Evidence: Building effective agents core advice: find the simplest solution possible and only increase complexity when needed; call LLM APIs directly before adopting frameworks; context-engineering post repeats 'do the simplest thing that works' as models improve (Dec 2024 and Sep 2025 sources). Source: https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/building-effective-agents ; https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-context-engineering-for-ai-agents

Anti-patterns: Putting timestamps, per-request retrieved memories, or per-agent paths into the static system prompt or ahead of the cache breakpoint; every request then writes a new cache entry and never reads one.; Changing tool definitions, adding or removing tools, reordering tools non-deterministically, or toggling tool_choice, images or thinking settings between turns of a cached session.; Switching models mid-session for a cheap side question (per-model caches make it costlier); use a sub-agent with a handoff instead.; Editing CLAUDE.md or the memory index mid-session and expecting the running session to see it; it applies only after /clear, /compact or restart (and it does not break the cache, which is the point).; Letting the always-loaded memory index or charter grow without a cap; MEMORY.md content past 200 lines or 25KB is silently dropped at next load, and over-long CLAUDE.md files reduce adherence ('the over-specified CLAUDE.md').; Saving facts that can be derived from the code or documents, duplicates, or frequently changing details; this is exactly what Claude Code's auto memory is told to skip.; Keeping persistent rules only in the initial prompt or in conversation; they vanish at compaction. Likewise relying on nested CLAUDE.md or path-scoped rules for must-survive instructions.; Treating compaction as free: running it cold after a long idle gap (full uncached reprocess), using a custom compaction prompt with a different system prompt or no tools (no shared prefix, full price), or forgetting a cache breakpoint at the end of the system prompt so a new summary invalidates it.; Irreversible pruning of context with no durable log; compacted or cleared content should remain recoverable from a stored session log.; Running consolidation (dreaming) as an in-place rewrite of live memory, expecting line-level imperative edits to work, or running it over unbounded transcript batches; Anthropic's design writes a new store, takes high-level instructions, and recommends small batches first.; Giving read_write memory access to sessions that process untrusted input (Discord messages, OCR'd documents, fetched web pages) with no provenance or review; injected text becomes trusted memory for every later session.; No versioning, no hash precondition, and no redaction path for memory edits, so concurrent writers (agent turn, dream job, human) clobber each other and leaked data cannot be scrubbed from history.; Dumping large retrieval results into context (huge top-K, UUID-heavy payloads, unbounded tool outputs) instead of concise-by-default responses with pagination and token caps; Claude Code itself caps tool results at 25,000 tokens.; Wrapping every API endpoint as a separate tool or shipping overlapping tools; selection accuracy degrades past 30 to 50 tools and definitions can reach tens of thousands of tokens before work starts.; Adding generic document summaries to chunks and expecting retrieval gains (the Contextual Retrieval experiments found very limited benefit), or applying RAG at all to a corpus under about 200k tokens that could simply be cached in the prompt.; Defaulting to multi-agent or evaluator layers: multi-agent runs cost about 15x chat tokens and one harness cost over 20x a solo run; add them only where an eval shows gains, and re-test them each model generation.; Marking long-task steps complete without verification, or letting the agent edit the test or checklist definitions; keep only a status field editable and verify end to end first.; Relying on vendor customer numbers as evidence (Rakuten 97% fewer first-pass errors, Harvey about 6x completion, 39% memory plus context editing uplift): none publish baselines, sample sizes or methods, so run your own before/after evals on ClawCode tasks.; Assuming SDK defaults without checking: settingSources omitted loads user, project and local settings, and auto memory loads regardless of settingSources; agents that share a cwd share one auto memory directory unless autoMemoryDirectory is set per agent.

Systems reviewed:

- **Effective context engineering for AI agents (Anthropic Applied AI, Sep 29, 2025) and the platform docs that restate it**: Anthropic's framing post. Context is a finite attention budget with diminishing returns ('context rot'); the goal is the smallest set of high-signal tokens that gets the behavior you want [1][2]. It names four long-horizon techniques: just-in-time retrieval via lightweight references, compaction, structured note-taking (agentic memory), and sub-agents [1]. Memory model: No storage layer; it is a set of rules. WRITES: the agent writes notes outside the window (NOTES.md or a to-do list) and reloads them after resets; Claude playing Pokemon kept tallies and maps this way across context resets [1]. READS: keep identifiers (file paths, queries, links) and load content with tools only when needed; folder layout, file names and timestamps act as retrieval signals; best results are hybrid, with a small amount loaded up front (Claude Code loads CLAUDE.md up front, then uses glob and grep) and the rest explored just in time. Tradeoff: runtime exploration is slower and needs good heuristics [1]. FORGETTING: compaction summarizes near the limit; tune the summary prompt for recall first, then trim for precision; the lightest form is clearing old tool results; Claude Code's compaction keeps architectural decisions, unresolved bugs, implementation details and the five most recently accessed files [1]. Sub-agents work in clean windows (may burn tens of thousands of tokens) and hand back a condensed 1,000 to 2,000 token summary [1]. System prompts should sit at the 'right altitude' (not brittle if-else logic, not vague); start minimal, add instructions only for observed failures; use a few diverse canonical examples rather than a pile of edge-case rules [1]. Choose compaction for long conversational flow, note-taking for iterative work with milestones, multi-agent for parallel research [1]. Docs add: 1M context windows exist on current models but 'more context isn't automatically better' [2]. Applicable to ClawCode: COPY: (1) Treat the SQLite store as the 'outside the window' memory and give the agent tools to query it, not a bulk dump: keep a tiny always-loaded index of lightweight references (memory id, title, type, updated date) and let the agent pull bodies via memory_get / memory_search. (2) Use metadata the post calls out as retrieval signals: path-like names, type tags, and timestamps on every memory so the agent can judge relevance before opening anything. (3) Keep ClawCode's system prompt minimal and failure-driven; resist adding memory rules until an eval shows a miss. (4) Route heavy reads (OCR'd document search, transcript mining) to a sub-agent that returns a 1,000 to 2,000 token digest. COST: low; mostly discipline plus an index generator. Risk: just-in-time retrieval adds tool-call latency per Discord turn, so keep a small hot set (top few memories) pre-loaded per turn as the hybrid pattern recommends. Sources: [1] https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-context-engineering-for-ai-agents (published Sep 29, 2025, confirmed via search result metadata), [2] https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/context-windows, [3] https://code.claude.com/docs/en/best-practices.md
- **Claude Code memory: CLAUDE.md, auto memory (MEMORY.md plus topic files), rules, skills, and what survives compaction**: Two complementary mechanisms carry knowledge across sessions: CLAUDE.md (instructions you write) and auto memory (notes Claude writes about corrections, preferences and project context). Both load at the start of every session and are context, not enforcement; hooks are the enforcement path [1][3][4]. Memory model: STORAGE: CLAUDE.md files at managed, user (~/.claude/CLAUDE.md), project and local scope, concatenated broadest to most specific; subdirectory CLAUDE.md files and path-scoped rules load on demand when matching files are touched; CLAUDE.md content is delivered as a user message after the system prompt, not inside the system prompt [1]. Auto memory lives in ~/.claude/projects/<project>/memory/ (derived from the git repo, shared across worktrees; override with autoMemoryDirectory; disable with autoMemoryEnabled false or CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_AUTO_MEMORY=1) as a MEMORY.md index plus one topic file per memory [1]. MEMORY.md is the index, one line per memory. TYPES: frontmatter type is one of user, feedback, project, reference [1]. WRITES: Claude decides per session whether anything is worth saving; it skips anything derivable from the codebase and anything already in CLAUDE.md; Claude Code stamps a 'modified' ISO timestamp into frontmatter when it writes [1]. In the SDK the agent writes with ordinary Write and Edit tools, so those must be enabled [5]. READS: only the first 200 lines or 25KB of MEMORY.md (whichever first) loads at session start; topic files are read on demand with normal file tools; after a MEMORY.md write Claude Code warns near the limit (keep one line per entry, move detail to topic files, merge or drop stale) and returns an error if over, because everything past the limit is dropped on next load [1]. Non-fork subagents do not receive the main session's auto memory; a subagent can have its own memory dir via the memory frontmatter field (user, project or local scope) whose first 200 lines or 25KB are put in that subagent's system prompt [1][6]. FORGETTING/COMPACTION: project-root CLAUDE.md and unscoped rules are re-injected from disk after compaction; auto memory is re-injected from disk; up to five recently modified files are re-read (a file over 5,000 tokens comes back as a path reference only); invoked skill bodies are re-injected capped at 5,000 tokens each and 25,000 total, oldest dropped; path-scoped rules and nested CLAUDE.md reload only on demand; instructions given only in conversation are lost [2]. Editing CLAUDE.md mid-session does not apply until /clear, /compact or restart [7]. SIZE RULES: target under 200 lines per CLAUDE.md because longer files cost context and reduce adherence; @imports do not reduce cost; on-demand CLAUDE.md and rules files are truncated at 40,000 characters on 200K-window sessions (150,000 to 200,000 on 1M-window sessions) [1]; litmus test for each line: would removing it cause mistakes; include non-guessable commands, deviations from defaults, gotchas; exclude anything derivable from code, long tutorials, frequently changing info [3]. Skills: only name plus description (capped at 1,536 characters each) sit in context, SKILL.md under 500 lines, body loads on invocation [8]; per-feature context costs are tabulated in [4][9]. Applicable to ClawCode: COPY (high value, low cost): (1) A three-layer always-on stack per agent: charter (CLAUDE.md-equivalent, under 200 lines, durable rules and persona), memory index (MEMORY.md-equivalent generated from SQLite: one line per memory, hard cap 200 lines or 25KB, oldest or lowest-score pruned first), and everything else retrieved on demand. (2) Adopt the four memory types (user, feedback, project, reference) as a first-class column and map ClawCode's tiers onto them; add the 'do not save what is derivable from the code or already in the charter' write rule to the memory-write tool description. (3) Stamp a modified timestamp on every write and surface it in search results so the model can judge staleness. (4) Use the SDK's autoMemoryDirectory per agent if you let Claude Code's built-in auto memory run: by default all agents sharing a cwd or repo would share one memory dir; otherwise set CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_AUTO_MEMORY=1 and inject ClawCode's own index. (5) After compaction, re-inject: a SessionStart hook that matches the compact source can add context [2]; use it to re-send the index and the current hot memories. (6) Non-fork sub-agents get no auto memory [1]: pass the needed memories in the delegation prompt. (7) Add a prompt-audit style lint (contradictions, stale dates, oversize index, duplicate titles), similar to what /doctor prompt-audit does for instruction files [1]. COST: index generator and lint are small; the main cost is giving up in-session freshness of the index (it is frozen at session start, which is also what protects the cache). Open question: docs disagree on exactly where MEMORY.md content sits; the SDK page says auto memory is loaded into the system prompt [5], the caching page lists auto memory in the 'project context' layer after the system prompt [7]; either way it is frozen per session. Sources: [1] https://code.claude.com/docs/en/memory.md, [2] https://code.claude.com/docs/en/context-window.md, [3] https://code.claude.com/docs/en/best-practices.md, [4] https://code.claude.com/docs/en/features-overview.md, [5] https://code.claude.com/docs/en/agent-sdk/claude-code-features, [6] https://code.claude.com/docs/en/sub-agents.md
- **Claude Agent SDK: sessions, resume/fork, automatic compaction, and system prompt/CLAUDE.md loading**: The SDK embeds the Claude Code loop. A session is the accumulated conversation (prompt, every tool call and result, responses) written to disk automatically; continue, resume by ID, and fork are options on query() [1]. It auto-compacts near the context limit and exposes hooks around it [2]. Memory model: SESSION STORAGE: jsonl transcripts under ~/.claude/projects/<encoded-cwd>/ (or $CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR); sessions persist the conversation, not the filesystem; resume across hosts needs a SessionStore adapter (transcripts mirrored to your backend) or moving the file; the docs also say that not relying on resume and instead passing captured results into a fresh session's prompt is often more robust; persistSession:false is TypeScript only [1]. Transcripts are swept after cleanupPeriodDays (docs show 30 days default in the usage-data context) but the memory directory is excluded from that sweep [10][11]. PROMPT ASSEMBLY: SDK default is a minimal system prompt; claude_code preset gives the CLI prompt; append adds to it; a custom string replaces everything [3]. CLAUDE.md is injected into the conversation as project context, not the system prompt, controlled by settingSources; omitting settingSources is equivalent to user, project, local; auto memory loads regardless of settingSources; for multi-tenant isolation use settingSources [] plus CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_AUTO_MEMORY=1 [4]. (A third-party search summary claimed the SDK loads no filesystem settings by default; the current official page says the opposite, so set settingSources explicitly.) CACHING: the system prompt is recorded on a session's first request and reused until compaction, so changing append on resume does not take effect for the next turns; snapshot:false rebuilds every request but breaks the cache; the preset embeds the auto memory location in the system prompt so sessions in different directories cannot share a cache entry; excludeDynamicSections:true moves per-user context (often the whole auto memory section) into the first user message so identical configurations share one entry, at the cost of slightly less instruction weight; in TypeScript a systemPrompt array with SYSTEM_PROMPT_DYNAMIC_BOUNDARY splits static and per-request text into two separately cached blocks (only when calling the API directly or on Claude Platform on AWS) [3]. To change instructions mid-session, put them in the next user message or return additionalContext from a UserPromptSubmit or PostToolUse hook [3]. COMPACTION: automatic when approaching the limit; emits a compact_boundary system message; summaries drop early instructions so persistent rules belong in CLAUDE.md (re-injected), a 'summary instructions' section in CLAUDE.md steers the compactor, PreCompact hook (trigger manual or auto) can archive the full transcript [2]. Default auto-compact is near the full window (about 967K tokens on native 1M models, 200K on Sonnet or Opus 4.6 without extended context) and is tunable from 100K to 1M via autoCompactWindow or CLAUDE_CODE_AUTO_COMPACT_WINDOW [12]. Subagents return only their final response, keeping the parent's context lean [2]. Applicable to ClawCode: COPY: (1) Pin one session per agent-and-channel, resume by ID, and mirror transcripts into ClawCode's own store through the SessionStore adapter or a PreCompact hook, because dreaming needs transcripts that Claude Code will eventually delete and because Anthropic's own managed design treats the session log as the durable record [13]. (2) Put static agent text in append with excludeDynamicSections:true (shared preset cache across agents) and send everything dynamic (retrieved memories, time, channel context) in the user turn or via UserPromptSubmit additionalContext. If ClawCode uses a custom prompt in TypeScript, use the array-with-boundary form so the static block caches. (3) Never rely on editing append for a resumed session; use hooks. (4) Put durable rules and a 'when summarizing, always keep...' section in the charter file so they survive compaction. (5) Set settingSources explicitly and decide deliberately about auto memory (see previous entry). COST: low; mostly configuration. Risk: excludeDynamicSections trades a little instruction adherence for cache sharing; measure on your own evals. Cache TTL is covered in the prompt caching entry. Sources: [1] https://code.claude.com/docs/en/agent-sdk/sessions, [2] https://code.claude.com/docs/en/agent-sdk/agent-loop, [3] https://code.claude.com/docs/en/agent-sdk/modifying-system-prompts, [4] https://code.claude.com/docs/en/agent-sdk/claude-code-features, [10] https://code.claude.com/docs/en/memory.md, [11] https://code.claude.com/docs/en/costs.md
- **Memory tool (memory_20250818) with context editing and server-side compaction (Claude API)**: A client-side file-style memory interface: Claude issues view, create, str_replace, insert, delete and rename commands against a /memories prefix; your app executes them against storage you control [1]. Paired with context editing (clear old tool results and thinking blocks) and compaction (server-side summarization) to keep long runs alive [2][3][4]. Memory model: TOOL: {type:'memory_20250818', name:'memory'} is the whole config; no beta header needed for the tool itself; supported on Claude 4 and later [1]. The API auto-adds a system instruction: always view the memory directory first, record progress as you go, and 'assume interruption' because the context may reset at any moment [1]. COMMAND SEMANTICS: view lists 2 levels deep with sizes, or returns a file with 6-wide line numbers and optional view_range; Claude's tool description truncates text views over 16,000 characters so expect ranged re-reads; create (may overwrite), str_replace (must match uniquely, error lists duplicate line numbers), insert at line, delete (recursive; cannot delete root), rename (no overwrite) [1]. Developer duties: reject any path outside /memories (traversal, URL-encoded variants), cap file sizes and view output, expire stale files, optionally strip sensitive data; Claude usually refuses to write secrets [1]. If memory gets cluttered the docs suggest a prompt line: keep it up to date, coherent and organized, rename or delete stale files, do not create new files unless necessary [1]. MULTISESSION PATTERN: an initializer session creates a progress log, a feature checklist and a startup script reference; later sessions open by reading them and end by updating the log; one feature at a time, mark complete only after end-to-end verification [1]. CONTEXT EDITING (beta context-management-2025-06-27): clear_tool_uses_20250919 triggers at 100,000 input tokens by default, keeps the last 3 tool uses, supports clear_at_least (so each cache-invalidating clear is worth it), exclude_tools, clear_tool_inputs; clear_thinking_20251015 controls thinking retention; edits happen server-side while your client keeps the full history; when context nears the clearing threshold Claude gets a warning and can save to memory first [2]. COMPACTION: threshold form (compact-2026-01-12, type compact_20260112) default trigger 150,000 input tokens, minimum 50,000; custom instructions replace the default prompt entirely; pause_after_compaction lets you re-insert recent turns; add cache_control to the compaction block and a breakpoint at the end of the system prompt or a new summary can invalidate the cached system prompt; billing is the sum of usage.iterations [3]. On-demand form (compact-2026-09-04, compaction:{type:'summarize'}): you decide when, instructions up to 16,384 characters, images, documents and fetched URLs inside summarized messages are lost, and system messages in the summarized range stop applying [4]. Docs say memory preserves what must survive summarization while compaction keeps the active context small [1]. Applicable to ClawCode: COPY the interface, not the product: the Agent SDK uses ordinary file tools for auto memory rather than this API tool [5], so the transferable parts are the command semantics, safety rules and prompts. (1) Expose a 'memory' MCP tool with these six commands over a virtual /memories tree mapped to SQLite rows (path to row), so every write goes through one choke point that updates FTS5 and sqlite-vec, bumps a version row, enforces size caps and rejects bad paths. Reuse Anthropic's error strings. (2) Cap view output near 16,000 characters with view_range paging. (3) Pre-compaction flush: use a PreCompact hook to run a short 'save anything durable now' turn (this is the same idea as the context-editing warning; the flush turn itself is our design, not an Anthropic feature). (4) Reuse the initializer/progress-log pattern for multi-day agent tasks. (5) For any direct API jobs (ingestion contextualization, dreaming), use compaction with pause_after_compaction and instructions, and put the cache breakpoint at the end of the system prompt. COST: the MCP shim is a small build; the write choke point also gives you provenance and versioning for free. Risk: if you replace file tools with a custom memory tool you lose whatever the model learned about file-style memory in Claude Code; run an A/B. Sources: [1] https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/agents-and-tools/tool-use/memory-tool, [2] https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/context-editing, [3] https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/compaction-threshold, [4] https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/compaction-on-demand, [5] https://code.claude.com/docs/en/agent-sdk/claude-code-features, [6] https://claude.com/blog/context-management
- **Claude Managed Agents memory stores (public beta since Apr 23, 2026)**: Hosted, workspace-scoped stores of small text documents that mount into an agent's sandbox as a directory; the agent reads and writes them with the normal file tools, and every change is versioned [1][2]. Memory model: STORAGE: a memory store is a collection of path-addressed text documents; attached at session creation only (cannot be attached or removed mid-session), up to 8 stores per session, each with access read_write (default) or read_only enforced at the filesystem level [1]. Mounted under /mnt/memory/<slug>/; a short description of each mount (name, path, access mode, store description, and per-session instructions up to 4,096 characters) is auto-added to the system prompt so the agent knows where to look [1]. LIMITS: each memory max 100 kB (about 25k tokens); max 10,000 memories per store; docs say structure memory as many small focused files [1]. WRITES: agent file writes under the mount persist back; API create does not overwrite, update takes optional content_sha256 precondition for optimistic concurrency [1]. READS: list with path_prefix and depth, get by id; the agent reads files as it would any file [1]. VERSIONING: every mutation creates an immutable memory version (memver_); versions retained 30 days but recent versions of a live memory are always kept; redact scrubs a historical version's content while keeping the audit record (not allowed on the current head); no restore endpoint, you rewrite old content [1]. FORGETTING: you delete or condense; when a store hits 10,000 writes to new paths fail; recommended: focused stores per user, per shared domain, per project; prune before full; attach a fresh store and mount the old one read_only; limit write access [1]. SECURITY: docs warn a successful prompt injection on a read_write store writes malicious content that later sessions read as trusted memory, so use read_only for reference and for any store the agent need not modify [1]. Beta headers: agent-memory-2026-07-22 on memory-store calls, managed-agents-2026-04-01 on sessions, never both on a memory-store call [1]. Self-hosted sandboxes get a synced local copy (default 15 second sync interval) rather than a live mount [1]. Applicable to ClawCode: DO NOT migrate (it replaces the local SQLite, Discord and OCR stack and ties ClawCode to beta hosted APIs); DO copy the design: (1) scope stores by owner and lifecycle (per user, per project, shared reference, per agent) with an access mode of read_only or read_write attached per session, and tell the agent about each scope in the system prompt with name, description and when to use it. (2) Hard limits: about 25k tokens per memory and a cap on memories per scope, forcing many small focused memories. (3) Add a versions table (immutable rows, retention window, redact operation) and a content hash precondition on updates so concurrent writers (agent turn vs dream job vs human edit) never clobber each other. (4) Make anything derived from untrusted input (Discord users, OCR'd documents, web fetches) land in a separate lower-trust scope that is read-only to later sessions unless promoted by review. COST: versions table and ACL columns are cheap in SQLite; promotion review is the real operational cost. Sources: [1] https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/memory, [2] https://claude.com/blog/claude-managed-agents-memory (dated Apr 23, 2026 per search result)
- **Dreams: Managed Agents memory consolidation (research preview, May 2026)**: An asynchronous job that reads an existing memory store plus past session transcripts and writes a new, reorganized memory store: duplicates merged, stale or contradicted entries replaced with the latest value, new insights surfaced [1]. Anthropic's blog frames it as a scheduled between-sessions process that surfaces recurring mistakes, converged workflows and shared team preferences [2]. Memory model: INPUTS: one memory store and 1 to 100 session transcripts, plus optional instructions (max 4,096 characters) and a model (several Opus, Sonnet and Fable versions) [1]. PROCESS: runs as an underlying session you can stream; typically minutes to a few hours depending on transcript volume; statuses pending, running, completed, failed, canceled [1]. OUTPUT: a separate output memory store; the input store is never modified, so you can review and discard; you then attach the output store in place of the input (or archive/delete it) [1][3]. STEERING: instructions are high-level synthesis guidance (focus areas, content to preserve, output conventions); the pipeline is a synthesis pass, not a text editor, so line-level imperative edits ('change sentence X') generally produce no change; do targeted fixes with the Memory Stores API on the output store [1]. COST CONTROL: billed at standard token rates for the chosen model, cost scales roughly linearly with number and length of sessions; docs say start with a small batch [1]. FAILURE MODES: timeout, input store too large, input store or session archived or deleted mid-run causes failure; on failure or cancel the partial output store is left for inspection [1]. Control model: you can let dreaming update memory automatically or review before applying [2]. Claims about it converting relative dates to absolute dates appear only in secondary coverage I could not confirm in the primary docs. Requires access request; beta header dreaming-2026-04-21 in addition to managed-agents-2026-04-01 [1]. Memory docs recommend dreaming as the way to consolidate a store that is filling up, then switch sessions to the output store [3]. Applicable to ClawCode: ClawCode already has periodic dreaming; adopt these properties: (1) NON-DESTRUCTIVE: write the consolidated result to a staging snapshot (shadow tables or a new scope), never edit live rows; produce a diff (merged, replaced, dropped, added) and either auto-apply by tier policy or hold for human approval; keep the pre-dream snapshot for rollback. (2) INPUT BOUNDS: feed the existing memory plus a capped batch of recent transcripts (Anthropic caps at 100 sessions per run); start small and measure. (3) STEERING: per-agent high-level instructions (what to prioritize, what to preserve verbatim) rather than expecting line edits; handle specific fixes through the memory tool afterwards. (4) CONCURRENCY: use a content hash precondition so an agent write during a dream is not overwritten; fail the dream if its input vanished. (5) POISONING: a dream is a laundering risk, so keep provenance on each memory (source: user, agent, document, Discord user) and refuse to promote content whose only provenance is untrusted input into the trusted tier. (6) EVALS: Anthropic publishes no consolidation benchmark, so track your own: index token count, duplicate rate, contradiction rate, and retrieval hit rate on a fixed question set before and after each dream. COST: dream tokens scale with transcripts; use a stable cached instruction prefix and a cheaper model for the first pass; staging plus diff UI is the main engineering effort. Sources: [1] https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/dreams, [2] https://claude.com/blog/new-in-claude-managed-agents (dated May 19, 2026 per search result; release notes list research preview on May 6, 2026), [3] https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/memory, [4] https://claude.com/code-with-claude/session/sf-memory-and-dreaming-for-self-learning-agents, [5] https://claude.com/code-with-claude/session/ldn-ext-agents-that-remember
- **Contextual Retrieval (Anthropic engineering post, Sep 19, 2024: older source)**: A RAG ingestion technique: before embedding and before building the BM25 index, prepend a short chunk-specific context (50 to 100 tokens) generated by Claude from the whole document and the chunk, so isolated chunks keep their meaning [1]. Memory model: WRITES (ingest): split documents into chunks of a few hundred tokens; for each chunk call a model with the full document plus the chunk and the prompt to give a succinct context situating the chunk 'for the purposes of improving search retrieval'; prepend the output to the chunk; embed it (Contextual Embeddings) and index it in BM25 (Contextual BM25). Prompt caching of the document makes this cheap: about $1.02 per million document tokens one-time, assuming 800-token chunks, 8k-token documents, 50-token instructions and 100 tokens of generated context per chunk [1]. READS: run vector and BM25 search, merge and dedupe with rank fusion, optionally retrieve top 150 and rerank to top 20 (they tested a Cohere reranker); passing 20 chunks beat 5 or 10 [1]. SKIP RAG: if the knowledge base is under 200,000 tokens (about 500 pages), put it all in the prompt (with caching) instead [1]. Chunk boundaries, size and overlap matter; custom contextualizer prompts (for example with a glossary) may beat the generic one; generic document summaries added to chunks gave very limited gains; evaluate on your own data [1]. Applicable to ClawCode: COPY into the OCR/document ingestion path: (1) contextualize each chunk at ingest with a cheap model, cache the document prefix, and store both the raw chunk and the contextualized text; embed the contextualized text with bge-small and index it in FTS5 (FTS5 bm25() is the 'contextual BM25' half). (2) Fuse BM25 and vector ranks (reciprocal rank fusion) rather than picking one. (3) Add a local reranker stage for the top 50 to 150 candidates if latency allows (the reranker choice is not from Anthropic; the post only reports the benefit of reranking). (4) For small per-agent memory stores (notes, not documents) skip chunking and contextualization and consider simply loading the index into the stable prefix, per the 200k-token advice. (5) For transcript-derived snippets, add session id, date and topic as the 'context' line (our extrapolation). COST: one extra LLM call per chunk at ingest (Anthropic's cost figure is a pricing-era estimate, recompute with current model prices); re-contextualize when a document changes. CAVEAT: Anthropic's numbers used Gemini and Voyage embeddings; bge-small is a smaller local model, so absolute failure rates will differ. Build a 50 to 100 question eval first. Sources: [1] https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/contextual-retrieval, [2] https://assets.anthropic.com/m/1632cded0a125333/original/Contextual-Retrieval-Appendix-2.pdf (full experiment breakdown, referenced in search results, not read in full)
- **Prompt caching rules that decide where dynamic memory goes (API docs plus Claude Code engineering lessons)**: The cache is prefix-matched in the fixed order tools, then system, then messages; any change at one level invalidates that level and everything after it. Memory placement is therefore a cost and latency decision as much as a relevance one [1][2][3]. Memory model: RULES: (1) Order content from most static to most dynamic: static system prompt and tools, then project context (CLAUDE.md, auto memory, unscoped rules), then session context, then conversation messages; the layer table in Claude Code says project context changes only at session start, /clear or /compact [2][3]. (2) Matching requires 100% identical content up to the breakpoint; keep the prefix byte-stable; past breakages included a timestamp in the static system prompt, non-deterministic tool ordering, and changing parameters of a tool [3]. (3) Do not edit the system prompt to reflect new information; insert it as messages (Claude Code appends a system-reminder block to the next user message or tool result) [3]. (4) Never add or remove tools mid-session; model state transitions as tools (plan mode keeps all tools) and defer rarely used tools with defer_loading stubs, whose full schema arrives as a tool_reference in the conversation so the prefix is untouched [3][4][5]. (5) Do not switch models mid-session (per-model caches); if you must, use a sub-agent with a handoff message [3][2]. (6) Fork side computations (like compaction) from the parent's exact prefix; reserve a compaction buffer; the compaction call then costs about a tenth and yields roughly a 20k-token summary [3]. (7) Put the explicit breakpoint on the last block whose prefix is identical across requests, never on a block that changes every request (timestamps, per-request context, the incoming user message), or you pay a write on every request and never read [1]. MECHANICS: up to 4 explicit breakpoints plus optional automatic caching; lookback of 20 block positions; 5-minute default TTL refreshed on each hit, 1-hour TTL optional; write multipliers 1.25x (5m) and 2x (1h) of base input price, read 0.1x (0.05x on Opus 5.5 and Sonnet 5.5, 0.025x on Fable 5.1 and Mythos 5.1) [1]; minimum cacheable prefix 512 tokens (Sonnet 5.5, Opus 5.5, Haiku 5.5, Opus 5, Fable 5, Mythos 5), 1,024 (Sonnet 5, Sonnet 4.6, Sonnet 4.5, Opus 4.8, Opus 4.1, Opus 4, Sonnet 4), 2,048 (Opus 4.7, Haiku 3.5, Mythos Preview), 4,096 (Opus 4.6, Opus 4.5, Haiku 4.5); shorter prompts are silently not cached [1]. A cache entry exists only after the first response starts, so parallel requests should wait [1]. INVALIDATION TABLE: changing tool definitions invalidates everything; toggling web search or citations invalidates system and messages; changing tool_choice, adding or removing images, or changing thinking or effort parameters invalidates messages (and more on some models) [1][5]. Claude Code specifics: edits to CLAUDE.md mid-session neither invalidate nor apply until /clear, /compact or restart; skills, plan mode and output style changes append as messages and keep the cache; compaction replaces the conversation layer but system and project layers can stay cached; cache scope in Claude Code is effectively one machine and directory because the system prompt embeds memory paths; TTL: one hour only on subscriptions by default, 5 minutes on API keys unless promptCacheTtl or CLAUDE_CODE_PROMPT_CACHE_TTL is set to 1h (v2.1.242+), subagents and compaction use a separate bucket (5 minutes by default) [2]. Applicable to ClawCode: Recommended layout (our inference from these rules): TOOLS: fixed order, memory and doc tools non-deferred, long-tail MCP tools deferred; never reorder or toggle per turn. SYSTEM (cached): static identity and memory protocol, no dates, no per-agent paths if you want cross-agent sharing (use excludeDynamicSections). PROJECT CONTEXT (cached per session): charter file plus the capped memory index, frozen at session start. CONVERSATION (uncached tail): per-turn retrieved memories and document snippets as tagged blocks in the user turn (current time, channel, hot memories), with the explicit breakpoint before them. Memory written mid-session is already in the transcript; the index refreshes at the next session or compaction. TTL economics (derived from the published multipliers, assumes a full cache write at each expiry): for a prefix reused N times in an hour with gaps over 5 minutes, 5-minute TTL costs about 1.25N base-input units per prefix token versus about 2 + 0.1(N-1) with the 1-hour TTL, so 1 hour wins from N of 2 or more; Discord agents with bursty, sparse traffic should set 1h; steady chat under 5-minute gaps does not need it. Ensure the stable prefix exceeds the model's minimum cacheable length. Avoid model switching and avoid cold-cache compaction (compact at natural breaks while warm). Log cache_read vs cache_creation tokens per turn and alert on hit rate, as Claude Code does. COST: nearly free to adopt; risk is silently losing cache through an innocent change (timestamp, tool order), so add a regression test that hashes the prefix across two simulated turns. Sources: [1] https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/prompt-caching, [2] https://code.claude.com/docs/en/prompt-caching.md, [3] https://claude.com/blog/lessons-from-building-claude-code-prompt-caching-is-everything (redirects to https://claude.dev/blog/lessons-from-building-claude-code-prompt-caching-is-everything/), [4] https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/agents-and-tools/tool-use/tool-use-with-prompt-caching, [5] https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/agents-and-tools/tool-use/tool-search-tool, [6] https://code.claude.com/docs/en/costs.md
- **Tool and agent design guidance: Building effective agents (2024, older), Writing tools for agents (2025), Advanced tool use and tool search**: Design rules for the tools an agent uses to read and write memory, and for keeping tool definitions from eating the stable prefix [1][2][3][4]. Memory model: BUILDING EFFECTIVE AGENTS: the building block is an augmented LLM (retrieval, tools, memory); start with the simplest solution and add complexity only when it measurably helps; patterns are prompt chaining, routing, parallelization, orchestrator-workers, evaluator-optimizer; agents need ground truth from the environment at each step, stopping conditions, and sandbox testing; frameworks can hide prompts, so call APIs directly first; tool format guidance includes poka-yoke design (for example require absolute file paths) and writing descriptions like docs for a new developer with examples, edge cases and boundaries between similar tools [1]. WRITING TOOLS FOR AGENTS: prototype, evaluate on realistic multi-call tasks and track accuracy, runtime, call count, token use and errors on a held-out set; build few high-impact tools and consolidate multi-step operations (schedule_event rather than list_users plus list_events plus create_event); namespace by service and resource; return high-signal context (names over UUIDs, a response_format enum of concise or detailed); limit tokens with pagination, filtering, truncation and sensible defaults (Claude Code caps tool responses at 25,000 tokens by default); make errors actionable; unambiguous parameter names (user_id not user); iterate by feeding eval transcripts back to Claude [2]. TOOL SEARCH (API tool_search_tool_regex_20251119 or bm25): mark tools defer_loading:true so only a search tool plus 3 to 5 core tools sit in context; use when you have 10 or more tools or over 10k tokens of definitions; selection accuracy degrades past 30 to 50 tools; up to 10,000 deferred tools, 5 results per search by default; a deferred tool cannot also carry cache_control; you can implement custom client-side search (for example embeddings) by returning tool_reference blocks [3][4]. Related: programmatic tool calling and tool-use examples [3]. Applicable to ClawCode: COPY: (1) Define a small memory and document toolset with consolidated verbs: memory_search (query, type filter, limit, cursor, response_format concise|detailed), memory_get (id or path, view_range), memory_write (create or replace with hash precondition), memory_delete, doc_search, doc_get; namespace with a shared prefix. (2) Return human-readable names, type, updated date, source and a snippet, not raw UUIDs or embeddings; default to concise and let the agent ask for detailed. (3) Hard-cap every response (around 25,000 tokens like Claude Code, much lower by default) with pagination and a 'more results available, narrow your query' hint. (4) Make errors instructive (what was wrong, what to call next). (5) Build a retrieval eval and a tool-call eval set and iterate descriptions against it. (6) If ClawCode exposes many MCP tools (Discord, OCR, admin), use tool search or deferred loading and keep the memory tools in the always-loaded 3 to 5. (7) Keep ClawCode a single-agent-per-conversation loop with sub-agents only for isolation, since multi-agent systems consume about 15x chat tokens [5]. COST: low; most value comes from eval discipline. Sources: [1] https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/building-effective-agents (2024; older, exact date not re-verified), [2] https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/writing-tools-for-agents (2025), [3] https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/advanced-tool-use (2025), [4] https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/agents-and-tools/tool-use/tool-search-tool, [5] https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/multi-agent-research-system
- **Long-running agent harness patterns: Effective harnesses (2025), Harness design (Mar 24, 2026), Managed Agents session log (Apr 8, 2026), Multi-agent research system (2025)**: Anthropic's engineering posts on how to keep agents coherent across context windows and sessions: external progress artifacts, resets versus compaction, an append-only session log separate from the context window, and external memory for plans [1][2][3][4]. Memory model: EFFECTIVE HARNESSES: agents work in discrete sessions with no memory; compaction alone was not enough; an initializer agent creates init.sh, a claude-progress.txt log and a git baseline plus a JSON feature list (200+ entries, all initially failing, edit rule: only the passes field may change; JSON chosen because models overwrite JSON less than Markdown); each later session starts by pwd, reading git log and progress file, reading the feature list, running a basic end-to-end test, then does one feature, commits and updates notes; fixes for premature 'done' claims: structured list plus verification before marking passing [1]. Claude prompting docs echo this: structured JSON for state like test status, freeform text for progress notes, git for checkpoints, and consider starting a fresh window over compaction because current models discover state from the filesystem well; be prescriptive in how the fresh session starts [5]. HARNESS DESIGN: context resets (clear window, fresh agent, structured handoff) fixed 'context anxiety' on Sonnet 4.5, but Opus 4.5 largely lost that behavior so the later harness dropped resets and used one continuous session with automatic compaction; each harness component encodes an assumption about what the model cannot do alone and should be stress-tested as models improve; agents communicated through files; a separate evaluator agent beats self-critique, and is worth its cost only when the task is beyond what the model does reliably alone [2]. MANAGED AGENTS: the session is an append-only event log that lives outside the context window, read with positional slices via getEvents; the harness transforms events (compaction, trimming, memory tool, arranging for cache hits) before they enter the window, and compacted messages stay recoverable because they remain in the log; brain, hands and session are decoupled so a crashed harness restarts with wake(sessionId) [3]. MULTI-AGENT RESEARCH: the lead saves its plan to external memory before the context passes 200,000 tokens (it would be truncated); subagents write outputs to external artifact stores and pass lightweight references back to avoid a game of telephone; agents summarize completed phases to memory and can spawn fresh subagents with clean contexts plus handoffs; resume from checkpoints after errors [4]. Applicable to ClawCode: COPY: (1) Keep an append-only event log of every Discord turn, tool call and compaction boundary in SQLite as the durable record (it also feeds dreaming), distinct from whatever the model currently sees; never do irreversible pruning of what the model saw. (2) For multi-session tasks, create a per-task progress artifact (free-text notes plus a JSON checklist whose statuses only the harness or verified steps may flip) and a documented session-start routine (read notes, read checklist, run a smoke check). (3) Use a PreCompact hook to write a structured handoff and re-inject it after compaction. (4) Prefer continuous sessions with compaction on current models; keep reset-plus-handoff as a configurable fallback and re-test the assumption on each model upgrade. (5) Pass large artifacts by reference (document id, chunk ids) between agents or sub-agents, not by pasting text. (6) Only add multi-agent or evaluator layers where an eval shows a gain, because cost climbs fast (below). COST: event log and handoff artifacts are modest; the discipline of re-testing harness assumptions per model is ongoing. Sources: [1] https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-harnesses-for-long-running-agents (2025), [2] https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/harness-design-long-running-apps (Mar 24, 2026), [3] https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/managed-agents (Apr 8, 2026), [4] https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/multi-agent-research-system (2025), [5] https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/prompt-engineering/claude-prompting-best-practices
- **Agent Skills progressive disclosure (Anthropic engineering post, 2025, plus Claude Code skills docs)**: A three-level just-in-time loading scheme for procedural knowledge: metadata always present, SKILL.md body on demand, linked files on demand [1][2]. Memory model: LEVEL 1: name and description of every installed skill are pre-loaded into the system prompt (in Claude Code the combined description text is truncated at 1,536 characters per skill; user-only skills with disable-model-invocation:true put nothing in context) [1][2]. LEVEL 2: when Claude judges a skill relevant it reads the full SKILL.md (keep under 500 lines); once loaded it stays in the conversation, so every line is a recurring token cost [2]. LEVEL 3: linked files (reference.md, examples.md) are opened only when needed; scripts can be executed without loading their source into context [1][2]. After compaction, Claude Code re-attaches the most recent invocation of each skill, keeping the first 5,000 tokens of each within a 25,000-token shared budget, so put the key instructions at the top [2][3]. Guidance: build skills from observed failures, split large bodies into referenced files, keep mutually exclusive contexts on separate paths, write the description carefully because it is the trigger, have Claude capture successful approaches and mistakes into skills, vet third-party skills [1]. Applicable to ClawCode: COPY: (1) Store procedural memory ('how to do X for this user') as skill-shaped entries (name, one-paragraph description, body, optional linked files) rather than as flat notes, with only the name and description in the always-loaded index. (2) Model the OCR/document library the same way: a catalogue of document titles and one-line descriptions always visible (bounded), bodies and page ranges fetched on demand. (3) Put the most important instructions first in any body that might be truncated or re-injected. COST: low; it is mainly a schema choice (kind=procedure) and an index rule. No measured benefit is published for skills. Sources: [1] https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/equipping-agents-for-the-real-world-with-agent-skills (2025), [2] https://code.claude.com/docs/en/skills.md, [3] https://code.claude.com/docs/en/context-window.md

#### How production agent harnesses implement long-term memory in 2025-2026 (OpenClaw, Hermes Agent, OpenAI Agents SDK and Codex, LangGraph/LangMem, CrewAI, AutoGen/Microsoft Agent Framework/Magentic, Cognee, Supermemory, Honcho), what each injects every turn versus searches on demand, when writes happen, measured results, and what ClawCode could copy

- Keep the always-injected core tiny, curated and hard-capped; push everything else to on-demand search. Over-cap writes should fail loudly so the agent must consolidate. Evidence: Hermes caps MEMORY.md at 2200 chars and USER.md at 1375 and returns an error on overflow with a 3-failures-per-turn budget; OpenClaw caps bootstrap files at 20000 chars each, 60000 total, USER.md 4000; Codex and the OpenAI sandbox inject only a short memory_summary.md and make the agent open details on demand. No source measured the optimal size. Source: https://hermes-agent.nousresearch.com/docs/user-guide/features/memory ; https://docs.openclaw.ai/concepts/agent-workspace.md ; https://openai.github.io/openai-agents-python/sandbox/memory/
- Freeze the injected memory at session start so the prompt prefix stays cacheable; mid-session writes go to disk and show up next session (or are reachable through tools). Evidence: Hermes loads MEMORY.md and USER.md once and states the reason is preserving the prefix cache; its background review fork reuses the parent's cached system prompt so reviews are cheap. Source: https://hermes-agent.nousresearch.com/docs/user-guide/features/memory ; https://github.com/NousResearch/hermes-agent/blob/main/agent/background_review.py
- Split memory writing into a cheap per-session extraction and a separate, locked, global consolidation, both off the hot path and gated on idleness. Evidence: Codex Phase 1 (idle at least 6 h, leased jobs) and Phase 2 (single global lock, git-diff input, consolidation sub-agent); OpenAI sandbox Memory Phase 1 and 2 at sandbox close; Foundry extraction, consolidation, retrieval; LangMem ReflectionExecutor debounce; Honcho deriver then dreamer; Hermes defers reviews until the machine is idle. Source: https://github.com/openai/codex/blob/main/codex-rs/memories/README.md ; https://openai.github.io/openai-agents-python/sandbox/memory/ ; https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/foundry/agents/concepts/what-is-memory ; https://langchain-ai.github.io/langmem/guides/delayed_processing/
- Gate consolidation runs on accumulated new material and idleness, not just a clock or a short idle timer. Evidence: Honcho dreams only after at least 50 new explicit conclusions, 60 minutes idle and 8 hours since the last dream; Codex waits for rollouts idle at least 6 h; Hermes curator needs 2 h idle and 7 days since last run. Source: https://github.com/plastic-labs/honcho/blob/main/src/config.py ; https://github.com/openai/codex/blob/main/codex-rs/config/src/types.rs ; https://github.com/NousResearch/hermes-agent/blob/main/agent/curator.py
- Promote to long-term memory on evidence of use (recall count across distinct queries, usage count, recency), not on an LLM's guess at importance alone. Evidence: OpenClaw deep dreaming requires minScore 0.75, at least 3 recalls and at least 3 unique queries, with a 14-day recency half-life and 30-day max age; Codex ranks consolidation inputs by usage_count then last_usage and drops memories unused for 30 days; Cognee improve reweights graph nodes by whether recalled context helped. Source: https://github.com/openclaw/openclaw/blob/main/docs/cli/memory.md ; https://github.com/openai/codex/blob/main/codex-rs/memories/README.md ; https://docs.cognee.ai/rust/operations.md
- Track provenance and taint outside the model-writable text, and never let automated, untrusted or tool-tainted sessions create promotable memory. Evidence: OpenClaw stores origin class (owner, agent, untrusted, system) and session kind in SQLite, excludes cron, heartbeat and subagent sessions from promotion, and taints any turn with network tool output; Codex can exclude chats that used MCP or web search (disable_on_external_context); Hermes scans every memory write for injection and exfiltration patterns because entries persist into every prompt; Foundry warns that LLM-extracted memory is open to injection and corruption. Source: https://docs.openclaw.ai/concepts/memory-architecture.md ; https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/customization/memories ; https://github.com/NousResearch/hermes-agent/blob/main/tools/memory_tool_store.py ; https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/foundry/agents/concepts/what-is-memory
- Let the consolidator emit validated operations (add, merge, supersede) instead of free-form rewrites, with a bounded-loss check, content-hash compare-and-swap, stored pre-image and append-only fallback. Evidence: OpenClaw accepts a rewrite only if at most 25 percent of prior entries are lost, every promoted candidate's source is cited, the size budget holds and the output parses; it replaces MEMORY.md by hash check then atomic rename and stores the pre-image; Codex keeps the memory directory as a git baseline and gives the consolidator the diff. Source: https://docs.openclaw.ai/concepts/dreaming.md ; https://docs.openclaw.ai/concepts/memory-architecture.md ; https://github.com/openai/codex/blob/main/codex-rs/memories/README.md
- Forget by archiving, windows and supersession rather than deleting: recency decay at retrieval, unused-for-N-days windows, newest-N caps, and links that mark older facts superseded. Evidence: Hermes curator archives (never deletes) skills stale after 14 days and archived after 30; Codex max_unused_days 30 and 256 raw-memory cap; OpenAI sandbox keeps only the newest 256 conversations for consolidation; Supermemory keeps old facts linked by updates, extends, derives; OpenClaw supersession keys; CrewAI and OpenClaw both use a 30-day decay half-life. Source: https://github.com/NousResearch/hermes-agent/blob/main/agent/curator.py ; https://github.com/openai/codex/blob/main/codex-rs/config/src/types.rs ; https://supermemory.ai/research/longmembench ; https://docs.crewai.com/en/concepts/memory
- Model time explicitly (when written versus when it happened) and support knowledge updates, because temporal and update questions are where memory systems fail. Evidence: LongMemEval found time-aware indexing and query expansion improved temporal recall 6.8 to 11.3 percent; Supermemory stores documentDate and eventDate; the Mem0 paper attributes OpenAI memory's 21.71 temporal score to memories lacking timestamps; Honcho's author says temporal reasoning is weak across all models. Source: https://arxiv.org/html/2410.10813v2 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2504.19413 ; https://honcho.dev/blog/benchmarking-honcho
- Separate stores by what they hold (who the user is, environment facts, procedures, episodes) and route each fact to exactly one store. Evidence: Hermes review prompt routes each fact to USER.md or MEMORY.md and forbids writing it to both because it bloats both to their caps; Hermes keeps procedures in skills and outcomes in session_search; LangMem and Foundry define semantic, episodic and procedural types; OpenClaw tiers keep instructions, curated core, episodic, prospective and review apart. Source: https://github.com/NousResearch/hermes-agent/blob/main/agent/background_review.py ; https://langchain-ai.github.io/langmem/concepts/conceptual_guide/ ; https://docs.openclaw.ai/concepts/memory-architecture.md
- Run a cheap, deterministic recall lane every turn and reserve expensive LLM recall for turns that show recall intent; give the agent plain search and open tools for deeper retrieval. Evidence: OpenClaw lane 1 has no model calls and lane 2 blocking sub-agent runs only on recall intent with a 15 s default timeout; CrewAI shallow recall needs no LLM and deep recall skips analysis for queries under 200 chars; Letta's grep-and-open agent scored 74.0 percent on LoCoMo versus 68.5 for Mem0g. Source: https://docs.openclaw.ai/concepts/memory-architecture.md ; https://docs.crewai.com/en/concepts/memory ; https://www.letta.com/blog/benchmarking-ai-agent-memory
- Always run a pre-compaction memory flush as a safety net, append-only to a single dated file, with a no-op reply when nothing is worth saving. Evidence: OpenClaw flushes when within 4000 tokens of compaction or at 2 MiB transcript, appends only to memory/YYYY-MM-DD.md, forbids timestamped variant files and treats MEMORY.md as read-only during the flush; failure never blocks compaction. Source: https://github.com/openclaw/openclaw/blob/main/extensions/memory-core/src/flush-plan.ts ; https://docs.openclaw.ai/concepts/memory.md
- Treat memory as advisory context, not ground truth: verify changeable claims against the live source and let the current user message override. Evidence: Codex read-path template says memory is not proof of current behavior and not to retrieve history speculatively; the OpenAI cookbook ranks latest user message above session notes above global notes; the sandbox docs say agents trust the current environment over memory. Source: https://github.com/openai/codex/blob/main/codex-rs/ext/memories/templates/memories/read_path_v2.md ; https://developers.openai.com/cookbook/examples/agents_sdk/context_personalization ; https://openai.github.io/openai-agents-python/sandbox/memory/
- Route explicit remember, forget and correct requests through a visible staging path that consolidation applies, and make memory use auditable. Evidence: Codex appends ad-hoc notes for explicit remember, forget or corrections and requires citation blocks for memory actually used; Foundry offers direct remember-or-forget commands and item-level CRUD; Hermes can stage writes for /memory approve. Source: https://github.com/openai/codex/blob/main/codex-rs/ext/memories/templates/memories/read_path_v2.md ; https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/foundry/agents/concepts/what-is-memory ; https://hermes-agent.nousresearch.com/docs/user-guide/features/memory
- Do not trust vendor benchmark headlines; memory beats full context on cost and latency, rarely on accuracy, and benchmarks are saturating. Evidence: In the Mem0 paper full context scored 72.90 on LoCoMo versus 66.88 for Mem0 while memory cut p95 latency 91 percent; Honcho reports Gemini 3 Pro alone gets 92 percent on LongMemEval S versus its own 90.4 to 92.6; Supermemory's 97 percent depends on gpt-4o (84.6 with gpt-5) and has an ambiguous metric label; Zep's LoCoMo claim conflicts across vendor tables. Source: https://arxiv.org/html/2504.19413 ; https://honcho.dev/blog/benchmarking-honcho ; https://supermemory.ai/research/longmembench ; https://mem0.ai/blog/ai-memory-benchmarks-in-2026

Anti-patterns: Timestamped flush files outside the consolidation glob: OpenClaw's flush prompt explicitly says do NOT create YYYY-MM-DD-HHMM.md variants and to append to the daily file, while ClawCode writes memory/YYYY-MM-DD-HHMM.md every 15 minutes that its weekly digest pattern never matches (https://github.com/openclaw/openclaw/blob/main/extensions/memory-core/src/flush-plan.ts, src/memory/memory-flush.ts in ClawCode).; Writing the same fact into both the user-profile store and the environment store: Hermes documents that this bloats both to their caps and crowds out useful facts (https://github.com/NousResearch/hermes-agent/blob/main/agent/background_review.py).; Persisting negative claims about tools or environment failures as durable rules ('browser tools do not work'); they harden into refusals the agent cites against itself long after the fix. Hermes bans these and unresolved failures dressed as workflows in its review prompt (same source).; Scheduled or automated sessions dominating retrieval or promotion: Hermes saw 'recall blindness' because cron vocabulary swamped BM25 and now hides subagent sessions and demotes cron sessions; OpenClaw excludes cron, heartbeat and subagent sessions from promotion (https://github.com/NousResearch/hermes-agent/blob/main/tools/session_search_tool.py, https://docs.openclaw.ai/concepts/memory-architecture.md).; Letting tool, web, MCP or OCR-derived content become durable memory unscreened: OpenClaw taints such turns, Codex can exclude them, Hermes scans writes for injection and exfiltration patterns, Foundry warns of memory corruption and prompt injection (https://docs.openclaw.ai/concepts/memory-architecture.md, https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/customization/memories, https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/foundry/agents/concepts/what-is-memory).; LLM rewrites of the whole Tier 1 file with no loss guard, no pre-image and no compare-and-swap: OpenClaw requires at most 25 percent entry loss, source citations, a size budget and a content-hash check before atomic rename; ClawCode's shared ~/Knowledgebase/MEMORY.md across four agents with no lock invites lost updates (https://docs.openclaw.ai/concepts/dreaming.md).; Overwriting a near-duplicate row at a cosine threshold without checking for contradiction (ClawCode replaces content at 0.85 and keeps max importance): CrewAI sends hits at or above 0.85 to an LLM that chooses keep, update, delete or insert_new, and Supermemory links updates rather than erasing history. This is my inference from those designs, not a measured failure (https://docs.crewai.com/en/concepts/memory, https://supermemory.ai/research/longmembench).; Counting retrieval as use: retrieved is not used. Codex asks the agent to cite memory actually used and ranks by usage_count; OpenClaw promotes only after multiple distinct queries recalled an entry. Inference for ClawCode's access-count hot tier (https://github.com/openai/codex/blob/main/codex-rs/memories/README.md, https://github.com/openclaw/openclaw/blob/main/docs/cli/memory.md).; Running background extraction on every message or with unbounded LLM-in-the-loop search: LangMem's guide calls per-message processing expensive and the Mem0 paper measured LangMem search p95 at 59.82 s (https://langchain-ai.github.io/langmem/background_quickstart/, https://arxiv.org/html/2504.19413).; A blocking recall sub-agent on every turn: OpenClaw gates it on recall intent, direct chats only, with a 15 s timeout and a 220-char summary cap (https://docs.openclaw.ai/concepts/active-memory/configuration.md).; Mutating the injected prefix mid-session: Hermes freezes its snapshot to preserve prompt caching; edits made during a session take effect next session (https://hermes-agent.nousresearch.com/docs/user-guide/features/memory).; A skill or procedure library that hoards one-off incident narratives (PR numbers, dates, quotes, one reference file per session): Hermes's review prompt names this as the failure shape and requires generalizable rules plus one clause of why (https://github.com/NousResearch/hermes-agent/blob/main/agent/background_review.py).; Relying on saturated synthetic or vendor benchmarks to judge memory quality: the in-repo ClawCode eval (11 and 15 synthetic queries, recall@5 = 1.0) cannot discriminate; Honcho's author says LongMemEval and LoCoMo are saturating and some temporal items are wrong (https://honcho.dev/blog/benchmarking-honcho).; Silent truncation of the injected core: OpenClaw keeps the disk file intact, truncates only the injected copy and gives /context list and openclaw doctor to see raw versus injected size; ClawCode's 70/20 head-tail truncation marker is in the same spirit but agents should be warned before the cap is hit (https://docs.openclaw.ai/concepts/memory.md).

Systems reviewed:

- **OpenClaw (formerly Clawdbot and Moltbot; ClawCode's ancestor)**: Open-source (MIT) self-hosted personal agent gateway with Discord, Slack, Telegram, WhatsApp and other channels. Name history: Warelay (2025-11-24), CLAWDIS (2025-12-03), Clawdbot (2026-01-02), Moltbot (2026-01-27, after an Anthropic trademark complaint), OpenClaw (2026-01-30); the creator joined OpenAI on 2026-02-14 and a nonprofit OpenClaw Foundation stewards the project (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OpenClaw). GitHub showed about 393k stars when fetched (https://github.com/openclaw/openclaw). Core memory stance in the official docs: the model only remembers what is saved to disk, as plain Markdown in the agent workspace plus one SQLite index, with no hidden state (https://docs.openclaw.ai/concepts/memory.md). The default plugin is memory-core; Honcho and LanceDB are alternative memory plugins, and a separate memory-wiki plugin compiles notes into a wiki vault (same page). A QMD sidecar backend (BM25 + vectors + learned reranking) existed and has been removed; builtin is now the only engine (https://docs.openclaw.ai/concepts/memory-qmd.md). Memory model: TIERS (https://docs.openclaw.ai/concepts/memory-architecture.md): instructions (AGENTS.md, SOUL.md, human-written only), curated core (MEMORY.md for compact durable non-profile facts and decisions, optional USER.md for preferences written as directives with observed-date and active/superseded metadata), episodic (memory/YYYY-MM-DD.md daily notes and transcripts, recall only), prospective (event-conditioned standing intents in SQLite plus cron), review (DREAMS.md diary for humans, never injected). INJECTED: AGENTS.md, SOUL.md and USER.md at session start; MEMORY.md only in the main private session, not groups; caps agents.defaults.bootstrapMaxChars 20000, bootstrapTotalMaxChars 60000, USER.md 4000 chars, injected copy truncated while the disk file stays intact (https://docs.openclaw.ai/concepts/agent-workspace.md). The architecture page says eligible core files refresh each turn within budget so consolidation shows up without a restart. Today and yesterday daily notes load only on a bare /new or /reset (https://docs.openclaw.ai/concepts/memory.md). Trigger injection (lane 1, no model call): entries in MEMORY.md and USER.md carry trailing comments like <!-- trigger: ... --> and <!-- importance: 9 -->; each inbound message is prefiltered lexically and by vector similarity and matches scoring at least 0.65 are injected as a hidden context block, max 3 per turn; daily notes and transcripts never auto-inject. Standing intents inject only when a rule-based trigger fires (defaults 24 h cooldown, 3-fire budget, 90-day expiry, max 3 per turn). ON DEMAND: memory_search (hybrid, can also return transcript hits), memory_get (file or line range), sessions_search then sessions_history, an intent tool. Lane 2 is the Active Memory plugin: a blocking recall sub-agent that in default 'escalate' mode runs only when the message shows recall intent and lane 1 found no strong hit; defaults timeoutMs 15000, maxSummaryChars 220, cacheTtlMs 15000, allowedChatTypes direct only; query modes message, recent, full share a 4000-char context budget (https://docs.openclaw.ai/concepts/active-memory/configuration.md, https://docs.openclaw.ai/concepts/active-memory/tuning.md). SEARCH BACKEND (https://docs.openclaw.ai/concepts/memory-builtin.md, https://docs.openclaw.ai/reference/memory-config.md): per-agent SQLite with FTS5 BM25 (trigram for CJK) plus sqlite-vec (software cosine fallback if the extension will not load), chunks of 400 tokens with 80 overlap and headings attached, default embedding OpenAI text-embedding-3-small or a local embeddinggemma-300m GGUF of about 0.3 GB, BM25-only when no provider; maxResults 6, minScore 0.35; ranking is hybrid relevance x recency decay (30-day half-life, applied only to dated YYYY-MM-DD files; MEMORY.md, USER.md and undated files are evergreen) x importance multiplier (1 to 10, null neutral) then MMR (lambda 0.7, Jaccard on snippet tokens, on by default); 200 candidates per leg per the docs, while source file src/agents/memory-search.ts still shows vectorWeight 0.7, textWeight 0.3, candidateMultiplier 4 (may be stale, https://github.com/openclaw/openclaw/blob/main/src/agents/memory-search.ts); file watcher reindexes after a 1.5 s debounce; transcript indexing is opt-in (experimental.sessionMemory, sources: [memory, sessions]); if semantic search hits the 30 s tool deadline it returns keyword hits with a partial-result warning. Provenance lives in SQLite, not Markdown: origin class owner/agent/untrusted/system (unclassifiable is never defaulted to owner), session kind (interactive/cron/heartbeat/subagent), observed time, supersession key; cron, heartbeat and subagent sessions produce no promotion-eligible memory; any turn containing network-sourced tool output is tainted untrusted until the next user message; project scope boosts entries matching up to 4 active git-remote keys. WRITES: (a) hot path by the agent editing Markdown per AGENTS.md; (b) pre-compaction memory flush, on by default: a silent turn when within softThresholdTokens (default 4000) of compaction or the transcript passes 2 MiB (https://github.com/openclaw/openclaw/blob/main/extensions/memory-core/src/flush-plan.ts); its prompt says store durable memories only in memory/YYYY-MM-DD.md, append only, treat MEMORY.md, DREAMS.md, SOUL.md, AGENTS.md as read-only, do NOT create timestamped variant files such as YYYY-MM-DD-HHMM.md, reply NO_REPLY if nothing; replayed whole notes are rejected; failure never blocks compaction (https://docs.openclaw.ai/concepts/memory.md); (c) background dreaming, enabled by default per https://docs.openclaw.ai/concepts/dreaming.md (one third-party mirror page said opt-in; the official config reference lists enabled: true), cron '0 3 * * *'.
