Three phases: light (dedupe, stage candidates, record reinforcement), REM (theme and reflection summaries), deep (the only phase that writes MEMORY.md). Deep ranks candidates from a short-term recall store using six weighted signals (relevance 0.30, frequency 0.24, query diversity 0.15, recency 0.15, consolidation 0.10, conceptual richness 0.06) and requires all gates: minScore 0.75, minRecallCount 3, minUniqueQueries 3, recencyHalfLifeDays 14, maxAgeDays 30 (https://github.com/openclaw/openclaw/blob/main/docs/cli/memory.md). A model turn then returns operations (add, merge, supersede), not prose; untrusted/system candidates are removed before the prompt; the rewrite is accepted only if it keeps at least 75 percent of prior entries (maxPriorEntryLossFraction 0.25), cites every promoted candidate's source, fits the bootstrap budget and parses; otherwise it falls back to append-only; promoted snippets are capped at 160 tokens and tagged with up to 3 concept tags and importance 1 to 10; MEMORY.md is replaced via content-hash check then atomic rename, the pre-image is stored in SQLite, and a summary goes to DREAMS.md. Backfill commands (rem-backfill, session-backfill) stage historical candidates reversibly. FORGETTING: no hard deletes; retrieval-time decay, a 30-day age gate on promotion, supersession keys, and the MEMORY.md budget forcing consolidation. Applicable to ClawCode: ClawCode already mirrors OpenClaw's Tier 1 caps (16000 and 4000 chars versus 20000 and 4000), FTS5 plus sqlite-vec hybrid, 30-day decay and a dream pass, so the gains are in the details. (1) Promote on evidence of use, not LLM score alone: add a recall_events row per retrieval hit (chunk id, query hash, time) and let the dream pass only consider chunks recalled at least 3 times from at least 3 distinct queries inside 30 days; cost is one small table and a query, no extra LLM calls, and it shrinks the 32K dream prompt. (2) Make dream output operations (add, merge, supersede with source refs) validated by a loss-fraction guard (25 percent), source-ref check and size budget, with append-only fallback, a stored pre-image and a content-hash compare-and-swap before atomic rename; this closes the known gap that edit and merge candidates are downgraded to appends and removes the no-lock risk of four agents writing ~/Knowledgebase/MEMORY.md; cost is moderate (schema, validator, pre-image table). (3) Provenance and taint: add origin class (owner, agent, untrusted, system) and session kind to every memory row and exclude cron, heartbeat, subagent and OCR-ingested or web-tainted turns from promotion; ClawCode has schedules, Discord users and document ingestion so this is the highest security value; cost is a migration plus plumbing in the flush, summarizer and dream builder. (4) Flush hygiene: OpenClaw forbids timestamped flush files because they break its daily-note handling; ClawCode writes memory/YYYY-MM-DD-HHMM.md every 15 minutes (src/memory/memory-flush.ts) and the consolidation glob only matches YYYY-MM-DD.md so those files are never digested. Either widen the glob to YYYY-MM-DD-*.md or append to the daily file; and add a token-proximity trigger (flush when context fill is within about 4000 tokens of compaction) which also closes the missing memory-pressure warning gap; cost is small. (5) Trigger-phrase injection for Tier 1 entries (threshold 0.65, max 3 per turn) is optional and cheap if retrieval already embeds Tier 1 entries. (6) Add MMR (lambda 0.7, Jaccard) after RRF to avoid five near-duplicate chunks; trivial cost. (7) Index extra roots such as ~/Knowledgebase with an evergreen flag that disables decay, as OpenClaw's extraPaths does. (8) Do not copy the blocking Active Memory sub-agent for every turn: its 15 s default timeout shows the latency cost; if used, gate it on recall-intent phrases and Discord direct chats only. Sources: https://docs.openclaw.ai/concepts/memory.md, https://docs.openclaw.ai/concepts/memory-architecture.md, https://docs.openclaw.ai/concepts/dreaming.md, https://docs.openclaw.ai/concepts/memory-builtin.md, https://docs.openclaw.ai/concepts/memory-search.md, https://docs.openclaw.ai/reference/memory-config.md
- **Hermes Agent (Nous Research)**: MIT-licensed self-improving agent with CLI plus Telegram, Discord, Slack, WhatsApp and Signal front ends; about 257k GitHub stars when fetched; markets a 'built-in learning loop' of curated memory, auto-created skills, FTS5 session search, Honcho user modeling and periodic nudges (https://github.com/NousResearch/hermes-agent). Memory is deliberately tiny and bounded; everything else is pushed to searchable session history, skills and optional external memory providers (https://hermes-agent.nousresearch.com/docs/user-guide/features/memory). Memory model: STORES: MEMORY.md (agent notes about the environment: tool quirks, conventions, paths) capped at 2200 chars (about 800 tokens) and USER.md (who the user is) capped at 1375 chars (about 500 tokens), in ~/.hermes/memories/, entries delimited by the section sign, per profile; a write that would exceed the cap returns an error so the agent must consolidate rather than silently dropping (official docs). Source: tools/memory_tool_store.py allows 3 failed consolidation attempts per turn then returns a terminal 'save skipped', refuses to rewrite a file that does not round-trip (drift guard with .bak snapshot), and scans every write for injection and exfiltration patterns because a poisoned entry persists into every future system prompt (https://github.com/NousResearch/hermes-agent/blob/main/tools/memory_tool_store.py). Optional memory.write_approval stages writes for /memory approve. INJECTED: MEMORY.md and USER.md as a frozen snapshot at session start; mid-session writes hit disk immediately but never change the prompt, to keep the prefix cache intact, and appear in the next session; context files (AGENTS.md, CLAUDE.md, SOUL.md and others) are auto-loaded; external provider recall (Honcho, Hindsight, Supermemory, Mem0, RetainDB, ByteRover, OpenViking, Holographic) is prefetched and returned in full up to a ceiling of 10x hooks.output_spill.max_chars (at least 100000 chars), with an optional head/tail spill. ON DEMAND: session_search over ~/.hermes/state.db (SQLite FTS5); per the official docs and source it makes no LLM calls and returns real DB messages in four shapes (discovery by query deduped by lineage, scroll around a message id, read a session head/tail, browse); subagent, tool and kanban sessions are hidden and cron sessions are searchable but demoted because their repetitive vocabulary dominated BM25 and caused 'recall blindness' (issue 19434 cited in https://github.com/NousResearch/hermes-agent/blob/main/tools/session_search_tool.py); per-message content is capped at 2000 chars on read. (Older third-party write-ups and the repo README say search results are LLM-summarized; the current docs and source say no.) Skills load progressively and do not count against the 2200-char budget; skill_manage supports create, patch, edit, delete, write_file, remove_file. WRITES: (a) hot path, the memory tool with add, patch, remove and a target of memory or user (the old replace action is gone); (b) nudges: memory.nudge_interval defaults to 10 user turns and skills.creation_nudge_interval to 15 tool iterations (https://github.com/NousResearch/hermes-agent/blob/main/cli-config.yaml.example, agent/agent_init.py); (c) background review: after a turn a daemon thread forks the agent, replays the conversation snapshot, inherits the parent's runtime and cached system prompt so it hits the same prefix cache, runs under a tool whitelist with its own nudges set to 0, and asks 'should any skill or memory be saved or updated'; its prompt forces each fact into exactly one store (USER.md for who the user is, MEMORY.md for environment facts), allows 'Nothing to save.', and a do-not-capture block bans environment-dependent failures, negative claims about tools ('browser tools do not work'), transient errors, one-off task narratives and unresolved failures dressed as workflows (https://github.com/NousResearch/hermes-agent/blob/main/agent/background_review.py). Config: auxiliary.background_review.model (default auto, a different model gets a compact digest instead of the replay), max_input_tokens (75 percent of the review model window, capped at 600000), defer: auto waits for idle on a local llama-server (defer_max_age_s 1800), enabled: false disables it; manual /refine still works. FORGETTING/CONSOLIDATION: memory is forgotten by being rewritten under the cap; skills are maintained by the Curator, which is inactivity-triggered (no cron), runs every 7 days when idle at least 2 hours, marks skills stale after 14 days and archives after 30, never deletes (archive only, recoverable), skips pinned skills, and its LLM consolidation fork is opt-in and off by default (https://github.com/NousResearch/hermes-agent/blob/main/agent/curator.py). Applicable to ClawCode: (1) Frozen Tier 1 snapshot: ClawCode already injects Tier 1 into a cached stable prefix; confirm that clawcode_memory_edit and dream appends do not rewrite the prefix mid-session (apply on next session or next idle rebuild); cost is a check, possibly a small change. (2) Size-cap errors as a consolidation forcing function plus a per-turn failure budget and a round-trip drift guard with .bak snapshot; ClawCode's 16000-char MEMORY.md is 7x Hermes's cap, and no source shows the optimal size, so measure token cost and answer quality at 4000, 8000 and 16000 before shrinking. (3) Background review fork after every N user turns (10) using the live prefix cache: ClawCode has fork.ts and a 15-minute Haiku flush that summarizes deltas; a fork that decides what to save with the one-store routing prompt and a 'Nothing to save.' exit would produce fewer, better entries than a periodic summary; cost is one extra cached call per 10 turns plus a tool whitelist. Copy the do-not-capture list verbatim in spirit, especially 'no negative claims about tools' (ClawCode's heartbeat and MCP flakiness would otherwise harden into permanent refusals). (4) Memory-write injection scan for strict patterns before any write to Tier 1; cheap and directly relevant since agents ingest OCR documents and Discord text. (5) Session search ranking: hide subagent sessions and demote cron and heartbeat sessions in src/memory/conversation-search.ts so scheduled-task vocabulary does not starve user conversations; trivial cost, real recall win. (6) Curator pattern (idle-triggered, archive never delete, pinned exemption, recoverable) fits ClawCode's procedures and skills folders better than cron. Sources: https://hermes-agent.nousresearch.com/docs/user-guide/features/memory, https://hermes-agent.nousresearch.com/docs/user-guide/features/overview, https://github.com/NousResearch/hermes-agent, https://github.com/NousResearch/hermes-agent/blob/main/agent/background_review.py, https://github.com/NousResearch/hermes-agent/blob/main/agent/curator.py, https://github.com/NousResearch/hermes-agent/blob/main/tools/memory_tool_store.py
- **OpenAI Agents SDK (Sessions, cookbook long-term notes, sandbox Memory)**: Three layers. (1) Sessions: client-side conversation history that the Runner loads before each run and appends to after it (https://openai.github.io/openai-agents-python/sessions/). (2) A cookbook pattern for long-term memory as a state object (https://developers.openai.com/cookbook/examples/agents_sdk/context_personalization, dated around Jan 2026). (3) A Memory capability for sandbox agents added with the April 2026 SDK update (announced 2026-04-15 per https://thenewstack.io/openai-agents-sdk-sandboxes/; the official blog returned HTTP 403 to my fetch), documented at https://openai.github.io/openai-agents-python/sandbox/memory/. openai-agents on PyPI was at 0.24.0 on 2026-10-15. Memory model: SESSIONS: SQLiteSession (in-memory DB if no path), AsyncSQLiteSession, RedisSession, SQLAlchemySession, DaprSession, MongoDBSession, OpenAIConversationsSession, AdvancedSQLiteSession (usage tracking and branch from turn n), EncryptedSession wrapper; SessionSettings(limit=N) keeps only the N most recent items; session_input_callback(history, new_input) lets the app prune or reorder; OpenAIResponsesCompactionSession wraps a session and auto-compacts via responses.compact after turns when should_trigger_compaction returns true (default threshold not stated), in 'auto', 'previous_response_id' or 'input' mode; the docs state sessions are conversation history only and document no semantic or cross-session long-term memory. COOKBOOK LONG-TERM NOTES: state has a structured profile, global_memory.notes (durable), session_memory.notes (staging) and trip_history; each note has text, a date and 1 to 3 keywords. Hot path: a save_memory_note tool appends to session notes only for durable, actionable, explicitly stated facts (no speculation, no agent instructions, no sensitive PII). Background: when the app decides the session is over, an LLM merges session notes into global notes, dedupes, resolves conflicts and drops session-only notes; the cookbook calls this 'the most sensitive and error-prone stage'. INJECTED every turn: profile as YAML frontmatter, the top 6 global notes sorted newest first, and a memory-policy block; session notes are injected once, on the turn after trimming flags a trim, then the flag resets. Precedence: latest user message, then session over global, then most recent note wins; memory is a default, not a hard constraint, and a conflict triggers one clarifying question; safety checks apply at capture, consolidation and injection; TTLs and confidence scores are suggested but not implemented. SANDBOX MEMORY: Memory() capability (read needs Shell(); live update needs Filesystem()); modes read+generate (default), read-only Memory(generate=None), generate-only Memory(read=None). Read: at run start memory_summary.md (general tips, user preferences, an overview of memories) is added to the developer prompt; the agent then greps MEMORY.md (a searchable index) for task keywords and opens rollout_summaries/ files only when more detail is needed; agents treat memory as guidance and trust the current environment, and may correct MEMORY.md during the run. Generate: after each run the segment is appended to sessions/<rollout-id>.jsonl; when the sandbox session closes, Phase 1 (extraction) has a model summarize each conversation into raw memory notes (system, developer and reasoning content omitted; long conversations truncated keeping beginning and end) and Phase 2 (consolidation) has an agent read the raw memories for one layout and write patterns to MEMORY.md and memory_summary.md. Forgetting: MemoryGenerateConfig.max_raw_memories_for_consolidation defaults to 256 and Phase 2 keeps only the newest conversations by last update when exceeded, 'which lets memory forget stale information'. Isolation is by MemoryLayoutConfig (memories_dir, sessions_dir), not by agent name; the conversation id resolves from conversation_id, then session.session_id, then RunConfig.group_id, then a generated id. Memory persists only if the same live sandbox or its snapshot is resumed. Applicable to ClawCode: (1) Two-phase design validated by OpenAI twice (cookbook and sandbox Memory): cheap per-session extraction into raw notes, then a separate consolidation agent that rewrites an index and a short always-injected summary. ClawCode's weekly and monthly digests plus dream pass are the same shape; the missing piece is a distinct always-injected memory_summary (a 1 to 2K token digest of preferences and routing hints) separate from the 16K MEMORY.md, which could cut the Tier 1 token bill; cost: one generated file per agent. (2) Progressive disclosure: inject only a summary and an index, make the agent grep the index then open per-session summaries on need; ClawCode's lazy clawcode_memory_search and recall tools already do this, so keep full snippets out of the prefix. (3) Session-over scoping of notes (session notes staged, promoted to global only at consolidation) maps to ClawCode's session summaries; adopt the cookbook's three-level precedence text in agent souls. (4) Recency-bounded forgetting (newest 256 raw memories feed consolidation) is a simple cap that avoids unbounded dream prompts. (5) Layout isolation is useful because four Recology agents currently share one vault index path. Sources: https://openai.github.io/openai-agents-python/sessions/, https://openai.github.io/openai-agents-python/sandbox/memory/, https://developers.openai.com/cookbook/examples/agents_sdk/context_personalization, https://thenewstack.io/openai-agents-sdk-sandboxes/, https://openai.com/index/the-next-evolution-of-the-agents-sdk/, https://pypi.org/project/openai-agents/
- **OpenAI Codex memories (open-source reference pipeline that the SDK sandbox Memory mirrors)**: Off-by-default feature of the Codex CLI and app that turns idle past chats into local memory files under ~/.codex/memories and injects them into future sessions (https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/customization/memories, redirected from developers.openai.com/codex/memories). The pipeline is open source in codex-rs/memories (https://github.com/openai/codex/blob/main/codex-rs/memories/README.md), which makes it the most detailed public reference for a harness-level extraction then consolidation design. Memory model: WHEN: triggered asynchronously when a root session starts, only if the session is not ephemeral, not a sub-agent, memory is enabled and the state DB exists. PHASE 1 (per rollout): claims a bounded set of rollouts from a state DB that are interactive, inside an age window and idle long enough (min_rollout_idle_hours default 6, https://github.com/openai/codex/blob/main/codex-rs/config/src/types.rs), leased so workers do not duplicate, runs a model on each in parallel with a concurrency cap, expects structured output (detailed raw_memory, compact rollout_summary, optional slug), redacts secrets, and records outcomes succeeded, succeeded_no_output or failed with retry backoff. PHASE 2 (global): takes a single global lock, selects stage-1 outputs, ignoring any whose last_usage is outside max_unused_days (default 30), falling back to generated_at for never-used ones, ranked by usage_count then recency, capped at max_raw_memories_for_consolidation (default 256, range 1 to 4096); syncs raw_memories.md and rollout_summaries/ into a memory directory that is itself a git repo baseline, writes phase2_workspace_diff.md, and only if the diff is non-empty spawns an internal consolidation sub-agent (no approvals, no network, local write only, no delegation) that updates MEMORY.md, memory_summary.md and skills/; the baseline resets only after success. INJECTED: memory_summary.md into the developer prompt (read_path_v2 template). ON DEMAND: the agent searches MEMORY.md and opens rollout_summaries only when additional evidence, chronology or uncertainty could change the answer; 'do not retrieve history speculatively'. The template says memory is not proof of current behavior, to verify consequential or changeable claims, never to edit generated memory files directly, and to cite memory actually used in an <oai-mem-citation> block at the end of the reply; explicit remember, forget or correction requests are appended as small Markdown notes under extensions/ad_hoc/notes/ and applied by consolidation. EXCLUSIONS: active or short-lived sessions; chats that used MCP tools, web search or tool search when memories.disable_on_external_context is true; background pass skipped when remaining rate limit is below memories.min_rate_limit_remaining_percent; per-chat /memories toggle; separate extract_model and consolidation_model overrides. Applicable to ClawCode: This is the closest published analogue to ClawCode's flush plus consolidation plus dream pipeline, and it suggests five concrete changes. (1) Usage-based selection: track usage_count and last_usage per memory (cited, not merely retrieved) and let only recently used memories feed consolidation and Tier 1; ClawCode's hot-tier rule (3 accesses in 7 days) is a crude version. Cost: ask the agent to emit a hidden citation tag for memory chunks it used, strip it before Discord delivery, increment usage on parse. (2) Git baseline for the memory directory: commit after each dream or consolidation and hand the consolidation step the diff since last success; gives audit, rollback and the 30-minute veto a durable artifact; cost is low (git init plus commit in dream-promote). (3) Single global lock and lease with watermark for consolidation across agents, which fits the shared ~/Knowledgebase situation; cost is low. (4) Explicit remember or forget requests become staged ad-hoc notes consolidated later rather than immediate writes into Tier 1; ClawCode's memory-cue regex currently writes a dated file immediately, which is fine, but corrections to existing memories (forget) have no path. (5) Exclude sessions with external context (MCP, web, OCR documents) from generation unless explicitly allowed, the cheapest anti-poisoning control. Cost of all five is moderate; none needs new infrastructure. Sources: https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/customization/memories, https://github.com/openai/codex/blob/main/codex-rs/memories/README.md, https://github.com/openai/codex/blob/main/codex-rs/ext/memories/templates/memories/read_path_v2.md, https://github.com/openai/codex/blob/main/codex-rs/config/src/types.rs
- **LangGraph and LangMem (LangChain)**: LangGraph provides thread-scoped short-term memory (state plus checkpointer) and a cross-thread long-term BaseStore of JSON documents addressed by namespace and key with semantic search; LangMem is the memory-management SDK on top: memory managers that extract, update and consolidate memories, tools the agent can call, a debounced background executor, and prompt optimizers (https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/concepts/memory, https://langchain-ai.github.io/langmem/concepts/conceptual_guide/). LangMem is pre-1.0: latest PyPI release 0.0.30 on 2025-10-27 and about 1.7k GitHub stars, with only dependency bumps since (https://pypi.org/project/langmem/, https://github.com/langchain-ai/langmem), so treat it as a design reference rather than an active dependency. Memory model: TYPES: semantic (facts; either a profile, one JSON document updated in place, good for quick lookups and user-editable data but error-prone as it grows, or a collection of narrow documents, which the docs say leads to higher recall but needs reconciliation of new with old), episodic (past experiences stored as few-shot examples with situation, reasoning and why it worked; the hard part is selecting relevant examples), procedural (the agent's own instructions, refined by reflection or metaprompting, with an optimizer that rewrites system prompts from trajectories and feedback; I verified only kind='gradient' with max_reflection_steps, other kinds were not on the fetched page). WRITES: hot path (the agent calls create_manage_memory_tool during the conversation; immediate and visible to the user but adds latency and splits the agent's attention; ChatGPT's save_memories is the cited example) versus background (create_memory_store_manager runs after the response or after inactivity; no user-facing latency, higher recall, but you must choose when to trigger: a delay that is rescheduled on new events, a cron, or manual). ReflectionExecutor debounces per thread: when new messages arrive before the delay expires it cancels the pending task and reschedules with the new messages; executor.submit(..., after_seconds=...) with the guide noting 30 to 60 minutes as typical for real apps and warning that local threads die between serverless invocations (https://langchain-ai.github.io/langmem/guides/delayed_processing/). Managers take Pydantic schemas (for example a Triple with subject, predicate, object and a context field that keeps memories interpretable), free-text instructions, enable_inserts and enable_deletes; updates pass prior memories as 'existing' and the LLM returns new items plus RemoveDoc objects for stale ones; the functional API leaves the meaning of removal (hard, soft, down-weight) to the caller (https://langchain-ai.github.io/langmem/guides/extract_semantic_memories/). The guide says collection recall should weigh similarity, importance and strength (recency and frequency) rather than similarity alone. INJECTED every turn: nothing by default; the developer decides (typically a profile rendered into the system prompt). ON DEMAND: store.search(namespace, filter, query) via create_search_memory_tool. Namespaces are hierarchical tuples with runtime templates like ('chat', '{user_id}', 'triples'). FORGETTING: only through manager deletes or the caller's TTL; LangMem implements none by itself. Applicable to ClawCode: (1) Debounce background extraction per session with a rescheduled delay (cancel and resubmit on new messages) instead of a fixed 15-minute flush timer; ClawCode's MemoryFlushTimer already skips idle windows, so the gain is avoiding summarizing a still-active thread twice; cost is small. (2) Profile versus collection split matches ClawCode's USER.md (profile) versus memories table (collection); adopt the advice to keep the profile small and split it if it grows. (3) Typed schemas with a mandatory 'context' field for extracted facts is a cheap way to make memories interpretable months later. (4) Episodic memory as few-shot examples is the one type ClawCode lacks; candidate: store a successful tool-use trajectory summary per task type and retrieve by task similarity (Magentic-UI does this with saved plans). Cost is moderate and the benefit unmeasured. (5) Avoid LangMem's default pattern of running the manager on every message (the docs themselves say it is expensive). Sources: https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/concepts/memory, https://langchain-ai.github.io/langmem/concepts/conceptual_guide/, https://langchain-ai.github.io/langmem/guides/delayed_processing/, https://langchain-ai.github.io/langmem/guides/extract_semantic_memories/, https://langchain-ai.github.io/langmem/background_quickstart/, https://langchain-ai.github.io/langmem/guides/optimize_compound_system/
- **CrewAI unified Memory**: A single Memory class (remember, recall, forget, tree, info, extract_memories) usable standalone or attached to Crews, Agents and Flows; crewai 1.15.30 was released 2026-10-24 (https://docs.crewai.com/en/concepts/memory, https://pypi.org/project/crewai/). Memory model: WRITES: remember() with scope, categories and importance omitted lets an LLM infer them (defaults scope '/', importance 0.5, still stored if the LLM fails); on save, if similarity to an existing record is at least consolidation_threshold 0.85 an LLM chooses keep, update, delete or insert_new (comparison limit 5; threshold 1.0 disables); remember_many() runs on a background thread and returns immediately, dedupes within the batch at cosine 0.98 with no LLM, and every recall() first drains pending writes. Crew integration: memory=True; after each task the crew extracts facts from the output and stores them; before each task the agent recalls relevant context and injects it into the task prompt. READS: composite = 0.5 x similarity + 0.3 x recency decay + 0.2 x importance, similarity = 1/(1+distance), decay = 0.5^(age_days/30) (semantic_weight, recency_weight, importance_weight, recency_half_life_days are configurable); shallow recall is a direct vector search with composite scoring and no LLM (about 200 ms); deep recall (default) runs a RecallFlow that analyzes the query (skipped for queries under 200 chars), selects scopes, searches in parallel and routes by confidence (thresholds 0.8, 0.5, complexity 0.7, exploration budget 1); each MemoryMatch carries match_reasons. STORAGE: LanceDB under ./.crewai/memory by default, OpenAI text-embedding-3-large (3072 dims) default embedder; hierarchical scopes inferred by the LLM (docs recommend depth 2 to 3), MemoryScope confines to a subtree, MemorySlice recalls across disjoint branches and can be read-only; source tags and private=True restrict recall; memory content is sent to the configured LLM so sensitive data may need a local model. INJECTED every turn: nothing global; recalled context is injected per task. FORGETTING: recency decay in scoring and explicit forget(); no scheduled consolidation. Applicable to ClawCode: (1) The save-time consolidation decision is the cleanest fix for ClawCode's dedup, which currently replaces content at cosine 0.85 and keeps max importance; with bge-small, near-duplicates can be contradictions ('limit is 5' versus 'limit is 6'). Route only hits at or above 0.85 to a single cheap Haiku call with keep, update, supersede or insert_new, and mark the loser superseded instead of overwriting; cost is one small LLM call in a rare path plus a column. (2) Shallow versus deep recall maps to ClawCode's pre-turn retrieval (cheap, every 3 turns) versus lazy tools; the 200-char rule for skipping LLM query analysis is a useful gating heuristic. (3) Composite weights 0.5/0.3/0.2 with a 30-day half-life are close to ClawCode's relevance; if ClawCode wants to rank pre-turn hits with importance and recency instead of only RRF plus path nudges, these are a sane starting point. (4) Drain-before-read for async writes prevents a turn missing the fact saved one second ago; ClawCode's scanner indexes new files within about 1 s but a flush in flight could still be missed. (5) Skip scope inference by LLM; ClawCode already has per-agent DBs and path scoping. Sources: https://docs.crewai.com/en/concepts/memory, https://pypi.org/project/crewai/
- **AutoGen, Microsoft Agent Framework, Foundry Agent Service Memory, Magentic-One and Magentic-UI**: AutoGen is in maintenance mode, limited to bug fixes and security patches, and its README tells new users to start with Microsoft Agent Framework (https://github.com/microsoft/autogen). Agent Framework (docs updated 2026-10-07) and Foundry Agent Service Memory (preview, docs updated 2026-06-02) are the live Microsoft memory stories; Magentic-One and Magentic-UI are research agents built on AutoGen. Memory model: AUTOGEN AgentChat: a Memory protocol with add, query, update_context, clear, close; ListMemory keeps entries chronologically and appends them all; ChromaDBVectorMemory (k, score_threshold, embedding function config; docs example k=3, threshold 0.4), RedisMemory and Mem0Memory exist; each run queries memory and update_context injects results as a system message beginning 'Relevant memory content (in chronological order):'; writes are manual add calls (https://microsoft.github.io/autogen/stable/user-guide/agentchat-user-guide/memory.html). AGENT FRAMEWORK: history providers (in-memory, Cosmos DB and Redis in preview) are separate from context providers with before_run and after_run hooks that extend instructions and extract memories from input messages; only one history provider should set load_messages=True; recommended stack is local history, a managed memory provider such as FoundryMemoryProvider(scope='user-123'), and an audit store last with store_context_messages=True (https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/agent-framework/get-started/memory). FOUNDRY MEMORY: managed store with three phases, extraction (preferences, facts, activity summaries), LLM consolidation (merge duplicates, resolve conflicts such as a new allergy) and retrieval; three types, user profile memory (retrieve near conversation start for stable personalization), chat summary memory (retrieve per turn from current messages) and procedural memory (retrieve when the user asks for a recurring workflow); memory search tool for read and write during conversations or low-level APIs; item-level CRUD, store-level default TTL, direct remember-or-forget commands; quotas 100 scopes per store and 10000 memories per scope; the doc warns that LLM-extracted memories are exposed to prompt injection and memory corruption and recommends content-safety screening and adversarial testing (https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/foundry/agents/concepts/what-is-memory). MAGENTIC-ONE: no cross-task memory; the Orchestrator keeps a Task Ledger (facts, educated guesses, plan) in an outer loop and a Progress Ledger (self-reflection per step) in an inner loop, and when a stall count exceeds 2 it updates the Task Ledger and replans (https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/articles/magentic-one-a-generalist-multi-agent-system-for-solving-complex-tasks/). MAGENTIC-UI: plan learning, the user can ask it to reflect on a finished task and save a step-by-step plan; plans are stored as task and plan pairs indexed by task description, shown in an editable gallery, may surface while the user types a similar task, and fall back to AutoGen's Task-Centric Memory to retrieve plans for similar tasks; the paper explicitly omits other memory types such as user preferences (https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/blog/magentic-ui-an-experimental-human-centered-web-agent, https://arxiv.org/pdf/2507.22358, the latter as summarized in search results since the PDF text was compressed). Applicable to ClawCode: (1) The ledger pattern (facts, guesses, plan; progress with stall counter) is within-task working memory; ClawCode already has goal-loop and background-task-board, so only the stall-count replan trigger (replan after more than 2 stalled steps) is new and cheap. (2) Foundry's split of retrieval timing is a clean rule for ClawCode: user-profile memory loaded once at session start (Tier 1), chat-summary memory retrieved per turn (pre-turn retrieval), procedural memory retrieved only when a recurring workflow is requested (path nudge for procedures). (3) The audit-store-last idea suggests logging exactly what memory context was injected each turn (ClawCode has context-assembler and compaction event logs) so bad memories can be traced to the turns they influenced. (4) Foundry's direct remember-or-forget commands plus item CRUD argue for a forget path that deletes or supersedes a specific memory by id from Discord. (5) Magentic-UI's user-reviewed plan library suits ClawCode's operator veto model; a 'save this as a procedure' command that stores a plan keyed by task description is cheap. Sources: https://github.com/microsoft/autogen, https://microsoft.github.io/autogen/stable/user-guide/agentchat-user-guide/memory.html, https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/agent-framework/get-started/memory, https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/foundry/agents/concepts/what-is-memory, https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/articles/magentic-one-a-generalist-multi-agent-system-for-solving-complex-tasks/, https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/blog/magentic-ui-an-experimental-human-centered-web-agent
- **Cognee**: Open-source (Apache-2.0, about 32.7k GitHub stars) memory layer that builds a knowledge graph plus vectors from documents and conversations; the 1.0 release (2026-06-26 per a partner post, while the official product page still showed v1 as coming soon, so verify) reduced the API to four verbs: remember, recall, improve, forget (https://github.com/topoteretes/cognee, https://dlthub.com/blog/cognee-1-0, https://docs.cognee.ai/rust/operations.md). Memory model: remember = add + cognify (ingest, chunk, extract entities, build graph) + an improve pass by default; with --session-id the input is stored as session-backed QA history (fast session memory), without it as permanent graph memory. recall auto-routes: if a session id is given it checks the session cache first and falls through to the graph; 16 SearchType strategies exist (GraphCompletion, GraphCompletionCot, GraphCompletionContextExtension, GraphSummaryCompletion, TripletCompletion, RagCompletion, Chunks, Summaries, Temporal, Cypher, NaturalLanguage, FeelingLucky, Feedback, CodingRules, ChunksLexical, HybridCompletion) and HybridCompletion is the default. improve is the consolidation and feedback step: it runs memify-style triplet enrichment and feedback weighting that reinforces graph nodes behind good answers and dampens those behind poor ones, and when session ids are supplied it distills each session's Q&A into entity-anchored lesson documents tagged with the session_learnings node-set and cognifies them into the permanent graph. forget deletes an item, a dataset or everything and cascades across relational, graph, vector and file storage. The core can run on one Postgres (relational metadata, pgvector, graph state; graph on Postgres is marked a demo feature), with per-user and per-group graphs and read/write/delete/share permissions; a Rust core, TypeScript SDK, Cognee Cloud and cognee-mcp (for Claude Code and Cursor) exist. INJECTED every turn: nothing built in; the app calls recall. WRITES: explicit remember calls on the hot path or batch; improve runs on demand or on a schedule the app chooses. Applicable to ClawCode: Do not adopt a graph database; ClawCode already has wikilink graph edges and FTS5 plus vectors. Two ideas transfer. (1) Session memory distinct from permanent memory with an explicit promotion step: ClawCode's session summaries are the session layer and the dream pass is the improve step; making promotion consume feedback (was the recalled chunk used, did the user correct it) would mirror Cognee's weight reinforcement; cost: record per-chunk outcome signals (used via citation tag, corrected via user message) and nudge importance by a bounded amount; moderate. (2) Distilling sessions into entity-anchored lesson documents (one note per entity or procedure with links back to source sessions) rather than chronological digests would make weekly digests more retrievable; cost is a prompt change in consolidation. (3) The forget cascade (one call removes rows, vectors and files) is worth having as a tested function because ClawCode stores in memories, FTS5, sqlite-vec, chunk tables and markdown files. Sources: https://github.com/topoteretes/cognee, https://dlthub.com/blog/cognee-1-0, https://docs.cognee.ai/rust/operations.md, https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24478, https://www.cognee.ai/research-and-evaluation-results, https://www.cognee.ai/knowledge-graph-memory-benchmarks
- **Supermemory**: MIT-licensed (about 31k GitHub stars) memory and context API: ingest documents, chats and connector data (Drive, Notion, Gmail), and get back document chunks, extracted memories in a temporal fact graph, and an always-on user profile, all under a hard namespace boundary (https://supermemory.ai/docs/concepts/how-it-works, https://github.com/supermemoryai/supermemory). Memory model: INGEST: queued, extracting, chunking (contextual chunking), embedding, indexing, done; a stable id lets updated conversations be re-ingested with diff billing. DREAMING (memory formation) is a second phase after chunks are indexed: 'dynamic' (default) groups related documents so memories form from coherent units and may continue after status done; 'instant' dreams each document immediately at one extra operation per document (blog post 'Introducing Dynamic Dreaming', 2026-05-25, listed on their blog; I could not fetch it). MEMORIES: atomic facts that resolve ambiguous references, each keeping its source chunk as evidence, linked by three relation types: updates (contradiction or correction), extends (added detail), derives (inference from several memories); older facts are kept, not erased; temporal fields documentDate (when the source was written) and eventDate (when the event happened) (https://supermemory.ai/research/longmembench). PROFILE (https://supermemory.ai/docs/user-profiles): POST /ns/{namespace}/profile returns static (long-term facts), dynamic (recent context, each entry prefixed [YYYY-MM-DD]) and buckets (up to 50 named buckets, built-in 'preferences'), described as 'a dynamic compaction that's done by supermemory in real-time'; entries have ids so a profile entry can be forgotten directly and older ones may be 'aggregated_' summaries; a fresh namespace can show an empty profile for minutes unless dreaming: 'instant' is passed. READ: inject profile.static under 'about the user' and profile.dynamic under 'current context' in the system prompt (always-on), and separately run hybrid search for query-ranked memories with their source chunks (docs example threshold 0.6); the profile call takes no query. FORGETTING: learning model decides what to forget, by relations and temporal supersession rather than deletion. Applicable to ClawCode: (1) Add updates/extends/derives style links (or a single superseded_by plus valid_to) to memories so that contradictions keep history and retrieval prefers the newest; this fixes ClawCode's overwrite-on-dedup and helps the knowledge-update and temporal question types where most systems are weakest. Cost: two columns and retrieval filters. (2) Store event_date separately from captured_at; LongMemEval found time-aware indexing and query expansion raised temporal recall by 6.8 to 11.3 percent (https://arxiv.org/html/2410.10813v2). Cost: extraction prompt plus a date filter. (3) The static/dynamic profile split is a good shape for USER.md: a short stable block plus a dated 'recent context' block regenerated by the dream pass; cost: dream prompt change. (4) Keep each memory's source chunk attached so any injected fact is auditable, which ClawCode partly has via chunk paths. (5) Do not chase their agentic multi-agent retrieval (ASMR); it is self-described as experimental and not their production engine. Sources: https://supermemory.ai/docs/concepts/how-it-works, https://supermemory.ai/docs/user-profiles, https://supermemory.ai/research/longmembench, https://supermemory.ai/research.md, https://thesumancoder.substack.com/p/the-end-of-vector-databases-how-supermemory, https://github.com/supermemoryai/supermemory
- **Honcho (Plastic Labs)**: AGPL-3.0 memory server (about 7.8k GitHub stars, honcho-ai 2.7.0 on PyPI 2026-10-22) that models 'peers' (users, agents, groups, ideas), reasons over their messages in the background, and serves context just-in-time through a chat (dialectic) endpoint and a get-context endpoint; it is one of the external memory providers Hermes Agent can use and OpenClaw lists as an alternative memory plugin (https://github.com/plastic-labs/honcho, https://honcho.dev/docs/v3/documentation/core-concepts/architecture, https://docs.openclaw.ai/concepts/memory.md). Memory model: ENTITIES: workspace, peer, session, message (messages may carry documents or emails); settings cascade workspace to peer to session. WRITE PATH: a message is written to PostgreSQL and a reasoning task is enqueued in the same request, so writes never wait on an LLM. BACKGROUND (per message, off a queue, https://github.com/plastic-labs/honcho/blob/main/CLAUDE.md): the Deriver is a single structured-output LLM call per batch (not an agent loop) that extracts explicit and deductive conclusions into vector collections; the Summarizer writes a short summary every 20 messages and a long summary every 60 (defaults). DREAMER (https://github.com/plastic-labs/honcho/blob/main/src/config.py): enabled by default; a dream becomes due when at least 50 new explicit conclusions exist (DOCUMENT_THRESHOLD), the collection has been idle at least 60 minutes, and at least 8 hours have passed since the last dream, polled every 300 s; it runs a deduction specialist (12 tool iterations) and an induction specialist (10) plus card refresh, prioritizing conclusions by surprisal computed with tree structures over embeddings; outputs are deductive and inductive conclusions with links to their premises (reasoning trees, traversable with get_reasoning_chain), consolidation of redundant or stale conclusions, and updated peer cards (compact biographical summaries). READ PATH: the Dialectic agent (the only tool-using agent on the synchronous path) loops over tools search_memory, search_messages, grep_messages, get_messages_by_date_range, search_messages_temporal, get_observation_context and get_reasoning_chain, at five reasoning levels (minimal, low, medium, high, max) each with its own model, and minimal uses only search_memory and search_messages; Get Context assembles relevant conclusions plus recent messages for prompt injection. Conclusion taxonomy from the design essay: explicit, deductive (certain from premises), inductive (patterns), abductive (best explanation) (https://honcho.dev/blog/memory-as-reasoning). INJECTED every turn: up to the integrator; typical pattern is peer card plus get-context output. FORGETTING: dream consolidation removes redundant or stale conclusions. Applicable to ClawCode: (1) Dream gating: replace ClawCode's idle-30-minutes-only trigger with Honcho's three-way gate (N new facts since last dream, idle at least 60 minutes, at least 8 hours since last run) so the haiku dream does not run on near-empty input and costs less; cost: a counter and a timestamp, trivial. (2) Surprisal-based selection: prioritize chunks whose embeddings are far from existing neighbors (novel facts) for dream review instead of the most recent chunks; ClawCode already has bge-small embeddings and sqlite-vec so nearest-neighbor distance is cheap to compute; moderate value. (3) Peer cards: a compact card (similar to USER.md) maintained only by the dream pass with a hard length cap, separate from agent-edited entries; cost: dream prompt change. (4) Conclusion levels and premise links (explicit versus inferred, with source ids) let the agent and the operator see which memories are inferences; add a 'derived_from' list to dream-created memories and show it in the Discord veto message; low cost. (5) Do not copy the dialectic chat agent (a second LLM in the read path); Letta and OpenClaw results suggest the main agent with simple search tools is competitive. Sources: https://github.com/plastic-labs/honcho, https://github.com/plastic-labs/honcho/blob/main/CLAUDE.md, https://github.com/plastic-labs/honcho/blob/main/src/config.py, https://honcho.dev/docs/v3/documentation/core-concepts/architecture, https://honcho.dev/docs/v3/documentation/core-concepts/reasoning, https://honcho.dev/blog/benchmarking-honcho
- **Cross-system benchmark evidence (LongMemEval, LoCoMo, Letta filesystem result, Mem0 paper)**: The only external yardsticks used by the systems above. LongMemEval (arXiv 2410.10813, ICLR 2025) has 500 questions over scalable chat histories testing information extraction, multi-session reasoning, temporal reasoning, knowledge updates and abstention. LoCoMo is a long multi-session conversation QA set. BEAM stresses memory up to 10M tokens with rubric scoring. Most published memory numbers come from the vendor selling the system. Memory model: Findings that translate into design rules. LongMemEval paper (https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.10813, https://arxiv.org/html/2410.10813v2): commercial assistants and long-context LLMs lose about 30 percent accuracy when memorizing across sustained interactions; storing rounds beats storing whole sessions; compressing into extracted facts loses information overall but helps multi-session reasoning; adding extracted facts to index keys gave an average +9.4 percent recall@k and +5.4 percent QA accuracy; time-aware indexing plus query expansion gave +6.8 to +11.3 percent temporal recall but only with a strong LLM doing the expansion; reading strategies (Chain-of-Note, JSON format) added up to 10 points of QA accuracy. Letta (2025-08-12, https://www.letta.com/blog/benchmarking-ai-agent-memory): an agent with GPT-4o mini that only had grep, search_files, open and close over a plain file of conversation history scored 74.0 percent on LoCoMo versus 68.5 percent reported for Mem0's graph variant, with tool rules forcing it to start with search_files; Letta concludes agentic multi-query search beats single-shot retrieval, that agent capability matters more than the storage mechanism, and that LoCoMo may not measure agent memory well. Applicable to ClawCode: (1) ClawCode agents are Claude Code sessions that are strong at grep and file tools, which supports keeping markdown plus FTS5 as the substrate and investing in search tool quality (query rewriting, multi-query, open-file) over new storage engines. (2) Index memories at the round or fact level with a pointer to the source rather than session-level blobs, and add LLM-extracted fact strings as extra FTS and embedding keys (+9.4 percent recall@k in the paper); cost: one extraction call per ingested session summary, cached. (3) Build a regression eval from real Discord and ticket interactions covering the five LongMemEval ability types, especially knowledge updates, temporal reasoning and abstention; ClawCode's current in-repo eval is 11 and 15 synthetic queries with recall@5 = 1.0 and MRR 0.39 and 0.35 (from the vault note), which saturates and cannot discriminate. A LongMemEval-S subset run as a smoke test is feasible but needs a judge model; cost moderate. (4) Treat every vendor headline as marketing until reproduced on your own data; Honcho itself reports Gemini 3 Pro alone scoring 92 percent on LongMemEval S. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.10813, https://arxiv.org/html/2410.10813v2, https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.19413, https://arxiv.org/html/2504.19413, https://www.letta.com/blog/benchmarking-ai-agent-memory, https://mem0.ai/blog/ai-memory-benchmarks-in-2026

#### Retrieval engineering for small local memory stores (thousands to low millions of short records): hybrid fusion, reranking, query rewriting, decay/importance, MMR, contextual headers, late chunking, embedding choice, sqlite-vec limits. Method notes: web sources read as of 2026-10-25; sqlite-vec limits were read from the upstream C source on GitHub; KNN timings were measured locally in this session on an Apple M4 Pro with synthetic random unit vectors (no model weights were downloaded, so no CPU embedding or reranker latency was measured); ClawCode code observations come from reading src/memory in the repo.

- At thousands to a few hundred thousand records, retrieval quality is the problem, not search speed: use exact brute-force vector search and spend effort on what is indexed and how candidates are scored. Evidence: Measured locally (M4 Pro, sqlite-vec 0.1.9, 384-d, k=20): float32 3.7 ms at 10K, 37 ms at 100K, 190 ms at 500K; int8 2.7, 28, 140 ms; bit 6.8 ms at 100K and 68 ms at 1M. Upstream author's practical limit is hundreds of thousands of vectors for a 100 ms target. vstash holds NDCG@10 flat (.685 to .690) from 5K to 50K chunks at 20.9 ms median end to end. Source: Local benchmark in this session (scratchpad vecbench.cjs) ; https://alexgarcia.xyz/blog/2024/sqlite-vec-stable-release/index.html ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.15484
- Hybrid BM25 + vector helps, but expect low-single-digit NDCG gains over a good dense baseline on short, mixed-vocabulary records, larger over weak or small dense models, and always keep the lexical channel for identifiers. Evidence: vstash: hybrid vs vector-only on 1,000 ML papers NDCG@10 .658 vs .568 with bge-small, but on a 786-chunk corpus hybrid was lower at NDCG@10 (.803 vs .832) while higher at NDCG@5 (.814 vs .809). LongMemEval-S with MiniLM: fusion +0.67 pp R@1 over BM25, not significant. LoCoMo: BM25 alone .640 Hit@1 beat mean-pooled dense (.384 to .427), and fusion added 8.8 to 17.2 pp over late-interaction dense alone. Source: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.15484 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2606.04194
- Prefer score-based (convex) fusion once you have a few hundred labeled queries; use RRF when you have none, and never leave k=60 untested on short lists. Evidence: Tuned CC beat RRF(60) on every one of nine BEIR/MS MARCO datasets by 1.5 to 3.2 NDCG points (e.g., FiQA .496 vs .464); OpenSearch measured RRF 3.86% lower average NDCG@10; LoCoMo .752 vs .718 Hit@1. RRF parameters tuned in-domain transfer poorly (HotpotQA fell to .621). With two top-20 lists at k=60 the rank-1 to rank-20 contribution ratio is only 1.31x (Qdrant defaults to k=2, ratio 7.3x). Source: https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2210.11934 ; https://opensearch.org/blog/introducing-reciprocal-rank-fusion-hybrid-search/ ; https://arxiv.org/html/2606.04194 ; https://qdrant.tech/documentation/concepts/hybrid-queries/
- Lexical weight in a convex blend usually lands between 0.2 and 0.4; the optimum is flat, so choose a robust value rather than over-tuning. Evidence: LoCoMo z-score fusion: lexical weight 0.40 optimal and Hit@1 stayed above .73 for 0.25 to 0.50. RAG best-practices paper: sparse weight 0.3 against dense 1.0. vstash defaults to 0.4 FTS weight with IDF-adaptive adjustment (up to +3.4% except the long-query ArguAna case). Source: https://arxiv.org/html/2606.04194 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2407.01219 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.15484
- Do not enable an off-the-shelf web-search cross-encoder by default for memory recall; use a reranker only if it is validated on your own queries, fine-tuned on memory data, or a strong LLM-class model off the latency-critical path. Evidence: vstash: ms-marco-MiniLM and bge-reranker-base changed NDCG by -0.3% to -3.1% for +560 to +2100 ms. LoCoMo: MiniLM-L-6-v2 on fused top-10 dropped Hit@1 .701 to .633 (-6.88 pp, CI -9.34 to -4.34). Counterpoints: Cohere rerank took Anthropic's failure rate 2.9% to 1.9%; a MiniLM-L6 fine-tuned on LoCoMo raised MRR .5824 to .6560; jina-reranker-v3 carried most of a RAG pipeline's quality in a 2026 ablation. Source: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.15484 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2606.04194 ; https://www.anthropic.com/news/contextual-retrieval ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.10842 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2606.28367v1
- Record granularity and index keys move recall more than embedding model size: index small units (round or fact), add extracted facts as extra keys that point to the parent, and avoid mean-pooling long multi-topic chunks. Evidence: LongMemEval (Stella V5): round-level recall@10 .692 to .784 and session-level .783 to .862 when keys = value + facts (+9.4% average recall); Contriever (110M) matched the 1.5B Stella at session level (R@5 .723 vs .720). LoCoMo: mean-pooled session embeddings Hit@1 .38 to .43 vs max-sim .52 to .66, with the gap growing with session length (+19.4 to +27.1 pp for e5-large-v2). Source: https://arxiv.org/html/2410.10813v2 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2606.04194
- Put context into the indexed text at write time (heading path, title, date, subject, optionally an LLM blurb) rather than trying to recover it at query time. Evidence: Anthropic: top-20 failure 5.7% to 3.7% with contextual embeddings and to 2.9% adding contextual BM25 (-35% and -49%), 1.9% with reranking; cost $1.02 per million document tokens at 2024 Claude 3 Haiku pricing. Late chunking gives a smaller, no-LLM gain: average nDCG@10 52.2 to 54.0 (fixed 256-token chunks); a later comparison found contextual retrieval slightly better (.308 vs .294 NDCG@10 on an NFCorpus subset) but costlier. Source: https://www.anthropic.com/news/contextual-retrieval ; https://arxiv.org/html/2409.04701v3 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2504.19754
- Query expansion (HyDE, rewrite, multi-query) helps weak retrievers and hurts strong ones; gate it behind low-confidence results and prefer deterministic expansions (date parsing, pronoun resolution) on the hot path. Evidence: Weller et al.: gain correlates negatively with baseline strength across 11 techniques, 12 datasets, 24 retrievers. HyDE with hybrid retrieval added +0.84 nDCG@10 on DL19 at 11.16 s vs 3.20 s per query. LongMemEval temporal expansion helped only with GPT-4o (+6.8% to +11.3% recall), not Llama 3.1 8B. Mem0 and Hindsight classify temporal intent with rules or a small model rather than a per-query large-LLM call. Source: https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.08541 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2407.01219 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2410.10813v2 ; https://docs.mem0.ai/core-concepts/memory-evaluation ; https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2512.12818v1
- Recency and importance should be bounded, type-aware tie-breakers applied after candidate selection, not a global prior and not a filter. Evidence: Frequency+decay hurt NDCG@10 on all 16 tested configurations on SciFact (-1.6% best case, -9.0% ungated) because access frequency is orthogonal to query relevance, yet helped (+4.6% average) on simulated access patterns that correlate with relevance. A-MAC's content-type prior contributed 0.107 F1 vs 0.013 for recency. Qdrant's example scales the decay term by 0.1; Generative Agents min-max normalizes all three terms and weights them equally. Half-lives in the references range from 2.9 to 5.8 days; ClawCode uses 30 days. Source: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.15484 ; https://arxiv.org/pdf/2603.04549 ; https://qdrant.tech/documentation/concepts/hybrid-queries/ ; https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2304.03442
- Add cheap read-time diversity: MMR at lambda about 0.5 within a document plus near-duplicate collapse around cosine 0.95, and widen each hit with adjacent context instead of retrieving more hits. Evidence: vstash: unique documents per top-5 3.2 to 5.0, NDCG@5 .814 to .829 (+1.8%); +/-1 chunk expansion gives 2.64x more text per hit for +0.12 ms. Qdrant warns MMR needs a candidate pool larger than the limit. Source: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.15484 ; https://github.com/stffns/vstash ; https://qdrant.tech/course/beginners/module-6/diversity-mmr/index.md
- Choose the embedder by retrieval quality on your own records and CPU cost, and compare MTEB numbers only within the same MTEB version. Evidence: MTEB v1 retrieval averages: bge-small 51.68, bge-base 53.25, arctic-embed-s 51.98, gte-small 49.46, nomic-v1.5 53.01; MTEB English v2 means (different tasks): Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B 70.70, EmbeddingGemma 69.67, Jina v5 nano 71.0. On a real 1,000-paper corpus bge-base beat bge-small by .044 NDCG@10 for 2.3x latency. CPU latency numbers for these models were not found in any primary source. Source: https://huggingface.co/BAAI/bge-small-en-v1.5 ; https://huggingface.co/BAAI/bge-base-en-v1.5 ; https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B ; https://huggingface.co/google/embeddinggemma-300m ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.15484
- Calibrate every similarity threshold on your own data; BGE-style cosine scores are compressed and thresholds do not transfer across corpora. Evidence: The bge v1.5 card says scores concentrate high, that 0.5 does not mean similar, and to pick a threshold (0.8, 0.85, 0.9) from your data. vstash's best-hit distance gate got F1 .996 on cross-domain ArguAna queries but .472 on in-domain NFCorpus. Source: https://huggingface.co/BAAI/bge-small-en-v1.5 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.15484
- Quantization is a storage lever more than a speed lever in sqlite-vec, and its quality cost depends on the model; measure it per model. Evidence: Local: int8 only 1.3x faster than float32 (140 vs 190 ms at 500K x 384-d) while bit was about 5.6x faster. Hugging Face retention: all-MiniLM-L6-v2 int8 90.79%, e5-base-v2 binary 74.77%, mxbai int8 97%, Cohere int8 100%. sqlite-vec's own rescore benchmark: bit with oversample 8 gave recall .988, oversample 4 gave .962, and it warns of 70 to 80 percent for models not trained for quantization. Source: Local benchmark in this session ; https://huggingface.co/blog/embedding-quantization ; https://github.com/asg017/sqlite-vec/pull/276
- Give a strong reader a wider, token-budgeted pool and let it narrow, instead of fixing a small k. Evidence: Anthropic tested 5, 10 and 20 chunks and found 20 best. LongMemEval: GPT-4o kept improving past 20K retrieved tokens while Llama 3.1 8B fell off beyond about 3K. Memanto's k=10 to 40 plus threshold change gave +20.4 pp on LongMemEval but is confounded. Mem0 defaults to top-200 at about 6,700 to 7,000 tokens (vendor). Source: https://www.anthropic.com/news/contextual-retrieval ; https://arxiv.org/html/2410.10813v2 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2604.22085 ; https://docs.mem0.ai/core-concepts/memory-evaluation
- Retrieval claims need a real, large enough eval set with confidence intervals; most memory-system leaderboards are self-run or confounded. Evidence: vstash's own LLM-judge tests used N=30 and are called directional; Mem0 publishes two different LoCoMo/LongMemEval pairs (91.6/93.4 and 92.5/94.4) with a +/-1 point judge CI and no per-signal ablation; Hindsight's 91.4% needs Gemini-3 (83.6% with OSS-20B); the LoCoMo reranker result had a CI of -9.34 to -4.34 pp only because they bootstrapped 4,000 resamples; ClawCode's own eval has 11 and 15 synthetic queries. Source: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.15484 ; https://mem0.ai/blog/mem0-the-token-efficient-memory-algorithm ; https://docs.mem0.ai/core-concepts/memory-evaluation ; https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2512.12818v1 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2606.04194

Anti-patterns: Adding fixed additive score nudges sized for cosine scores onto RRF scores. In ClawCode fused scores span about 0.0125 to 0.0328, so +0.2 (vault) or -0.2 (archive) path weights dominate instead of breaking ties; any vault chunk at rank 20 of one list outranks the best non-vault chunk, contradicting the code comment that calls them tiebreakers.; Using RRF k=60 on two short top-20 lists as an untested default: rank 1 and rank 20 differ by only 1.31x, so ordering collapses to 'in both lists'. Qdrant defaults to k=2; Bruch et al. show RRF is parameter sensitive.; Enabling an MS MARCO-trained cross-encoder (ms-marco-MiniLM, bge-reranker-base) on every pre-turn retrieval for conversational or technical memory: published small-corpus results show -0.3% to -3.1% NDCG at +560 to +2100 ms, and -6.9 pp Hit@1 on LoCoMo.; Applying access-frequency or access-count times decay as a global ranking prior for every query; it is orthogonal to query relevance and lowered NDCG on every configuration tested (-1.6% to -9.0%).; Hard time-window cutoffs (dropping dated files older than 14 days) instead of soft, type-aware decay; old decisions, preferences and procedures are exactly what long-term memory is for.; Embedding long, multi-topic chunks (whole sessions, long H2 sections) and relying on mean pooling: session-level mean-pooled dense retrieval scored Hit@1 .38 to .43, below BM25's .640 on LoCoMo; split into rounds or facts and index the parent via extra keys.; Running HyDE, multi-query or an LLM rewrite on every turn with a strong embedder: expansions correlate negatively with retriever strength and cost 3.5x latency for under 1 nDCG point in a hybrid setup; 8 pseudo-documents did not beat 1.; Comparing embedding models across MTEB versions or by leaderboard mean (MTEB v1 retrieval vs MTEB English v2 mean are different task sets) and trusting vendor latency claims such as '<15 ms on EdgeTPU' or '<5 ms' without hardware; benchmark on the target CPU with batch size 1 and 20 to 60 token inputs.; Tuning fusion weights, decay half-lives or thresholds on a tiny synthetic eval (ClawCode has 11 and 15 queries) or on an LLM judge with N=30; differences reported in the literature are 1.5 to 4 points.; Planning for ANN too early: sqlite-vec's stable v0.1.9 is exact brute force, its rescore, IVF and DiskANN indexes are v0.1.10 alphas, DiskANN inserts are 93x to 121x slower and DELETEs are expensive, and at 30K vectors per agent a scan costs about 11 ms. (vstash's paper also mislabels sqlite-vec as ANN.); Trusting int8 recall claims without checking the model: all-MiniLM-L6-v2 lost 9.2% of nDCG@10 at int8 in Hugging Face's test and e5-base-v2 lost 25% at binary; a fixed [-1, 1] int8 range wastes most of the 127 levels for unit-norm 384-d vectors (typical components about 0.05).; Treating vendor memory benchmarks as comparable evidence: Mem0's April 2026 numbers run on a proprietary platform with undisclosed fusion and no ablation, and the same company's pages disagree (91.6/93.4 vs 92.5/94.4); Hindsight's 91.4% depends on the Gemini-3 answerer.; Letting an LLM rewrite or contextualize every chunk on every re-index: Anthropic's $1.02 per million document tokens assumed 2024 Claude 3 Haiku pricing and prompt caching; re-run only for changed documents and use deterministic headers for everything else.; Skipping the 'no relevant memory' case: always injecting top-k even when the best vector distance is poor adds noise; vstash gates on best-hit distance, but the threshold is corpus dependent and must be calibrated.

Systems reviewed:

- **Reciprocal Rank Fusion (RRF) for BM25 + vector lists**: Merges the FTS5 BM25 list and the sqlite-vec KNN list using ranks only, so cosine distance and BM25 never have to be calibrated against each other. This is what ClawCode does today (k=60, vec top-20 + BM25 top-20). Memory model: Formula: RRF(d) = sum over lists r of w_r / (k + rank_r(d)), 1-based ranks. Cormack, Clarke and Buettcher (SIGIR 2009, old source) fixed k=60 in a pilot and never changed it; their pilot on fusing 30 systems (TREC topics 351-400) shows a flat plateau: MAP .2072 at k=0, .2123 at 10, .2139 at 30, .2145 at 60, .2147 at 80, .2142 at 100, .2098 at 500, and RRF beat Condorcet and CombMNZ by 4 to 5 percent. That result fuses many systems, not two. Qdrant's default k is 2 (settable since v1.16; weighted RRF since v1.17), OpenSearch's default is 60. Alex Garcia's reference SQL for FTS5 + sqlite-vec uses rrf_k=60 with weights 1.0/1.0 and a full outer join of two CTEs. vstash uses weights w_vec=0.6, w_fts=0.4 and an adaptive per-query weight from a sigmoid of the mean IDF of the stemmed query terms (rare terms push weight to FTS). My arithmetic: with two lists truncated to 20, k=60 gives per-list contributions from 1/61=0.0164 (rank 1) to 1/80=0.0125 (rank 20), a 1.31x spread, so the fused score is mostly 'appears in both lists'; k=10 gives a 2.7x spread and k=2 gives 7.3x. Applicable to ClawCode: Keep RRF as the no-labels default but (1) test k in {2, 5, 10, 60} on real queries; at top-20 and k=60 the rank signal is nearly flat. (2) Found while reading src/memory/memory-retrieval.ts: the comment says k=60 makes additive path weights (vault +0.2, procedures +0.1, archive -0.2) 'tiebreakers', but fused scores only span about 0.0125 to 0.0328 (2/61 max), so a +0.2 nudge is roughly 10x the whole spread: any vault chunk, even rank 20 in one list (0.2125), outranks the best non-vault chunk (0.0328), and archive chunks go negative. That is a lexicographic path-class ordering, not a nudge. Fix by scaling nudges to about 0.001 to 0.005 or by multiplying (score x 1.1 / x 0.8). Cost: a few lines plus a regression test; zero runtime cost. (3) Add per-query adaptive weights from fts5vocab IDF if the eval shows identifier-heavy queries losing (vstash: +0.1% SciFact to +3.4% FiQA over fixed weights, cached IDF table, negligible latency). Sources: http://cormack.uwaterloo.ca/cormacksigir09-rrf.pdf, https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2210.11934, https://opensearch.org/blog/introducing-reciprocal-rank-fusion-hybrid-search/, https://qdrant.tech/documentation/concepts/hybrid-queries/, https://alexgarcia.xyz/blog/2024/sqlite-vec-hybrid-search/index.html, https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.15484
- **Convex combination of normalized scores (CC, TM2C2, z-score, DBSF)**: Fuses BM25 and cosine by a weighted sum after normalizing each to a comparable scale, so score margins (a very strong lexical hit) survive fusion instead of being flattened to ranks. Memory model: Formula (Bruch et al.): f(d) = alpha * norm(semantic) + (1 - alpha) * norm(lexical), 0 <= alpha <= 1. TM2C2 = 'theoretical min-max': min is the function's theoretical infimum (0 for BM25, -1 for cosine), max comes from the candidate set; they show linear normalizations are rank-equivalent up to a shift in alpha and that alpha converges with under 5 percent of the training data. Variants: z-score within the candidate set (alpha*z(BM25) + (1-alpha)*z(dense)); Qdrant DBSF maps each list's mu +/- 3 sigma onto 0 to 1 and sums (a single outlier can skew it). Weights reported in other studies: lexical weight 0.4 on z-scores with Hit@1 above .73 for any lexical weight in [0.25, 0.50] (LoCoMo, 2606.04194); sparse weight 0.3 relative to dense 1.0 in S_h = 0.3*S_sparse + S_dense (Wang et al. 2024 RAG best practices); 0.4 lexical in vstash's fixed RRF weights. FTS5 returns bm25() negated (lower is better), so use -bm25 before normalizing; k1=1.2 and b=0.75 are hard-coded. Applicable to ClawCode: Add convex fusion as an alternative scorer behind a config flag and A/B it against RRF. Implementation sketch (my engineering suggestion, not from a source): take the union of vec top-N and BM25 top-N, then complete missing scores exactly instead of zero-filling (cosine for FTS-only hits via vec_distance_cosine on the stored vector; BM25 for vec-only hits via an FTS5 MATCH restricted to those rowids), normalize BM25 by the candidate max, use cosine as (cos+1)/(max+1), and start at lexical weight 0.3 to 0.4. Cost: roughly 60 lines in memory-retrieval.ts, one extra FTS5 query, no new dependency. Precondition: a real eval set. The in-repo eval has 11 and 15 synthetic queries, far too few to tune alpha or to detect the 2 to 4 point differences these papers report; log 150 to 300 real recall queries with the memory the agent actually used. Sources: https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2210.11934, https://opensearch.org/blog/introducing-reciprocal-rank-fusion-hybrid-search/, https://qdrant.tech/documentation/concepts/hybrid-queries/, https://arxiv.org/html/2606.04194, https://arxiv.org/html/2407.01219, https://www.sqlite.org/fts5.html
- **SQLite FTS5 BM25 knobs**: The lexical half of ClawCode's retrieval. FTS5 exposes few knobs, so what you index matters more than how you score. Memory model: bm25() constants k1=1.2 and b=0.75 are hard-coded and not tunable; the result is multiplied by -1 so ORDER BY rank ascending gives best first; extra arguments are per-column weights, e.g. bm25(ft, 10.0, 5.0) or persistently via INSERT INTO ft(ft, rank) VALUES('rank','bm25(10.0, 5.0)'); ORDER BY rank is faster than ORDER BY bm25(). tokenize='porter' stems English; tokenize='trigram' enables substring match (queries under 3 characters match nothing). vstash builds queries by double-quoting each word and OR-joining, which avoids FTS5 operator injection, and builds an IDF table lazily from the fts5vocab virtual table. FTS5 cannot filter by metadata inside the index. Applicable to ClawCode: Cheapest lexical wins: put the heading path and title in a separate FTS column and weight it (for example bm25(fts, 3.0, 1.0)) instead of relying on text alone; use a second trigram table only for identifiers, ticket numbers and file paths; build the IDF vocabulary from fts5vocab if adaptive weighting is added. Cost: schema migration plus reindex of memory_chunks_fts. Sources: https://www.sqlite.org/fts5.html, https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.15484, https://github.com/stffns/vstash
- **vstash (local-first SQLite + sqlite-vec + FTS5 + BGE-small), arXiv 2604.15484, Apr 2026**: The closest published analogue to ClawCode's stack: one SQLite file, bge-small-en-v1.5 (384-d) via FastEmbed ONNX, sqlite-vec for vectors, FTS5 with Porter stemming, RRF with adaptive IDF weights, intra-document MMR, +/-1 neighbour chunk expansion, and a distance-based confidence signal. Single-author preprint, 8 GitHub stars; treat as indicative. Memory model: Write: chunk (semantic or code-aware, 1024 tokens with 128 overlap in the BEIR runs), embed at about 700 chunks per second on CPU (self-reported). Read: vec top-k + FTS top-k, RRF (k=60, w_v .6, w_f .4, overridden by adaptive IDF weights), optional recency boost boosted = rrf * (1 + B * exp(-0.05 * days_ago)) with B=0 by default (half-life 13.9 days), MMR (lambda=0.5), context expansion +/-1 chunk. Forgetting: none; they tested frequency+decay scoring score = a*rrf + b*min(1, log(1+f)/log(1+S)) with f = (1+access_count)*exp(-L*days_ago), S=100, and removed it. Confidence: cosine distance of the best vector hit; tiers at distance <=0.95 high, 0.95 to 0.98 medium, >0.98 low. Applicable to ClawCode: Three portable ideas: (1) adaptive IDF fusion weights (cheap); (2) a relevance gate based on best-hit distance so the pre-turn injector can inject nothing when nothing is relevant (ClawCode currently always injects top 5 under a 1500 token budget; vstash's gate is domain dependent so calibrate on your own data); (3) self-supervised embedding refinement: mine queries where vec-heavy and FTS-heavy retrieval disagree and fine-tune bge-small with MultipleNegativesRankingLoss (lr 3e-6, 2 epochs, batch 64; TripletLoss destroyed the model), which gave +7.4% SciFact and +19.5% NFCorpus NDCG@10 over base. Cost for (3): a training pipeline and ONNX re-export; only worth it once there are thousands of real queries. Do not copy their at-scale claim blindly: it validated only to 50K chunks. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.15484, https://github.com/stffns/vstash
- **Cross-encoder reranking (ms-marco MiniLM, bge-reranker, Qwen3-Reranker, mxbai, fine-tuned memory rerankers)**: Second-stage rescoring of the top-N fused candidates with a (query, passage) cross-encoder. ClawCode has an optional Xenova/bge-reranker-base (q8 ONNX, roughly 278M-parameter XLM-R base) on top-20 returning top-5 with a timeout fallback. Memory model: Stage 2 only: top-N candidates (Anthropic used 150 then kept 20) are scored as pairs; scores are logits (sigmoid for 0 to 1). Qwen3-Reranker is an LLM-style yes/no logit scorer with a task instruction, 0.6B parameters and 28 layers. Hindsight's TEMPR uses cross-encoder/ms-marco-MiniLM-L-6-v2 over its RRF output and includes temporal metadata in the reranker input. Fine-tuning a small cross-encoder on conversational memory is what moves the needle (ConvMemory v2: 22.7M-parameter MiniLM fine-tuned on LoCoMo). Applicable to ClawCode: At ClawCode's scale, treat the reranker as unproven and default it off for the pre-turn path (it adds latency to every cadence turn and the published small-corpus evidence is negative for off-the-shelf web-trained models). Concretely: (1) run an A/B on logged real queries with paired bootstrap CIs before enabling; (2) keep it only on the lazy clawcode_memory_search path where a 0.5 to 2 s cost is tolerable and use a larger pool (Anthropic: 150 to 20); (3) if the gain is real, fine-tune a MiniLM-L6/L12 cross-encoder on your own recall logs (positives = chunks the agent actually used or the operator confirmed) rather than shipping bge-reranker-base; (4) Qwen3-Reranker-0.6B has the best published authors-run numbers among sub-1B options but is 28 layers, so only viable off the hot path. Cost of (3): label-collection plus a one-off training job; inference stays about 22M parameters. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.15484, https://arxiv.org/html/2606.04194, https://www.anthropic.com/news/contextual-retrieval, https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.10842, https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28062, https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06132
- **Query rewriting, HyDE, query2doc and time-aware query expansion for memory recall**: Changes what is embedded or searched: an LLM writes a hypothetical answer (HyDE, Dec 2022) or a pseudo-document (query2doc, 2023), or extracts a time range for temporal questions (LongMemEval, ICLR 2025). Memory model: HyDE: sample N hypothetical documents d_k, embed, average: v = mean_k f(d_k), optionally including the query as f(q) with weight 1/(N+1). Retriever was Contriever (unsupervised). query2doc: append LLM pseudo-doc to the query before BM25 or dense retrieval. LongMemEval time-aware expansion: LLM extracts a time range, candidates are filtered by timestamp, and the query is 'question + time range'. Mem0's April 2026 memory algorithm classifies temporal intent without an extra LLM call and adds a temporal score; Hindsight uses a rule-based date parser (two date-parsing libraries) with flan-t5-small as fallback and keeps memories whose occurrence interval overlaps the range, scored by midpoint proximity. Applicable to ClawCode: Do not add a per-turn LLM rewrite. The lazy tool path already has an LLM writing the query (the agent), which is query rewriting for free. For the pre-turn path use cheap deterministic steps: (1) embed 'last user message + last assistant sentence' to resolve pronouns; (2) a rule-based date parser (chrono-node style) that turns 'last Tuesday' or 'in March' into a created_at filter or boost, with no LLM; (3) only try HyDE on the lazy path when the first search returns a low-confidence best hit. Cost: (1) and (2) are small code; HyDE adds one Haiku call (seconds) per use. The evidence says expansion helps weak retrievers and hurts strong ones, so gate it. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2212.10496, https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2212.10496, https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.08541, https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.07678, https://arxiv.org/html/2407.01219, https://arxiv.org/html/2410.10813v2
- **Time decay and importance weighting formulas (Generative Agents, LangChain, MemoryBank, A-MAC, Qdrant/Elastic decay, vstash)**: Re-weights retrieval by age, access, and a write-time importance score. ClawCode: relevance = importance * 0.5^(days_since_access/30), memory-side score = 0.7*similarity + 0.3*relevance, then a multiplicative importance boost; importance is a deterministic heuristic (length, code, numbers, proper nouns, +0.2). Memory model: Generative Agents (Park et al., Apr 2023): score = alpha_rec*recency + alpha_imp*importance + alpha_rel*relevance, all alpha = 1, each min-max scaled to [0,1]; recency = 0.995^(hours since last retrieval) (half-life 138 h = 5.8 days); importance = LLM integer 1 to 10 at write time; relevance = cosine; reflection fires when summed importance of recent events exceeds 150 (2 to 3 times a day). LangChain TimeWeightedVectorStoreRetriever: score = semantic_similarity + (1 - decay_rate)^hours_passed since last access, default decay_rate 0.01 (half-life 69 h). A-MAC admission (write time): R = exp(-lambda*tau), lambda=0.01 per hour (half-life 69 h) plus rule-based content-type prior; learned weights by grid search. vstash opt-in boost: rrf*(1 + B*exp(-0.05*days)). Qdrant formula query: sum of $score and a decay term times 0.1 (example exp_decay with scale 180 days, midpoint 0.5). Elasticsearch exp decay: S = exp(lambda*max(0,|x-origin|-offset)), lambda = ln(decay)/scale, default decay 0.5; combined multiplicatively by default. MemoryBank (2023) is Ebbinghaus-inspired and reinforces on recall (formula not verified from the abstract). Applicable to ClawCode: ClawCode's 30-day half-life is far slower than the 3 to 6 day half-lives in the references, which is reasonable for work notes. Changes the evidence supports: (1) keep decay as a bounded multiplier or small additive term (Qdrant caps it at 0.1x) applied after candidates are chosen, never as a filter; ClawCode's rule that drops dated files older than 14 days is a hard cutoff and conflicts with this; (2) make the half-life depend on memory type (preferences, identity, decisions, procedures: no decay; transient state and daily logs: days), because A-MAC's content-type prior was by far the most important feature; (3) do not use access_count as a ranking prior on the hot path (query-orthogonal) but keep it for tiering and archiving; (4) if importance stays heuristic, add a type prior (preference, decision, constraint, fact, chatter) classified at write time by the existing Haiku summarizer call at negligible extra cost. Cost: small; mostly config plus a classifier field in the memory row. Sources: https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2304.03442, https://python.langchain.com/docs/how_to/time_weighted_vectorstore, https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.10250, https://arxiv.org/pdf/2603.04549, https://www.elastic.co/docs/reference/query-languages/query-dsl/query-dsl-function-score-query, https://qdrant.tech/documentation/concepts/hybrid-queries/
- **MMR diversity, near-duplicate collapse and neighbour-context expansion**: Stops the top-k from being five paraphrases of one memory or five adjacent chunks of one file, and widens each hit with its neighbours.
