Memory model: Maximal Marginal Relevance (Carbonell and Goldstein, 1998, old source; the scanned PDF could not be machine-read, formula taken from vstash and library docs): next = argmax over candidates c not in S of [lambda * Sim(c, q) - (1 - lambda) * max over s in S of Sim(c, s)], lambda=1 is pure relevance, lambda=0 pure diversity. vstash uses lambda=0.5 on min-max-normalized fused scores and only among chunks of the same document, and stops when the best remaining MMR value is negative. LangChain's lambda_mult default is 0.5 with fetch_k=20; Qdrant exposes a 'diversity' parameter in [0,1] and warns the candidate pool must exceed the limit for MMR to matter. vstash v0.39 also drops chunks whose cosine to a higher-ranked kept chunk is 0.95 or more. Context expansion fetches +/-1 adjacent chunk by sequence number. Applicable to ClawCode: ClawCode dedups at insert (cosine 0.85) but not at read time, and consolidated digests plus their source chunks can co-occur. Add MMR (lambda 0.5 to 0.7) over the fused top-20 using stored embeddings (about 20 x 20 dot products of 384 floats, microseconds), restricted within a path/document, plus a 0.95 near-duplicate collapse across paths. Add +/-1 neighbour expansion for H2 chunks in the lazy search tool only (token cost 2.6x per hit in vstash). Note the BGE v1.5 card says its similarity distribution is compressed and thresholds (0.8 to 0.9) should be set on your own data, so calibrate the 0.85 insert-dedup and 0.95 collapse thresholds empirically. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.15484, https://github.com/stffns/vstash, https://www.cs.cmu.edu/~jgc/publication/The_Use_MMR_Diversity_Based_LTMIR_1998.pdf, https://qdrant.tech/course/beginners/module-6/diversity-mmr/index.md, https://huggingface.co/BAAI/bge-small-en-v1.5, https://github.com/D-Star-AI/dsRAG
- **Contextual Retrieval (Anthropic), contextual chunk headers, and fact-key expansion**: Adds context to each chunk or memory at write time so both the embedding and the BM25 index can match what the text is about, not only what it says. Anthropic (Sep 2024): an LLM writes a 50 to 100 token situating blurb that is prepended to the chunk before embedding and BM25 indexing. Memory model: Write path only. Anthropic prompt: wrap the whole document in <document> tags and the chunk in <chunk> tags, ask for 'a short succinct context to situate this chunk within the overall document' and nothing else; generated with Claude 3 Haiku using prompt caching. Deterministic variant (dsRAG 'contextual chunk headers'): prepend document title and section-heading path, no LLM. Memory variant (LongMemEval): index each round plus LLM-extracted user facts as extra keys, K = V + fact, return the parent record. Anthropic also says to skip RAG entirely when the knowledge base is under about 200,000 tokens (roughly 500 pages) and use prompt caching instead. Applicable to ClawCode: Three tiers by cost. (a) Free: prepend 'path > H1 > H2' (and for ingested documents, doc title, page and section) to chunk text before embedding and put it in a weighted FTS column; requires reindexing memory_chunks and document chunks. (b) Cheap: for agent-saved memories, add the date and subject at write time ('2026-03-04, Jas, ticket 158003: ...'), and let the existing summarizer extract 1 to 3 atomic facts as extra index keys that point back to the parent row (dedupe by taking max score per parent). (c) LLM context blurbs for OCR'd documents via the dream pass or ingest pipeline: Anthropic's cost estimate is $1.02 per million document tokens, but that was computed at 2024 Claude 3 Haiku prices with 800-token chunks, 8k-token documents and 100 tokens of generated context per chunk, so recompute with current pricing; re-run only when a document changes. Note tier-1 files (MEMORY.md, USER.md) are already the 'put it all in context' pattern, which Anthropic recommends for small corpora. Sources: https://www.anthropic.com/news/contextual-retrieval, https://arxiv.org/html/2410.10813v2, https://github.com/D-Star-AI/dsRAG, https://arxiv.org/html/2504.19754
- **Late chunking (Jina, arXiv 2409.04701, Sep 2024)**: Runs a long-context embedding model over the whole document first and then mean-pools token vectors per chunk, so each chunk embedding carries surrounding context without any LLM call. Memory model: Write path: tokenize the full document (up to the model's limit, or overlapping macro-chunks beyond it), take last-hidden-state token embeddings, then pool per chunk span. Needs a long-context encoder that exposes token outputs (jina-embeddings-v2/v3, nomic-embed-text-v1 with 8192 context; bge-small is capped at 512 tokens). It is documented not to help when the target is a short needle in unrelated text. Applicable to ClawCode: Low priority. ClawCode's memories are short and already atomic, so there is nothing for late chunking to add there. It is only relevant to OCR'd document ingestion, and only after moving to a long-context embedder (nomic-embed-text-v1.5, 8192; Granite-small-r2, 8192; Qwen3-Embedding, 32K) and writing custom pooling code on top of transformers.js token outputs. A deterministic heading header plus overlap gets part of the benefit for free. Cost if attempted: custom embedding code path and a full re-embed of documents. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.04701, https://arxiv.org/html/2409.04701v3, https://arxiv.org/html/2504.19754, https://huggingface.co/nomic-ai/nomic-embed-text-v1.5
- **Embedding model choice for short memories (bge-small, bge-base, gte, nomic, arctic-embed-s, Granite-r2, Qwen3-Embedding, EmbeddingGemma, Jina v5)**: Chooses the encoder behind sqlite-vec. ClawCode uses bge-small-en-v1.5 (v2, int8) with legacy all-MiniLM-L6-v2 (v1), local ONNX via @huggingface/transformers. Memory model: MTEB English v1 (56 datasets) numbers from model cards, retrieval category average (nDCG@10 over 15 datasets) / overall: bge-small-en-v1.5 33.4M params, 384-d, 512 tokens: 51.68 / 62.17; bge-base-en-v1.5 110M, 768-d: 53.25 / 63.55; gte-small 33.4M, 384-d: 49.46 / 61.36; snowflake-arctic-embed-s 33M, 384-d: 51.98 retrieval; nomic-embed-text-v1.5 137M, 768-d, 8192 context, Apache-2.0: retrieval 53.01 (via HF quantization blog), overall MTEB 62.28 at 768-d, 61.96 at 512, 61.04 at 256, 59.34 at 128, 56.10 at 64 (matryoshka; needs search_query:/search_document: prefixes); all-MiniLM-L6-v2: 41.66 retrieval (HF blog) vs 56.3 overall. MTEB English v2 (different task set, not comparable to the above): Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B (0.6B, 1024-d with MRL 32 to 1024, 32K context): mean 70.70, retrieval 61.83 (4B 74.60/68.46; 8B 75.22/69.44), instructions improve results about 1% to 5%; EmbeddingGemma-300M (308M, 768-d, MRL 512/256/128, 2K context): mean 69.67 at 768-d, 69.18 at 512, 68.37 at 256, 66.66 at 128, Q8_0 69.49, Q4_0 69.31, 'under 200MB RAM' and '<15 ms for 256 tokens on EdgeTPU' are Google claims; Granite-embedding-small-english-r2 (47M, 384-d, 8192 context, Apache-2.0): MTEB-v2 61.1, BEIR retrieval 50.9; jina-embeddings-v5-text-nano (239M, 768-d): MTEB English v2 71.0, but CC BY-NC 4.0 (non-commercial). Prefix rules: bge v1.5 query instruction 'Represent this sentence for searching relevant passages:' is optional with a slight drop and applies only to short queries against long passages; never to passages. Applicable to ClawCode: Stay on bge-small unless a real-query eval shows recall misses; it is on the Pareto edge for CPU. If an upgrade is wanted, in order of cost-effectiveness: (1) bge-base-en-v1.5 (3x compute, +1.57 MTEB retrieval points; vstash measured NDCG@10 .702 vs .658, +6.7% relative, at 9.1 vs 4.0 ms end-to-end search); (2) nomic-embed-text-v1.5 with a 256-d matryoshka cut (61.04 vs 62.28 overall, 1.5x cheaper scans than 384-d is not an advantage over bge-small, so only worth it for the 8192 context and Apache license); (3) Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B or EmbeddingGemma only off the hot path (about 10x to 18x the parameters of bge-small; no CPU latency source was found). The 1.5B Stella V5 was no better than 110M Contriever at session granularity on LongMemEval (R@5 .720 vs .723), so model size matters less than record granularity and keys. Verify two embedder details in src/memory/embedder-bge-small.ts: whether the query instruction prefix is applied to short pre-turn queries, and that thresholds (cosine 0.75 topic shift, 0.85 dedup) are calibrated, since BGE v1.5 similarities are compressed. ClawCode already has dual-write v1 to v2 migration machinery, which lowers the cost of a v3 model switch. Check licenses: Jina v5 is non-commercial; Gemma has its own license; bge, nomic, Qwen3, Granite are permissive. Sources: https://huggingface.co/BAAI/bge-small-en-v1.5, https://huggingface.co/BAAI/bge-base-en-v1.5, https://huggingface.co/thenlper/gte-small, https://huggingface.co/Snowflake/snowflake-arctic-embed-s, https://huggingface.co/nomic-ai/nomic-embed-text-v1.5, https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B
- **sqlite-vec: brute force vs ANN, limits, and quantization**: ClawCode's vector index. vec0 virtual tables do exact (brute-force) KNN over float32, int8 or bit vectors; ClawCode has v0.1.9 installed (stable, pre-v1 warning in the README). After a long hiatus the project restarted in March 2026 (v0.1.7 on 2026-03-17 with real DELETE support), and v0.1.10-alpha.1 to alpha.4 (2026-03-31 to 2026-05-18) add the first ANN-style indexes. Memory model: Limits read from sqlite-vec.c: max vector dimensions 8192, max k per KNN query 4096, chunk_size default 1024 (max 4096, multiple of 8), up to 16 vector columns, 16 metadata columns, 4 partition keys, 16 auxiliary columns; metadata columns support =, !=, >, >=, <, <= (no IS NULL, LIKE, GLOB, REGEXP) and a KNN plan accepts an optional rowid IN (...) constraint; partition keys should hold hundreds of vectors each. Distance metric for vec0 defaults to L2; cosine is set per column (distance_metric=cosine). int8 distance runs on raw int8 values with no scale header, so a shared fixed range must be used (ClawCode maps [-1, 1] to int8). Alpha ANN indexes (v0.1.10): rescore (quantize to int8 or bit, oversample k*N coarse candidates, rescore with full vectors from a key-value table), IVF (experimental, needs a build flag and kmeans training, nprobe/nlist, nlist ~ sqrt(N)), DiskANN (Vamana graph with compressed neighbours, no training but very slow inserts and expensive DELETEs in alpha.2). Rescore is still brute force and grows linearly. Applicable to ClawCode: At ClawCode's per-agent sizes (about 30K vectors, 11 agents) brute force is a non-problem: about 11 ms for float32 and 8 ms for int8 per query on this machine; the embedding of the query and any reranker dominate. Do not adopt ANN until a store passes roughly 1M vectors or a latency budget under about 50 ms is violated; at that point use the stable rescore index (bit, oversample 8) rather than IVF or DiskANN. Specific notes: (1) int8 only bought a 1.3x speedup over float32 here, so its main value is 4x less disk and RAM; (2) Hugging Face measured int8 retention of 90.8% (all-MiniLM-L6-v2) to 100% (Cohere v3), so the internal '<2% recall loss' claim for bge-small should be re-verified on real vectors; with unit-norm 384-d vectors the RMS component is about 0.051, so on a [-1, 1] int8 range typical components use only about 6 to 19 of 127 levels (my arithmetic; a tighter shared range such as +/-0.25 with clamping is worth testing, inference not from a source); (3) k is capped at 4096 and the pre-turn pools (20 to 40) are far below it; (4) use the rowid IN (...) constraint to restrict KNN to a candidate subset such as one agent scope or a time window; (5) pin the extension version because alpha releases change shadow-table formats. Sources: https://github.com/asg017/sqlite-vec, https://github.com/asg017/sqlite-vec/releases, https://raw.githubusercontent.com/asg017/sqlite-vec/main/sqlite-vec.c, https://github.com/asg017/sqlite-vec/pull/276, https://github.com/asg017/sqlite-vec/pull/277, https://github.com/asg017/sqlite-vec/pull/278
- **Production agent-memory retrieval stacks: Hindsight TEMPR, Zep/Graphiti, Mem0, and LongMemEval design findings**: How the systems that publish benchmark numbers actually retrieve: multi-channel candidate generation, rank fusion, optional reranking, token-budget packing, and a temporal channel. Memory model: Hindsight TEMPR (arXiv 2512.12818, Dec 2025): four parallel channels (semantic cosine on pgvector HNSW, BM25 on a GIN index, graph spreading activation seeded by top semantic hits, and a temporal channel only when the query has a time constraint), fused with RRF (k=60 given as an example), then cross-encoder/ms-marco-MiniLM-L-6-v2 rerank with temporal metadata in the input, then greedy packing to a caller token budget. Zep/Graphiti (arXiv 2501.13956): cosine, Okapi BM25 (Neo4j Lucene) and BFS over the graph; rerankers RRF, MMR, episode-mentions, node distance, and an LLM cross-encoder; BGE-m3 embeddings (1024-d). Mem0 (2025 paper): LLM extraction with ADD/UPDATE/DELETE/NOOP against the top s=10 similar memories. Mem0 April 2026 algorithm: single-pass ADD-only extraction; retrieval fuses semantic, BM25 with verb lemmatization, entity matching and a temporal-intent score by 'rank scoring' (formula undisclosed), top-K default 200, about 6,700 to 7,000 tokens per query. LongMemEval (arXiv 2410.10813): flat retrieval, round-level values, keys = value + extracted facts, query = question + time range, Chain-of-Note reading. Applicable to ClawCode: What is portable: (1) keep BM25 and vector as separate always-on channels plus a conditional temporal channel driven by a deterministic date parser; (2) pack by token budget, not by fixed k; (3) strong readers (Claude) benefit from larger pools: Anthropic found top-20 better than top-10 and top-5, while LongMemEval notes a weak 8B reader degrades past about 3K retrieved tokens and GPT-4o keeps improving past 20K; ClawCode's 5 chunks / 1500 tokens pre-turn budget is conservative and the lazy search tool could return 15 to 20 short snippets when the agent asks; (4) an entity channel (proper nouns, ticket IDs, file paths) via the trigram FTS table is a cheap analogue of Mem0's entity boost. Do not copy graph traversal: it needs LLM entity extraction on every write and no source isolates its gain over hybrid retrieval at this scale. Treat vendor leaderboards as marketing. Sources: https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2512.12818v1, https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2501.13956, https://arxiv.org/html/2504.19413, https://mem0.ai/blog/mem0-the-token-efficient-memory-algorithm, https://docs.mem0.ai/core-concepts/memory-evaluation, https://arxiv.org/html/2604.22085

#### Evaluating agent long-term memory (LongMemEval, LoCoMo, MemBench, BEAM, planted-fact regression suites) and memory for uploaded images and files (captions, OCR, CLIP-family and ColPali-style embeddings, page/file summaries, provenance links), for a small single-machine harness like ClawCode. Researched 2026-10-25; sources from 2024 are marked older. Local code read: src/memory/eval/recall-eval.ts, scripts/memory-recall-eval.ts, src/documents/store.ts, src/document-ingest/*, src/manager/auto-ingest-handler.ts, src/vision/*, src/memory/store.ts.

- Decompose every memory failure into write, retrieve and use, and score each stage separately, with the write stage first. Evidence: LongMemEval frames memory as indexing, retrieval and reading with separate recall and QA metrics. AMemGym classifies each miss as write, read or utilization failure (mean rates for RAG: write .377, read .172, utilization .067). Ground Truth First found questions whose underlying fact was written weakly missed 24.2% of the time vs 1.6% for clean writes, and 79% of all QA misses had a weak-written fact. In ClawCode terms: audit what landed in memories and conversation_turns before blaming retrieval. Source: https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.10813 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2603.01966 ; https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.21962
- Generate ground truth first, from fictional entities, and render text from it; never extract answer keys from generated dialogue and never use real-world facts. Evidence: Penfield's audit found 6.4% of LoCoMo answers wrong (99 of 1,540), capping a perfect system near 93.6%. MemGym measured frontier models scoring about 0.70 to 0.85 from pretraining alone on unfictionalized memory questions, near zero after fictionalization. Ground Truth First instantiates questions mechanically from a seeded fact script so gold is correct by construction, and validates answerability with an open-book oracle (215 of 220 passed). Source: https://penfieldlabs.substack.com/p/we-audited-locomo-64-of-the-answer ; https://arxiv.org/html/2605.20833 ; https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.21962
- Always run floors and ceilings: no-memory control, equal-budget recency window, full history, and oracle evidence; report paired deltas with the reasoner held fixed. Evidence: Ground Truth First: no-memory 15.2%, recency window at the same token budget 73.0%, hybrid memory 96.8%, full history 97.9% (a tie, because the synthetic history fit in context). MemGym defines memory gain as a paired difference under the same reasoner and warns coding tasks show near-zero gain because state lives in files. Mastra's gpt-4o full-context baseline was 60.2% vs oracle 82.4%. Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.21962 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2605.20833 ; https://mastra.ai/research/observational-memory
- Test at more than one history length and tenure, because rankings invert and small histories cannot separate memory from reading everything. Evidence: Ground Truth First: curated-map memory led at 3 weeks but its recall of evicted content fell 96.3% to 72.2% by week 9, while the graph rose 94.4% to 100%; plain vector retrieval fell 88.9% to 70.4% because newer similar records bury old ones. BEAM: retrieval slightly hurts at 100K tokens but removing it costs 8.5% at 10M. LoCoMo (about 9K to 26K tokens) and LongMemEval-S (about 115K) fit in current windows per Zep and Penfield. Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.21962 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2510.27246v1 ; https://penfieldlabs.substack.com/p/we-audited-locomo-64-of-the-answer
- Make updates, supersession, abstention and forgetting first-class question types, and run them after consolidation passes. Evidence: MemoryAgentBench: every method scored at most 7% on multi-hop FactConsolidation, the weakest of four competencies. LongMemEval has dedicated knowledge-update and abstention types (30 false-premise questions). Zep notes LoCoMo has no knowledge-update questions. Ground Truth First found curated eviction lowers old-content recall, so consolidation needs its own probes. Source: https://arxiv.org/html/2507.05257v2 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2410.10813v2 ; https://www.getzep.com/blog/lies-damn-lies-statistics-is-mem0-really-sota-in-agent-memory/ ; https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.21962
- Include semantic-gap probes where the cue and the later query share almost no words. Evidence: LoCoMo-Plus filters pairs by BM25 and MPNet similarity so lexical or embedding match cannot solve them; memory systems scored 14.9 to 17.2 (Mem0 15.80) and RAG 12 to 16, below full-context GPT-4o at 21.05, while Gemini-2.5-Pro fell from 71.78 on LoCoMo to 26.06. Source: https://arxiv.org/html/2602.10715
- Treat the judge as part of the benchmark: version the rubric, use a different model family for corpus generation, spot-check leniency adversarially, replicate, and re-judge headline numbers with a second family. Evidence: LoCoMo's gpt-4o-mini judge accepted 62.81% of wrong but on-topic answers (Penfield). Ground Truth First used three stochastic replicates because single runs masked whole defect classes, versioned its judge rubric like code, and found some conclusions flip under a cross-family judge (full history vs layered memory at week 9); judging consumed more LLM calls than answering (15.0k vs 12.7k). LongMemEval reports over 97% judge agreement with experts using per-type prompts. Source: https://penfieldlabs.substack.com/p/we-audited-locomo-64-of-the-answer ; https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.21962 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2410.10813v2
- Ingest a history once and query it many times; evaluate agents on-policy rather than replaying transcripts when the agent decides what to save. Evidence: MemoryAgentBench's inject-once design (5 contexts for 300 LongMemEval questions). AMemGym found memory-agent rankings shift between off-policy and on-policy runs, with a smaller effect for native LLMs. Source: https://arxiv.org/html/2507.05257v2 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2603.01966
- Do not compare vendor scores unless reader, judge, retrieval budget and benchmark version match; report tokens and latency next to accuracy; distinguish Recall@k from answer accuracy. Evidence: Mem0's own 2026 blog says none of the listed scores used the same model stack, judge or retrieval configuration and that Zep's claimed 94.7% LoCoMo conflicts with a third-party 75.1%. Zep puts Mem0 at 66.88 to 68.44 vs full context 72.90 on LoCoMo. Supermemory's 97% is Recall@20, not accuracy. MemBench reports read and write time per mechanism. Source: https://mem0.ai/blog/ai-memory-benchmarks-in-2026 ; https://www.getzep.com/blog/lies-damn-lies-statistics-is-mem0-really-sota-in-agent-memory/ ; https://supermemory.ai/research.md ; https://arxiv.org/html/2506.21605v1
- For image and file memory, index text surrogates (caption, OCR, numeric transcription of charts and tables, one-sentence page digest, per-upload summary) and fetch the original only when the question needs it. Evidence: M3Proctor: about 72 s index build vs about 43,271 s for MIRIX, about 4,591 tokens per query vs about 26,988, raw visuals used on only 26.4% of queries, best overall score 0.484. Mem-Gallery: dumping every image into context scored 0.3354, worse than text-only full memory at 0.3625, while an organized multimodal retriever (MuRAG) reached 0.6966. Source: https://arxiv.org/html/2606.07402 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2601.03515
- Keep a visual lane (or at least the original image reachable) for details that captions drop: counts, attributes, spatial relations, incidental marks. Evidence: DMV-Bench: visual retrieval 79.7% vs verbal 65.1%, image injection 79.9% vs caption injection 72.0%, and the captioner verbalized both the color and the object of the planted cue in only 16.5% of captions. MemLens attributes memory-agent errors to lossy storage-time compression of images. Source: https://arxiv.org/html/2606.27499 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2605.14906
- Link a file to the conversation round it arrived in, and return that round and its neighbors when the file is retrieved. Evidence: V-Mem ablation: all-lane unit-level retrieval 0.680, with modality routing 0.689, with matching through shared rounds 0.818, with generated anchors 0.825; round linking produced almost all of the gain. Graphiti links every derived fact to the episodes that produced it with bidirectional indices for citation. Source: https://arxiv.org/html/2608.01543 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2501.13956
- Use stable entity ids across modalities instead of free-text mentions. Evidence: M3-Agent: removing identity equivalence cut accuracy from 30.7 to 19.5 on M3-Bench-robot and from 48.9 to 39.7 on web; removing semantic memory cut it to 13.6 and 29.7. Source: https://arxiv.org/html/2508.09736v1
- Write the index text for retrieval and give each chunk its document context. Evidence: Anthropic Contextual Retrieval cut top-20 retrieval failures by 35% (contextual embeddings), 49% (plus contextual BM25) and 67% (plus reranking). MMDocIR reports text retrievers using VLM-generated text significantly beat those using OCR text. Jason Liu's reported tip is to tell the summarizer that its output will be embedded for search (anecdote: recall at 5 from 28% with CLIP to 75% with LLM summaries). Source: https://www.anthropic.com/news/contextual-retrieval ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.08828 ; https://simonwillison.net/2025/Sep/6/jason-liu/
- Fuse text and image evidence rather than choosing one; neither pure-caption nor pure-embedding retrieval wins consistently. Evidence: UniDoc-Bench: text-image fusion consistently beat unimodal and joint-embedding retrieval, and 'current multimodal embeddings remain inadequate'. ViDoRe v3: hybrid context 54.7% vs best text 52.1% vs best visual 54.5% on hard queries, and text reranking added 13.2 NDCG@10 points vs 0.2 for visual reranking. The caption-vs-embedding comparisons conflict (13 mAP@5 for direct embeddings on 40 QA pairs vs Jason Liu's 28% to 75% for summaries). Source: https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.03663 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2601.08620v1 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.16654 ; https://simonwillison.net/2025/Sep/6/jason-liu/
- At single-machine scale prefer brute force, small CPU models and pooling over graph databases, big visual retrievers and approximate-index machinery. Evidence: ColModernVBERT (250M params) is 0.6 nDCG@5 below ColPali (2.92B) with 20 ms vs 175 ms CPU query latency. Token pooling factor 3 removes 66.7% of page vectors at 97.8% retention. M3DocRAG needed IVFFlat only at 41,005 pages (21 s exact vs 1.8 s). Anthropic says to skip RAG for knowledge bases under about 200,000 tokens. BEAM and MemBench show retrieval pays off as memory grows past about 100K tokens. Source: https://arxiv.org/html/2510.01149v2 ; https://github.com/illuin-tech/colpali ; https://arxiv.org/html/2411.04952 ; https://www.anthropic.com/news/contextual-retrieval ; https://arxiv.org/html/2510.27246v1
- Carry provenance and trust class through every representation of ingested content, and test it with injection probes. Evidence: Ground Truth First: injection resistance tracked whether provenance boundaries survived representation; a provenance-typed graph and the raw transcript blocked all 14 planted probes, while a flat-text wiki reintroduced an injection surface. Graphiti keeps raw episodes as the non-lossy source of every fact. Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.21962 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2501.13956

Anti-patterns: Headlining LoCoMo or any single vendor-run score. The answer key has about 6.4% errors, the judge accepts up to 62.81% of wrong but topical answers, conversations fit in context, and full context beat Mem0 in Mem0's own setup (sources: Penfield audit, Zep blog).; Reporting Recall@k as if it were answer accuracy (Supermemory's 97% is Recall@20), or comparing numbers produced with different readers, judges and retrieval budgets.; Building the benchmark conversation-first and extracting answers from the generated text; using real-world facts that the model already knows (MemGym: 0.70 to 0.85 from pretraining alone).; Evaluating only short horizons or only small stores. Rankings invert with tenure (Ground Truth First) and retrieval can hurt at 100K tokens yet be decisive at 10M (BEAM).; One run, one judge, no replicates, and no no-memory or full-history control. Single runs masked entire defect classes in Ground Truth First; its full-history baseline tied the best memory system on short histories.; Replaying a fixed transcript to test an agent that chooses what to save (off-policy). AMemGym shows the ranking of memory agents changes on-policy.; Testing retrieval over static files only. That never checks what the agent wrote, whether stale facts were superseded, or whether eviction, decay and dedup destroyed old facts (weak writes missed 24.2% of questions; curated eviction lost 96.3% to 72.2% on old content).; Naively composing two curated stores or two memory layers without an eval. Ground Truth First's deterministic hybrid scored 89.5%, below both parents.; Putting every uploaded image into context, or building heavyweight multi-agent multimodal memory. Full Memory Multimodal scored below text-only full memory in Mem-Gallery, and MIRIX took about 43,271 s to index vs 72 s for M3Proctor.; Trusting captions alone for details that matter (counts, colors, small marks, spatial layout). Captions captured both color and object of a planted cue in only 16.5% of cases (DMV-Bench), and MemLens blames lossy storage-time compression.; Applying CLIP-family embeddings to text-heavy screenshots and documents, truncating SigLIP 2 queries beyond its 64-token text limit, or captioning both sides of an image-to-image match (mRAG: Recall@1 down 4.68 and 5.24 points with captions on both sides).; Chunking concatenated OCR text with no page markers, no file title and no summary (what ClawCode does today: pages are joined, heading is null, chunks are 500 words-heuristic tokens), so chunks lose their page and document context (Contextual Retrieval: 35% to 67% fewer retrieval failures when context is restored).; Gating OCR quality on Tesseract's own confidence score without measuring recall on scanned pages; the evidence that VLM text indexes better than OCR text (MMDocIR, ViDoRe v1 OCR pipelines at 66.1 vs 81.3) says to measure, not to trust the gate.; Adopting ColPali-class visual retrieval for text-dominant PDFs, or storing unpooled fp16 patch vectors for a large corpus (about 257.5 KB per page). Text reranking beat visual reranking by 13.2 vs 0.2 points in ViDoRe v3 and pooling hurts text-dense pages.; Treating file contents and third-party messages as trusted memory. Attachments are attacker-controlled; keep provenance and trust class on every derived chunk and fact, and include injection probes in the suite.

Systems reviewed:

- **LongMemEval (ICLR 2025; arXiv Oct 2024, older source but still the reference design)**: Benchmark of 500 hand-written questions embedded in scalable user-assistant chat histories. Seven question types: single-session-user, single-session-assistant, single-session-preference, multi-session, knowledge-update, temporal-reasoning, and abstention (30 false-premise variants, ids end in _abs). Variants: oracle (evidence sessions only), S (about 115k tokens, roughly 40 to 50 sessions) and M (about 500 sessions, about 1.5M tokens). A raw-material release lets you build custom histories with your own questions and evidence sessions and a sampler that pads with filler sessions. Data was cleaned again in 2025/09. Memory model: Not a memory system. It defines a three-stage view of any memory: indexing (what is the key, what is the value), retrieval, reading. Evidence is annotated at turn level (has_answer flags) and session level (answer_session_ids), so retrieval (Recall@k, NDCG@k with flat BM25, Contriever, Stella, GTE retrievers) is scored apart from end-to-end QA. QA is judged by GPT-4o with a separate expert-written prompt per question type (paper reports over 97% agreement with human experts). Every question has a question_date and sessions have timestamps. Optimizations studied: round (turn) level values instead of whole sessions, fact-augmented key expansion (key = value plus extracted user facts), time-aware query expansion (LLM extracts a time range, search is narrowed), and chain-of-note reading with JSON-structured context. Applicable to ClawCode: 1) Use the seven types as the case-type checklist for a ClawCode fixture (knowledge-update, temporal, abstention and multi-session are the ones the current fixture cannot express). 2) src/memory/eval/recall-eval.ts scores Recall@K and MRR against heading or memory-key substrings; add evidence turn ids as an expect target (memories.source_turn_ids and conversation_turns already exist) so conversational recall gets turn-level Recall@k like LongMemEval. 3) Keep the retrieval-only score deterministic for CI (the repo already uses a hash embedder) and run the judged end-to-end pass nightly. 4) Build a stratified subset of S or custom histories (my suggestion: 6 to 10 questions per type); ingesting about 115k tokens per question through the write path (session summarizer, consolidation) is the main cost, so reuse one ingested history for many questions. 5) Test the paper's three levers in ClawCode's own retrieval: turn-level chunks, fact-extracted keys pointing at turns, and an LLM-derived time range instead of the fixed 14-day pre-turn window. Cost: a few days of fixture work plus judge calls (Claude Sonnet with a versioned per-type rubric); no new infrastructure. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.10813, https://arxiv.org/html/2410.10813v2, https://github.com/xiaowu0162/LongMemEval, https://penfieldlabs.substack.com/p/we-audited-locomo-64-of-the-answer
- **LoCoMo (ACL 2024, older) and the 2025-26 critiques of it**: Long-conversation QA benchmark: LLM agents grounded in personas and temporal event graphs, human-edited, conversations averaging about 300 turns and about 9K tokens (Zep puts them at 16K to 26K) over up to 35 sessions, with question categories (single-hop, multi-hop, temporal, open-domain, adversarial) plus image-sharing in dialogues. Still the most cited vendor benchmark. Memory model: Not a memory system. Scored with an LLM judge or F1 depending on the paper. Image turns are represented by BLIP-generated captions, which Zep notes sometimes lack the information the question needs. Applicable to ClawCode: Do not use it as the headline metric. Use it only as a smoke test, and borrow two ideas: image-grounded questions whose answer is missing from the caption (a caption-fidelity probe for ClawCode's vision_json summaries), and Letta's baseline of letting the agent grep and search a plain file store (74.0% with gpt-4o-mini, Aug 2025), which ClawCode should run as a no-frills baseline against its own tiers. Cost: negligible if done as an extra baseline in the same harness. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.17753, https://penfieldlabs.substack.com/p/we-audited-locomo-64-of-the-answer, https://www.getzep.com/blog/lies-damn-lies-statistics-is-mem0-really-sota-in-agent-memory/, https://www.letta.com/blog/benchmarking-ai-agent-memory, https://mem0.ai/blog/ai-memory-benchmarks-in-2026
- **LoCoMo-Plus (Feb 2026): implicit cue-trigger memory**: Adds 'cognitive memory' tests to LoCoMo: a cue (a short dialogue that implicitly reveals a user's state, goal, value or causal context) is inserted, and much later a trigger query depends on it while sharing little surface wording. Four types: causal, state, goal, value. There is no single gold string; a response is valid if it respects the latent constraint. Memory model: Construction pipeline: LLM drafts cues, humans keep only persistent and non-trivial ones, an LLM writes trigger queries with a time-gap label, BM25 and MPNet similarity filters remove pairs with high lexical or semantic overlap, humans confirm the pair really needs the cue. Judge sees the query without task-type disclosure and scores constraint consistency (three-level labels for factual items, binary for temporal, adversarial and cognitive). Applicable to ClawCode: ClawCode retrieval is hybrid BM25 plus bge-small vectors fused by RRF, which is exactly the family that fails when cue and trigger share no words. Add 10 to 20 cue-trigger pairs per agent persona to the fixture (for example a stated constraint or goal followed weeks later by an unrelated-sounding request), run them with and without the dreaming pass, and see whether consolidation (reflections, summaries) bridges the gap. Cost: authoring plus an LLM judge; copy the lexical-overlap filter so the pairs are really hard. Sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2602.10715, https://arxiv.org/pdf/2602.10715
- **MemBench (ACL Findings 2025)**: Benchmark of memory mechanisms across effectiveness, efficiency and capacity, built on the MemSim framework. Two memory levels (factual: stated explicitly; reflective: preferences inferred from repeated expression) and two scenarios (participation: user-agent dialogue; observation: agent passively records user messages). All questions are multiple choice. Memory model: Metrics: accuracy; Recall@10 for retrieval mechanisms against key evidence dialogues; capacity (does accuracy collapse as memory tokens grow, tested at about 10k and about 100k tokens); efficiency as read time and write time per operation. Mechanisms implemented in MemEngine with Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and multilingual-e5-small: FullMemory, RecentMemory, RetrievalMemory, GenerativeAgent, MemoryBank, MemGPT, SCMemory. Applicable to ClawCode: Adopt the reporting shape, not the dataset: every ClawCode eval run should print accuracy, Recall@k, read ms and write ms, at two store sizes. src/benchmarks already measures latency percentiles for turns and src/memory/eval measures Recall@K; joining them into one report (and adding a 10x larger synthetic store) gives the capacity curve. Reflective-memory probes (inferred preferences) are the test for the dreaming pass. Cost: low; mostly report plumbing. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21605, https://arxiv.org/html/2506.21605v1
- **MemoryAgentBench (Hu, Wang, McAuley; arXiv Jul 2025, ICLR 2026)**: Evaluates memory agents on four competencies: accurate retrieval, test-time learning, long-range understanding, and selective forgetting (called conflict resolution in v1). Adds EventQA and FactConsolidation (single-hop and multi-hop) datasets and reformats existing long-context sets into incremental multi-turn form. Design principle: inject once, query many, because injecting 1M tokens for one question is wasteful (LongMemEval S* uses 5 contexts for 300 questions). Memory model: Compares long-context models (GPT-4o, GPT-4.1-mini, Gemini-2.0-Flash, Claude-3.7-Sonnet), BM25 and embedding RAG, structure-augmented RAG (RAPTOR, GraphRAG, MemoRAG, HippoRAG-v2, Mem0, Cognee, Zep) and agentic memory (Self-RAG, MemGPT, MIRIX). Applicable to ClawCode: Two uses. (1) FactConsolidation is the template for a supersession test: plant a fact, later plant a contradicting one, probe after the dreaming and dedup passes (ClawCode has decay.ts, dedup.ts, consolidation.ts and tiers, all of which can silently drop or keep the stale fact). (2) The inject-once pattern: build one 3-week synthetic agent store and run 100+ probes against it, so evaluation cost is dominated by probes, not ingestion. Cost: a fixture generator and a time-travel hook (fake clock) for decay and consolidation. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.05257, https://arxiv.org/html/2507.05257v2
- **BEAM and LIGHT (Tavakoli et al., ICLR 2026; arXiv Oct 2025)**: BEAM: 100 generated conversations of 100K, 500K, 1M and 10M tokens with 2,000 validated probing questions over ten abilities (abstention, contradiction resolution, event ordering, information extraction, instruction following, knowledge update, multi-hop reasoning, preference following, summarization, temporal reasoning). Conversations are built from plans (domain, themes, user profile, relationship graph, timeline); questions are LLM-proposed then human-validated; scoring is rubric 'nuggets' (0, 0.5, 1) with Kendall tau-b for ordering. LIGHT is the authors' memory: episodic retrieval over extracted key-value pairs and summaries, a working-memory window, and a scratchpad of salient facts. Memory model: LIGHT combines top-k episodic retrieval, recent turns, and a running scratchpad filtered per question. Ablation at 10M tokens: removing episodic retrieval costs 8.5%, noise filtering 8.3%, working memory 5.7%, scratchpad 3.7%; at 100K retrieval slightly hurts because the scratchpad already covers most needs. Applicable to ClawCode: Most useful as a scale argument: ClawCode agents accumulate months of turns per agent, which is the 1M to 10M regime where plain long-context fails and retrieval matters; at small sizes (under about 100K tokens) a stable scratchpad (ClawCode's always-loaded tier) is enough. So the suite should include at least two scales and report which tier answered. Rubric-nugget scoring is also a cheaper-than-exact-match way to grade multi-fact answers. Cost: synthetic long histories need a generator; the BEAM data is on Hugging Face (BEAM-10M). Sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2510.27246v1, https://arxiv.org/pdf/2510.27246, https://mem0.ai/blog/ai-memory-benchmarks-in-2026
- **AMA-Bench (arXiv Feb 2026): memory for agent trajectories, not chat**: Argues dialogue-centric benchmarks miss real agent memory, which is trajectories of states, actions, observations and tool outputs. Real trajectories (about 6K to 289K tokens, up to nearly 1M) with 12 annotator-written questions each, plus synthetic trajectories at 8K to 128K tokens with programmatic gold QA. Questions are anchored to 'needles', the minimal turns needed to answer. Four capabilities: recall, causal inference, state updating, state abstraction. Memory model: Proposed AMA-Agent: an LLM abstracts each (observation, action, observation) triple into state nodes in a causality graph (directed causal edges, undirected association edges, embeddings); retrieval takes top-5 nodes by similarity, self-checks sufficiency, and falls back to depth-limited graph traversal (about 10% of cases) or a keyword-search script (about 23.5%). Applicable to ClawCode: ClawCode agents work on tasks with long tool outputs (src/memory/tool-output-prune.ts, subagent-capture.ts), so a chat-only suite misses their main failure mode: a needle buried in a tool result that was pruned. Add 'needle in tool output' cases: plant a value in a long tool result, let pruning and compaction run, then ask for it ten turns later. The keyword-search fallback result supports keeping FTS5 as a first-class tool the agent can call, not only a pre-turn ranker. Cost: low for the needle cases; the graph is not needed. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.22769, https://arxiv.org/html/2602.22769
- **Ground Truth First (Spencer, arXiv Jul 2026, single-author preprint): a longitudinal memory eval instrument**: A seeded generator emits a fictional user's fact timeline first (each fact with a validity interval, volatility class from permanent to ephemeral, and channel: chat, or email sent vs received), an LLM from a different model family renders it into chat and email, a fidelity verifier checks every planted fact appears, and questions are instantiated mechanically from the script so gold answers are correct by construction (an oracle open-book audit then validated 215 of 220 questions). 383 validated questions over 15 types: single-fact, inference-only, episodic, temporal, multi-session, knowledge-update (current and past), preference, expired-state, conflict-resolution, work-memory, abstention, abstention-false-premise, injection-probe; as-of-date question sets at weeks 3, 6, 9. Memory model: Compares a budgeted curated Markdown map (distill, cartograph, evict under budget), plain vector retrieval, a provenance-typed graph, a hybrid of graph and map, and a no-memory control, all with the same answerer (claude-haiku-4-5), a versioned judge (claude-sonnet-5), three stochastic replicates, and cross-family re-judging (DeepSeek v3.2). Also audits the write stage: each stored memory is scored against the script's atomic fact checklist (correct, distorted, absent; hallucination, distortion, misattribution). Applicable to ClawCode: This is the closest template for ClawCode's regression suite. Copy: (1) ground truth before text, with fictional entities so the model cannot answer from pretraining; (2) validity intervals and as-of questions to test knowledge-update and decay; (3) a no-memory control, a full-history baseline and a token-matched recency window as floors and ceilings; (4) three replicates and a second judge from another family for any headline number; (5) a write-stage audit of what the agent actually saved, scored against the checklist, separate from QA; (6) injection probes where the planted instruction arrives inside an uploaded file or a third-party message (ClawCode ingests attacker-controlled Discord attachments). Simulate weeks by running the dreaming pass and decay against a fake clock and probe at checkpoints. Cost: a generator and renderer (one day to a week), about 0.002 USD per question of input tokens for the answerer at list price per the paper, but judging used more LLM calls than answering (15.0k vs 12.7k of 34,076 calls in the original study). Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21962, https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.21962
- **AMemGym (ICLR 2026) and MemGym (May 2026): on-policy and memory-isolated evaluation**: AMemGym: interactive, on-policy benchmark. Profiles sampled from 100K Nemotron personas, predefined state-evolution trajectories, an LLM-simulated user (gpt-4.1) that exposes the states through pre-generated opening utterances; base config 20 users, 200 questions, 10 evolution periods, about 943 sessions, needs 128K context. MemGym: memory benchmark over agent regimes (tau2-bench tool dialogue, deep-research search, coding with SWE-Gym and CodeQA, WebArena-Infinity computer use) with memory-isolated scoring (paired baseline vs memory runs with the same reasoner) and MemRM, a Qwen3-1.7B QLoRA reward model that scores memory compression quality cheaply. Memory model: AMemGym diagnoses each miss as a write failure (state never recorded), read failure (recorded but not retrieved) or utilization failure (known but answered wrong) and reports a normalized memory score (S_overall minus S_random) over (S_upper_bound minus S_random), where the upper bound is given the ground-truth states. MemGym defines 'illusory memory pressure': settings that look memory-intensive but are solvable from pretraining or by re-reading the repository. Applicable to ClawCode: ClawCode agents decide what to save with their own tools, so an off-policy transcript replay misjudges them; AMemGym shows rankings shift between off-policy and on-policy. Adopt: scripted user utterances (cheaper than a simulated user, and what AMemGym uses at the start of each session) fed to a real agent through the Discord-free IPC path used by the bench harness (src/benchmarks/harness.ts already spawns an isolated daemon with a temp HOME), then diagnose misses as write, read or utilization by checking what landed in memories and conversation_turns. Report the normalized score with the ground-truth-in-prompt run as the upper bound. Cost: moderate (needs live agent runs, so tokens), but the isolated-daemon harness already exists. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.01966, https://arxiv.org/html/2603.01966, https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20833, https://arxiv.org/html/2605.20833
- **Letta Leaderboard (May 2025) and Letta's agentic-memory evaluation stance**: Benchmark of how well models manage agentic memory through tools rather than how good a retriever is: read, write and update tasks over core memory (in-context blocks) and archival memory (external). Synthetic fictional facts and questions so the agent has no other source. Read tasks place facts in memory; write tasks have a simulated user chat about the facts and the agent must save them before history is removed; update tasks introduce a contradicting fact after the block is written. Memory model: Scoring by GPT-4.1 against ground truth in SimpleQA style, with a penalty for extraneous memory operations. Letta's August 2025 post argues memory quality depends on how the agent uses tools (its gpt-4o-mini agent with grep, open and semantic file search got 74.0% on LoCoMo) more than on the retrieval mechanism, and recommends dynamic, long-running task evals such as Terminal-Bench over static retrieval tests. Applicable to ClawCode: ClawCode exposes four memory tools (clawcode_memory_search, recall, edit, archive). Add three eval tracks with the same fictional-fact trick: read (facts present, does the agent call search), write (facts only in chat, then clear the session, does a later session recall them), update (contradiction arrives, is the stale memory edited or superseded). Count tool calls and penalize unnecessary ones; Letta found Haiku-class models overuse memory operations, which hurt their core-memory score. Cost: low to moderate; fixtures plus a tool-call counter from existing logs. Sources: https://www.letta.com/blog/letta-leaderboard, https://www.letta.com/blog/benchmarking-ai-agent-memory
- **Practitioner planted-fact regression harness (CallSphere blog; low-authority but consistent with the papers)**: Describes a CI-style eval where each case is a scripted conversation of about 8 to 14 turns with hidden per-turn annotations: plants (facts introduced), probes (facts the turn should trigger recall of) and contradicts (facts superseded), plus an expected_facts map of canonical subject:predicate keys to values and a stable user_id per namespace. Memory model: Guidance: plant on turn 1, probe no earlier than turn 4, put 2 to 4 filler turns between plant and probe, plant deliberate contradictions (old employer then new employer), reuse user ids across cases to catch cross-user leakage. Metrics: recall@k of probed facts, precision of asserted facts, contradiction rate, staleness handling; tighter thresholds on precision and contradictions than on recall. Fact extraction and 'is the probe satisfied' are small LLM-judge calls because pattern matching on free text is brittle. Run the full suite as a CI gate on PRs that touch memory code, prompts or schemas, plus a small held-out nightly subset to catch silent model-snapshot drift. Applicable to ClawCode: Direct fit for the fixture schema: extend src/memory/__tests__/fixtures/recall-eval.json with turns[] carrying plants, probes and contradicts, and keep expected_facts as the key space so the same fixture drives the retrieval-only CI test and the judged nightly run. The cross-user leakage case matters because ClawCode runs many per-agent SQLite stores plus shared-facts-store.ts. A nightly held-out subset also catches Claude model-snapshot drift under the Agent SDK. Cost: low. Sources: https://callsphere.ai/blog/evaluating-agent-memory-recall-precision-pipeline
- **Supermemory MemoryBench (open source, MIT) and Supermemory's published claims**: Framework to run LoCoMo, LongMemEval and ConvoMem against memory providers (supermemory, mem0, zep, later filesystem-memory and hybrid-RAG providers) through staged, checkpointed pipelines: ingest, index, search, answer, evaluate, report. Judge model is swappable (GPT-4o, Claude, Gemini). Reports a composite MemScore of accuracy, latency and token use. Includes a Claude skill to scaffold provider adapters. Memory model: Provider adapter interface per memory layer; each stage resumable. Supermemory's own research page claims 97% overall Recall@20 on LongMemEval-S with aggregation (a retrieval metric, not answer accuracy, and self-published). Applicable to ClawCode: Write one ClawCode provider adapter (ingest turns into a temp agent store, search through clawcode_memory_search, answer through a pinned Claude model) and a second 'filesystem plus grep' provider as the baseline. That gives LongMemEval and LoCoMo numbers on identical reader and judge settings without building a runner. Cost: about a day for the adapter; it is a dependency on a vendor-maintained tool, so pin a commit. Sources: https://supermemory.ai/docs/memorybench/overview.md, https://supermemory.ai/research.md, https://supermemory.ai/changelog/2026-02-25-improvements.md
- **Mastra Observational Memory (vendor report; runner open source)**: Replaces raw message history with a dense dated text log of observations. An Observer agent turns raw messages into dated, prioritized observations; a Reflector merges related items and drops superseded ones. Triggers are token-count thresholds. Observations sit at the start of the context and active messages at the end, with no per-turn retrieval, so the prefix is prompt-cacheable. Memory model: Write: Observer and Reflector run in the background on thresholds. Each observation carries up to three dates (created, date mentioned in content, computed relative date). Read: the whole observation log is in context. Forgetting: the Reflector condenses and removes superseded entries. About 6x text compression on LongMemEval, average context about 30k tokens, and a claimed 4x to 10x token cost cut from prompt caching. Applicable to ClawCode: ClawCode sessions run on the Claude Agent SDK where a stable prefix is cacheable; ClawCode already has conversation-brief.ts, context-summary.ts and tier-manager.ts, which are the same idea in tiers. The transferable parts: three dates per observation (the mentioned-date and the resolved relative date are what make temporal questions answerable) and token-threshold triggers instead of cron-only dreaming. Cost: an extra observer LLM call per threshold crossing; moderate engineering to keep the prefix byte-stable so caching still hits. Sources: https://mastra.ai/research/observational-memory
- **Zep and Graphiti (temporal knowledge graph; arXiv Jan 2025, older)**: Memory layer whose core, Graphiti, builds a temporal knowledge graph from conversations and structured data. Episodes (raw message, text or JSON) are a non-lossy store; semantic entity nodes and fact edges are extracted from them; communities summarize clusters. Memory model: Write: LLM extraction of entities and facts from each episode, entity resolution against existing nodes, and an LLM contradiction check that sets the old edge's invalid time to the new edge's valid time (bi-temporal: created and expired in system time, valid and invalid in world time). Provenance: episodic edges link episodes to entities, and bidirectional indices link episodes and semantic edges, so any derived fact can be traced to source for citation. Read: cosine on fact embeddings, BM25 on a Lucene index, BFS from seed nodes, reranked by RRF, MMR, episode-mention frequency, node distance or a cross-encoder. Nothing is deleted; facts are invalidated. Applicable to ClawCode: ClawCode does not need a graph database, but it should copy two schema ideas. (1) Add valid_at and invalid_at (or a superseded_by pointer) to memories so knowledge-update and as-of probes have something to test, and let the dreaming pass, not the hot path, do the contradiction check. (2) Make provenance two-directional for files: memories.source_turn_ids already points memory to turns; add the same for document chunks and file-derived memories so a fact can cite 'page 7 of the Tuesday upload'. Cost: one migration and an LLM pass in consolidation. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.13956, https://arxiv.org/html/2501.13956, https://github.com/getzep/graphiti
- **Anthropic memory tool and context editing (Sep 29 2025)**: Client-side file-based memory tool for Claude (create, read, update, delete files in a persistent memory directory the developer hosts) plus context editing that clears stale tool calls and results as the window fills. Memory model: Storage is plain files under the developer's control; Claude decides what to write and read. Context editing is the forgetting mechanism for tool output. Applicable to ClawCode: ClawCode agents are Claude Agent SDK sessions, so this file-memory pattern is the natural 'dumb baseline' to benchmark against: a memory directory plus grep and read, with no embeddings. If the tiered SQLite design cannot beat it on the same fixture, the extra machinery is not earning its keep. Cost: a few hours to wire a baseline arm. Sources: https://claude.com/blog/context-management
- **Anthropic Contextual Retrieval (Sep 2024, older)**: Before embedding and BM25 indexing, an LLM writes a 50 to 100 token context for each chunk using the whole document, and that context is prepended to the chunk (contextual embeddings plus contextual BM25). Memory model: Index-time enrichment: document title, period, section and neighbor facts get baked into each chunk. Reranking over about 150 retrieved chunks, passing the top 20, adds a further gain. Anthropic also says to skip RAG and put everything in the prompt when the knowledge base is under about 200,000 tokens. Applicable to ClawCode: This is the single cheapest improvement to ClawCode's file memory. crossIngestToMemory writes memory_chunks with heading null and the 500-token chunker works on one concatenated string, so a chunk like 'Total due: 4,310.22' has no idea which file, page or sender it came from. Prepend a generated context line (file title, page number, one-sentence file digest, upload date, uploader) to the text that is embedded and FTS-indexed, and store page number in heading. Cost per Anthropic's assumptions about 1.02 USD per million document tokens with prompt caching; ClawCode already has a Claude vision pass for images, so the added call is similar. A reranker hook already exists (src/memory/reranker.ts). Measure with the recall suite before and after. Sources: https://www.anthropic.com/news/contextual-retrieval
- **M3Proctor / M3Exam (arXiv Jun 2026): text-only index of images and pages, raw visuals on demand**: Benchmark of multimodal conversational memory (query-centric, cross-modal grounding and implicit inference) plus a proposed method that keeps the index text-only and escalates to raw images only when needed. Memory model: Index build: each image gets a caption, charts and tables get numeric transcriptions, document pages get their text layer plus a one-sentence digest, and each session gets a summary chunk. A cached LLM call tags which modality a question depends on (modality bias) and re-ranks chunks with a bonus. A cost-aware cascade answers from text first and fetches the raw source only when the text answer looks unreliable or the bias is not satisfied; a fused evidence score gates each raw fetch. Applicable to ClawCode: This matches what ClawCode already half does: vision_json (kind, summary, text lines, entities, importance) is indexed as text and the hit carries source_image_path so the agent can Read the original (the contact-sheet manifest in src/vision/turn-images.ts follows the same idea). Make it explicit: (1) add a one-sentence page digest and a per-session or per-upload summary chunk, (2) transcribe charts and tables to numbers at ingest (ClawCode stores chartShape as trend text only), (3) tag each hit with a modality-dependence flag and tell the agent in the hit rendering to open the original when the question is visual or numeric. Cost: low, mostly prompt changes in the existing vision provider. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07402, https://arxiv.org/html/2606.07402
- **V-Mem (arXiv Aug 2026): dual text and image lanes linked by conversation round**: Multimodal agent memory that routes retrieval by the modality of the query and of the target evidence, and matches across modalities through the conversation round they share. Memory model: A round is one user-assistant exchange; its text spans and images are separate units that share the round id. Images are indexed twice: the caption in a text index (all-MiniLM-L6-v2 384-d plus BM25) and the raw image in a SigLIP2-base-patch16-384 lane (768-d); signals fused by RRF with k=60. Text query seeking an image: search text lane restricted to rounds with images, return the images of the top rounds (plus or minus 1 neighbor rounds); an LLM can first write a roughly 45-word hypothetical caption to use as the query. Image plus text query: extract up to 8 keywords from the query image with a vision LLM and append them. Applicable to ClawCode: The clearest evidence for how to link a file to the conversation turn it came from. ClawCode stores discord_message_id on conversation_turns and message_id on documents, but there is no first-class join and retrieval does not return the surrounding turns. Add a turn_id (or session_id, turn_index) to documents and to file-derived chunks, and on a hit return the plus or minus 1 turns around the upload so the agent sees what the user said when they attached it. The SigLIP2 image lane is optional; the round linking is not. Cost: one column, one join, one retrieval expansion; the image lane adds a roughly 0.4B-parameter model (siglip2-base card) and a second vec table. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01543, https://arxiv.org/html/2608.01543
- **DualMem and DMV-Bench (arXiv Jun 2026): where captions lose**: Interactive benchmark of visual memory: agents do chains of shopping sessions in a 1,000-variant furniture catalog; each viewed product image carries a unique incidental pixel-level cue that must be recalled later, with a text-leakage contract so the signal is in the pixels. DualMem stores parallel visual and verbal codes. Memory model: Verbal code (caption) and visual code (image embedding, SigLIP-2 or CLIP), retrieval weight alpha between them, optional image injection into the answer prompt. Ablations isolate retrieval from injection. Applicable to ClawCode: Run this as a negative test on ClawCode's own captions: pick 20 uploaded photos, plant a small distinguishing detail (a badge number, a color, a count), and ask about it a week later. Where caption-only fails, keep the image reachable (ClawCode does: archived WebP plus source_image_path) and consider a SigLIP2 lane only for images the vision step classifies as photo (the kind field already separates screenshot, photo, chart, diagram, document). Caveat: the benchmark is a worst case for captions (incidental cues in product images); screenshots and documents are a different regime where OCR text carries the signal. Cost: eval only, unless the image lane is added. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.27499, https://arxiv.org/html/2606.27499
- **Mem-Gallery (ACL 2026; arXiv Jan 2026) and MemLens (arXiv May 2026, work in progress)**: Mem-Gallery: multi-session image-and-text conversations (20 dialogues, 1,711 questions over nine categories including visual search, visual reasoning and test-time learning) evaluated over thirteen memory systems. MemLens: 789 questions across five abilities at 32K to 256K token contexts, 27 vision-language models and 7 memory agents. Memory model: Mem-Gallery separates 8 text-only systems fed GPT-5.1 captions (FIFO, NaiveRAG, Generative Agents, Reflexion, MemGPT, A-Mem, MemoryOS, Full Memory Text) from 5 multimodal systems that keep images (MuRAG, UniversalRAG, NGM, AUGUSTUS, Full Memory Multimodal). MemLens text-only agents (Mem0, MemOS, MemAgent-7B, Memory-T1) get BLIP-2 captions; multimodal agents (M3-Agent, M2A, M3C) store a fixed visual representation. Applicable to ClawCode: Two lessons. (1) Keep-image-but-organize-it beats captions, but dumping every image into context is the worst arm, so ClawCode's current policy (budgeted contact sheets, path-only overflow, original on request) is the right shape. (2) Include an image-ablation check in any image-memory fixture: MemLens found removing evidence images dropped two frontier models below 2% accuracy on the 80.4% of questions with image evidence, which proves the questions truly need the image; apply the same check so ClawCode probes cannot be answered from filename or chat text. Cost: eval design only. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.03515, https://arxiv.org/html/2601.03515, https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.14906, https://arxiv.org/html/2605.14906
- **MIRIX (arXiv Jul 2025): six typed memories with a Resource Memory for files and screenshots**: Multi-agent memory system with six components (Core, Episodic, Semantic, Procedural, Resource, Knowledge Vault) managed by specialist agents; evaluated on ScreenshotVQA (about 20,000 high-resolution screenshots per sequence) and LoCoMo. Memory model: Screenshot every 1.5 s (1 s in the dataset script), near-duplicates discarded (similarity above 0.99 to the previous frame), memory update triggered after 20 unique screenshots (about 60 s) with streaming upload; raw images are not stored for ScreenshotVQA, only extracted information in SQLite. Resource Memory rows: title, summary, resource_type (doc, markdown, pdf_text, image, voice_transcript), content. Retrieval via embedding_match, bm25_match or string_match; active retrieval pulls the top 10 from each of the six components into the system prompt tagged by source. The paper does not describe an explicit link from resource rows to episodic events. Applicable to ClawCode: Reusable bits: dedupe before captioning (ClawCode already stores content_sha256 and a 64-bit dHash and marks duplicate_of), and typed resource rows with a short summary field separate from content (documents has no summary column; add one for file-level summaries). Not reusable: six memory agents, which cost far more than they return at one-machine scale (M3Proctor reports 43,271 s index time for MIRIX vs 72 s). Cost: a summary column plus an LLM digest call per file. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.07957, https://arxiv.org/html/2507.07957
- **M3-Agent (ByteDance Seed, arXiv Aug 2025): entity-centric multimodal graph with stable identity ids**: Agent that watches and listens to a stream, builds long-term memory, and answers later by iterative search (up to five rounds, k=2) with an RL-trained controller; M3-Bench (100 robot videos plus 929 web videos) and VideoMME-long for evaluation. Memory model: Graph of nodes (id, modality text, image or audio, content, embedding, confidence weight, metadata) with undirected relation edges. Episodic entries record observed events; semantic entries hold identities, attributes, relationships and world facts. Face and voice recognizers assign persistent face_id and voice_id; memory entries refer to people by these ids rather than by descriptive text, and identity links become edges so linked nodes are grouped under one character_id at retrieval. Conflicts: the most reinforced entry accumulates weight and overrides weaker ones. Tools: search_node (by text, image or audio query) and search_clip (episodic plus semantic memory of top clips, via timestamps in metadata). Applicable to ClawCode: The transferable idea is the stable entity id shared across modalities. ClawCode's ImageAnalysis.entities is a free-text string array and chat memories mention the same people, vehicles and sites in different wordings. Add a small entities table (id, canonical name, aliases, type) filled by the dreaming pass, and store entity ids on memories, document rows and chunks, so 'the Canyon chain' in a photo and in chat resolve to one node. No face or voice recognition needed. Cost: an entity table, an LLM canonicalization pass, and a join; moderate. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.09736, https://arxiv.org/html/2508.09736v1
- **Mem0 multimodal support (docs, 2026) and Supermemory ingestion (docs)**: Both turn uploads into text. Mem0 open source: with enable_vision set, each image goes to the configured vision LLM, which extracts text and key details that are stored as ordinary memories (searchable and filterable like any memory); vision_details low, auto or high (high advised for receipts and documents); without enable_vision image turns are silently dropped. Platform API takes image URLs or base64, MDX, TXT and PDF URLs, processes asynchronously. Supermemory: images get OCR text, visual descriptions and diagram interpretation; PDFs get text, tables and headers with OCR for scans, and on paid tiers page-by-page OCR that merges figure descriptions into the text, falling back to standard extraction on failure; text split on sentence boundaries with about 200 characters of overlap; a 'dreaming' option groups related documents before forming memories. Memory model: Mem0: extracted facts become memories under a user_id, no raw image kept in the documented path (a benchmark paper describes a variant that also embeds the original image). Supermemory: documents are raw input, memories are extracted facts and semantic chunks linked in a knowledge graph; limits 50 MB per file. Neither documents how a file is linked to the conversation turn. Applicable to ClawCode: ClawCode already exceeds both on image handling (structured analysis with kind, summary, text, entities, importance; dedupe; archive; contact sheets). Two small takeaways: send images as separate calls so one bad image does not poison a batch (Mem0 advises this; ClawCode batches in src/vision/ingest-batcher.ts, so confirm failure isolation), and choose the vision detail level by image kind (high for documents and receipts, low for photos) to save tokens. Cost: negligible. Sources: https://docs.mem0.ai/open-source/features/multimodal-support, https://docs.mem0.ai/platform/features/multimodal-support, https://supermemory.ai/docs/memory-api/features/auto-multi-modal.md, https://supermemory.ai/docs/concepts/super-rag, https://arxiv.org/html/2606.07402
- **Letta Filesystem (Jul 2025, since deprecated) and its replacement**: Managed file memory for agents: folders of uploaded files, parsed (markitdown by default, Mistral OCR optional per the release notes), chunked and embedded, with agent tools grep, open (line-level reads that also open or close files in context) and semantic_search; Letta showed which files are in context and defaulted to a 30k token context, recommending 100k to 200k for file-heavy work. Now documented as deprecated and disabled on the API, replaced by direct filesystem access, context repositories (memfs), an extracting-PDF-text skill, and the external tool qmd for semantic search over local documents. No rationale is given. Memory model: Files are first-class in-context objects the agent opens and closes, not facts distilled into memories. Retrieval is agent-driven (grep, then open, then read), which is what produced Letta's 74.0% LoCoMo result. Applicable to ClawCode: ClawCode agents already have a workspace filesystem and the documents/ paths. The cheapest robust file memory is: keep the derived markdown (OCR text plus vision summary, with page markers) next to the original in the workspace, keep a manifest, and let the agent grep and Read, with the SQLite index as an accelerator rather than the only path. Letta's own retirement of the managed product in favor of direct file access is evidence that the thick layer was not worth maintaining. Cost: near zero; mostly a convention and a manifest file. Sources: https://www.letta.com/blog/letta-filesystem/, https://docs.letta.com/v1-sdk/concepts/filesystem/index.md, https://newreleases.io/project/github/letta-ai/letta/release/0.9.0, https://www.letta.com/blog/benchmarking-ai-agent-memory
- **ColPali, ColQwen and the ViDoRe benchmarks (ColPali ICLR 2025, arXiv Jul 2024, older; ViDoRe v3 ACL 2026)**: Embed a rendered page image into about 1,030 patch vectors of 128 dimensions and score a text query by MaxSim late interaction, skipping OCR and layout parsing. Models: ColPali (PaliGemma 3B, Gemma license), ColQwen2 and ColQwen2.5 (Apache 2.0), ColSmol 256M and 500M (Apache 2.0), newer ColQwen3-based models. ViDoRe v1 (2024), v2 (May 2025) and v3 (10 corpora, about 26,000 pages, 3,099 human-verified queries in 6 languages). Memory model: Unit is the page; no text store. Index is a multi-vector per page held in a vector database or flat files; query time does MaxSim, brute force or approximate (IVFFlat in M3DocRAG, PLAID via the colpali-engine plaid extra). Hierarchical token pooling merges redundant patches (white background). The repo README says colpali-engine is deprecated in favor of Sentence Transformers 6+ (MultiVectorEncoder), and notes ColQwen errors on Apple MPS with torch 2.6.0 fixed by torch 2.5.1. Applicable to ClawCode: ClawCode already renders scanned-PDF pages to PNG (pdftoppm at 150 DPI, 2000 px cap), so page images exist. Treat late interaction as a second retrieval lane for visually structured pages (forms, tables, charts, poor scans), not a replacement for OCR text: shortlist pages with the existing hybrid text retrieval, then rescore with MaxSim, or brute-force MaxSim when the corpus is a few thousand pages. Storage (my arithmetic from the paper's figures): 1,030 x 128 x 2 bytes is about 257.5 KB per page in fp16, so 1,000 pages is about 0.26 GB, 10,000 pages about 2.6 GB, and about 0.9 GB with pooling factor 3. sqlite-vec has no multi-vector type, so store vectors as BLOBs in a side table and compute MaxSim in application code. Costs: a model runtime outside the current bge-small ONNX path (Python sidecar or onnxruntime-node with image preprocessing); indexing time per page on CPU or MPS is not published anywhere I found, so measure first. Do not use for text-dominant PDFs: in ViDoRe v3 text reranking helped far more than visual reranking, and pooling hurts text-dense pages. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.01449, https://arxiv.org/html/2407.01449v6, https://github.com/illuin-tech/colpali, https://arxiv.org/html/2601.08620v1, https://arxiv.org/html/2411.04952
- **ColModernVBERT with FastEmbed ONNX (arXiv Oct 2025): the late-interaction option sized for one machine**: ModernVBERT is a 250M-parameter vision-language encoder, and ColModernVBERT is its late-interaction document retriever (MIT license). Qdrant publishes an ONNX export loadable through FastEmbed (LateInteractionMultimodalEmbedding, model name Qdrant/colmodernvbert; embed_image for pages, embed_text for queries; 128-d vectors; images resized to 512x512; max sequence 8192). Memory model: Same multi-vector page index as ColPali. Large images are split into 512x512 tiles, each tile yields 1,024 patch representations compressed by pixel shuffle to 64 tokens, plus a downscaled global view; the model card example produced 1,149 vectors for one page. Applicable to ClawCode: The one visual retriever that plausibly fits a single CPU or MPS machine without a GPU: roughly 1 GB of weights in fp32 and about 290 KB per page (1,149 x 128 x 2 bytes, my arithmetic). Run it as a sidecar (ClawCode already shells out to pdftoppm and tesseract) over pages already rendered at ingest. Gate adoption on ClawCode's own recall suite with page-level probes, because the only published latency is per query (20 ms vs 175 ms for ColPali on CPU, batch size 1, authors' figure) and no per-page indexing time was found. ONNX outputs may differ slightly from PyTorch per the card. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.01149, https://arxiv.org/html/2510.01149v2, https://huggingface.co/ModernVBERT/colmodernvbert, https://huggingface.co/Qdrant/colmodernvbert/blob/main/README.md
- **Single-vector image embeddings (SigLIP 2, jina-clip-v2, nomic-embed-vision) and the caption-vs-embedding evidence**: Map an image and a short text into one shared vector so a text query finds a picture directly. SigLIP 2 (Google, Feb 2025): sizes B 86M, L 303M, So400m 400M, g 1B vision parameters, Apache 2.0 checkpoints; its text tower has a 64-token limit and was trained on lowercased text (padding to max_length 64 is required), so long captions do not fit. jina-clip-v2: 865M parameters (561M text, 304M EVA02-L14 vision), 512x512 input, Matryoshka truncation from 1,024 to 64 dims, 89 languages. nomic-embed-vision-v1.5: about 92.9M parameters, Apache 2.0, aligned to the nomic-embed-text-v1.5 space, so it is a separate space from ClawCode's bge-small. Memory model: One vector per image in a vec0 table, query by embedding the text prompt with the paired text tower. Captions are a different strategy: a vision LLM writes a description that is indexed with the same text embedder as everything else. Applicable to ClawCode: Use only for images the vision step labels photo (visual-similarity queries such as 'the one with the red truck'), not for screenshots, charts or documents, where OCR plus summary carries the information. The cheapest path is a separate table vec_image_chunks (768-d for SigLIP2 base or nomic-embed-vision) keyed by the existing document source, queried in parallel with the text lanes and fused by the existing RRF. Prefer SigLIP 2 base: DMV-Bench shows SigLIP-2 79.6 vs CLIP 69.7 in a visual lane, and V-Mem used SigLIP2-base-patch16-384. Do not caption both sides of an image-to-image match. Whichever side you choose, tell the captioner the text will be embedded for later search (Jason Liu's reported tip). Cost: a second ONNX model of about 100 MB to 1.5 GB, a vec table, and an eval to prove it beats caption-only on ClawCode's own photos. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.14786, https://huggingface.co/docs/transformers/model_doc/siglip2, https://jina.ai/news/jina-clip-v2-multilingual-multimodal-embeddings-for-text-and-images/, https://huggingface.co/nomic-ai/nomic-embed-vision-v1.5, https://simonwillison.net/2025/Sep/6/jason-liu/, https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.16654
- **OCR and document parsing at small scale: MMDocIR evidence, Docling, PaddleOCR-VL**: MMDocIR (Jan 2025) benchmarks page-level and layout-level retrieval (paragraphs, equations, figures, tables, charts) over long documents (1,685 expert-annotated questions plus 173,843 bootstrapped). Docling (IBM, MIT, arXiv Jan 2025) converts PDFs and Office files with DocLayNet layout, TableFormer tables and EasyOCR, with chunkers that keep page number and bounding box for grounding. PaddleOCR-VL (0.9B, arXiv Oct 2025) is a compact document-parsing VLM (NaViT-style encoder plus ERNIE-4.5-0.3B, 109 languages). Memory model: OCR and parsers produce text (and layout) per page; index text is only as good as that transcription. MMDocIR's conclusion is that text retrievers fed VLM-generated text significantly outperform ones fed OCR text, and that visual retrievers beat text retrievers. Applicable to ClawCode: ClawCode's OCR chain is Tesseract CLI, then tesseract.js WASM, then Claude vision, taking the first result whose Tesseract confidence is at least 0.70 (src/document-ingest/ocr/index.ts). The evidence above says VLM transcription indexes better than classic OCR text, so test whether that confidence gate lets weak Tesseract text into the index: build 30 scanned-page probes and compare Tesseract-only, Claude-vision-only and the gated chain on Recall@5 (my suggestion; no source measures ClawCode's gate). Page boundaries are currently lost: ingest() joins page texts with blank lines and chunkText works on that one string, so add page markers before chunking and persist page number per chunk. Docling is an option if ClawCode wants layout-aware page and bbox metadata without paid vision calls, at a CPU cost. Cost: low for page markers; moderate for a Docling sidecar. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.08828, https://arxiv.org/pdf/2501.17887, https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.14528, https://arxiv.org/pdf/2601.21957, https://www.llamaindex.ai/blog/omnidocbench-is-saturated-what-s-next-for-ocr-benchmarks
- **PageIndex (VectifyAI): vectorless hierarchical tree index with node summaries**: Builds a hierarchical table-of-contents tree per document (structure extracted from layout without an LLM, then summarized by an LLM) and lets an LLM navigate the tree to find sections, with no vector database and no chunking. Local mode handles text PDFs; OCR and image understanding are in the hosted version. Memory model: File-level summary at the root, section and page summaries at nodes, retrieval by LLM reasoning over summaries, results traceable to explicit page or section references. Indexing cost claimed at about 0.001 USD per page. Applicable to ClawCode: The shape is what ClawCode lacks: a file summary, per-page or per-section digests and a way to navigate from one to the other. Without adopting the library, add a document_pages table (source, page number, text, one-sentence digest, optional image path) and a file-level summary row, embed the digests as their own chunks with a type flag, and expose them through the existing search tool so the agent can go file, then page, then chunk. For small files the agent can simply open the page text. Cost: one extra cheap LLM call per page (Haiku-class) at ingest, which ClawCode already does for vision. Sources: https://github.com/VectifyAI/PageIndex
